# EDITORIAL PREFACE

The purpose of this series of small volumes on the primary forms of religious life in India is to provide truly reliable information for the benefit of everyone seeking the welfare of India. Both the editor and the writers intend to work in the spirit of modern scientific inquiry, seeking only the truth. While doing so, they strive to interpret the religious systems under review with the same imagination and empathy that define the best religious studies today. 

Furthermore, they believe they can offer new insights gained from their close personal interactions <note>original: "intercourse"</note> with the people who practice the faiths described here. In every instance, their study of relevant literature has been significantly supplemented by persistent questioning of those who could provide firsthand information. In each case, the religion being described is compared and contrasted with Christianity. The authors believe that readers, especially those in India, will recognize the value of this practical method for highlighting the most important <note>original: "salient"</note> features of Indian religious life.

<summary>This preface outlines the goals of "The Religious Life of India" series, emphasizing a scientific yet sympathetic approach to studying Indian religions through both literature and direct interaction with practitioners.</summary>
<keywords>editorial preface, religious studies, Indian religions, Christianity, methodology, primary research, Hinduism</keywords>

### PREFACE

The material for this account of the village gods of South India has been gathered almost entirely from my own observations and inquiries. I have found very little help from existing books, as I believe this is the first attempt to systematically address this specific side of Indian religion. This work does not claim to be a complete <note>original: "exhaustive"</note> account of every rite and ceremony performed in the worship of village deities. The variety of rituals across the different districts of South India is nearly endless, and I have not even tried to include every ceremony that I have personally encountered.

Perhaps it would be more accurate to call this book "An Introduction to the Study of the Village Gods of South India." However, I believe that all the main types of this specific form of Hinduism are included in the following pages. I hope enough has been said to give the reader a fairly complete idea of its general character and to allow for comparisons with similar forms of religion in other parts of the world.

I would like to thank the editor of the magazine *The Nineteenth Century and After* <note>A prominent British monthly review founded in 1877; the title changed as the century turned.</note> for permission to reprint parts of my articles in Chapters IV, VI, and VII. I am grateful to Mrs. Whitehead for the drawings used for the illustrations, and to Miss Stephen, the Archdeacon

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that his study of South Indian village gods is based on primary field research and aims to provide a systematic introduction to the subject. He acknowledges that while not every local ritual is covered, the book represents the major types of these religious practices.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the Introduction of the book, establishing the historical and cultural foundation for the study of village deities. It follows the list of illustrations and transitions from a visual overview to the conceptual origins of Indian folk religion.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

The worship of village gods is the most ancient form of Indian religion. Before the Aryan invasion <note>The "Aryan Invasion Theory" was the standard historical framework when this was written; modern scholarship often refers to this period as the "Indo-Aryan Migration."</note>, which probably took place in the second millennium BCE, the ancient inhabitants of India—who are sometimes called Dravidians—were a dark-skinned people with religious beliefs and customs that likely did not differ much from those of other early societies. They believed the world was inhabited by a vast number of spirits, both good and bad, who were responsible for all unusual events, especially diseases and disasters.

The goal of their religion was to appease <note>original: "propitiate"</note> these countless spirits. At the same time, each village seems to have been under the protection of a specific spirit who served as its guardian deity. These village deities probably came into existence during the period when people began to settle down in agricultural communities. We may see in them the seeds <note>original: "germs"</note> of the national deities that were so prominent among the Semitic peoples <note>In the context of the early 20th century, "race" or "peoples" was often used to describe linguistic or ethnic groups.</note> and the great empires of Egypt, Nineveh, and Babylon.

Where a family developed into a clan, the clan into a tribe, the tribe into a nation, and the nation into a conquering empire, the god of the family naturally developed into an imperial deity. But in ancient India, before the arrival of the Aryans, the population seems to have been divided into small agricultural and herding <note>original: "pastoral"</note> communities. There were no nations and no conquering empires. It was not until the Aryan invaders had conquered North India and settled down in the country that there was any growth of philosophical thought in India regarding the world as a whole. The mystery of the universe did not interest the simple Dravidian people. They only looked for an

<summary>This introduction outlines the origins of village god worship in India, attributing it to the pre-Aryan Dravidian inhabitants who sought to appease spirits and protect their small agricultural communities.</summary>
<keywords>village gods, Aryan invasion, Dravidians, agricultural communities, guardian deities, religious evolution, ancient India</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the Introduction, moving from the origins of village deities to the historical interaction between ancient Dravidian traditions and the later arrival of Aryan culture and Brahmanical systems.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

...explanation of the facts and troubles of village life. Their religion, therefore, did not progress beyond a simple <term>animism</term> <note>The belief that natural objects, places, and creatures possess a distinct spiritual essence</note> and a belief in village deities. Later on, after the <term>Aryans</term> <note>Indo-Aryan peoples who migrated into the Indian subcontinent during the second millennium BCE</note> had moved across a large part of India, and the <term>Brahmins</term> <note>original: "Brāhmans"; the priestly class who established themselves at the top of the social hierarchy</note> had established their dominance as a priestly caste, the old <term>Dravidian</term> <note>The indigenous people and cultures of southern India</note> traditions were influenced by the higher-status religion of the <term>Aryans</term>, and in turn, strongly influenced them.

The earliest Indian philosophical systems appeared in the sixth century BCE, driven by the desire to escape from <term>transmigration</term> <note>The belief that the soul is reborn into a new body after death</note>. Two of these developed into new religions that were opposed to <term>Hinduism</term>, namely <term>Jainism</term> and <term>Buddhism</term>, while other systems remained within the old faith. All of these exercised a deep influence on Indian thought and also modified religious practices in certain ways. On the other hand, the simple ideas and rugged rituals of the widespread indigenous <note>original: "aboriginal"</note> tribes, who were constantly interacting with the lives of the <term>Aryans</term>, found their way into the Aryan religion at many points. Thus, the ancient worship of many nature deities found in the <term>Rigveda</term> <note>original: "Ṛigveda"; the oldest collection of Hindu sacred hymns</note>—with its animal sacrifices performed in the open air and its simple, healthy rules for family and social life—was gradually transformed. It became a vast collection of competing sects holding complex philosophical ideas and intricate theological systems. These groups often condemned animal sacrifice, yet they worshipped physical idols and were bound by countless superstitions. <term>Caste</term> emerged and solidified into the most rigid system of social division the world has ever seen, inspired and justified by the doctrines of <term>transmigration</term> and <term>karma</term> <note>The principle that a person's actions in this life determine their fate in the next</note>.

What we now call <term>Hinduism</term>, therefore, is a strange mixture of the most diverse religious forms, ranging from the most complex and difficult systems of philosophy to basic forms of <term>animism</term>. At the same time, the ancient forms of <term>Dravidian</term> religion have been, in their turn, greatly changed by <term>Brahmin</term> influence. For the most part, the same people in both towns and villages worship the village deities alongside the <term>Brahmin</term> gods. There are still a few indigenous tribes in some of the hill regions who remain unaffected by <term>Brahmin</term> ideas or...

# INTRODUCTION 13

customs, but in the vast majority of districts, the worship of the village deities and the worship of Shiva <note>original: "Śiva"</note> and Vishnu go on side by side—just as in China, Confucianism and Taoism are not rival religions but complementary creeds.

For the student of comparative religion, the study of the unusual <note>original: "weird"</note> rites and ceremonies connected with the propitiation <note>The act of winning the favor of or making peace with a deity</note> of the village deities is interesting. This is because it reveals many points of contact with primitive forms of religion in other lands, and it also allows the student to see these primitive religious ideas in very different stages of development. To the Christian, the study has an even greater interest because, despite all their repulsive features, these rites contain instinctive ideas and yearnings that find their satisfaction in the highest truths of Christianity.

In this second edition, I have tried to fix the defects and omissions that were kindly pointed out by reviewers, and some chapters have been rearranged. However, it has been difficult to know where to stop when attempting to fill in those gaps. The number of different gods and goddesses worshipped throughout South India is enormous, and the variety of local customs is almost infinite. It is inevitable, therefore, that a large number of deities and customs—which are both interesting and important—should be omitted in a small book that can only aim at being a brief introduction to a vast subject.

The chapter on the probable origin of the worship of village gods (Chapter 8) has naturally provoked the most criticism. In the previous chapters, I have stated what I have heard and seen myself. In this chapter, I rashly entered the field of speculation <note>original: "conjecture"</note> and framed a hypothesis as to what may have happened about 7,000 years ago. Naturally, I have laid myself open to attack. But despite the criticisms made against my theory, I do not feel inclined to give it up, though it must necessarily remain impossible to prove. I am still of the opinion that the totemistic <note>A theory that religious groups originated from a spiritual connection to a sacred object or animal, known as a totem</note> theory of the origin of the sacrifices to the <term>village deities</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "grāma-devatās"</note>, or village

<summary>The author explains that village deity worship exists alongside major Hindu traditions and defends his controversial "totemistic" theory regarding the prehistoric origins of South Indian religious sacrifices.</summary>
<keywords>South India, comparative religion, Shiva, Vishnu, village deities, totemism, grāma-devatās, religious sacrifice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of the origin of village deity worship, specifically addressing and critiquing Professor Elmore’s theory of sacrifice as a symbol of historical conquest.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

goddesses, as distinct from the offerings made to ancestral spirits or other deities, is, on the whole, most consistent with the facts. Professor Elmore, in his capable and very interesting book, *Dravidian Gods in Modern Hinduism*, criticizes the totemistic theory <note>A theory suggesting that religious groups or clans originated from a spiritual connection to a specific animal or plant "totem"</note> regarding the origin of the buffalo sacrifice—which is the most significant offering made to the <term>grāma-devatās</term> <note>original: "grāma-devatās"; village deities</note>—on three main grounds. Primarily, he argues that the stories currently told by the people suggest a historical origin for these rituals. Professor Elmore suspects from these stories that the sacrifices symbolize the "extreme punishment and disgrace of a defeated enemy." 

In this theory, actions like decapitating the animal, placing the foreleg in its mouth, smearing its nose with fat, and placing a lighted lamp on its forehead are intended to represent "the ultimate humiliation of a feared, despised, and defeated enemy." Likewise, the procession of the buffalo through the village with a garland around its neck before the sacrifice is described as "the remains of a victory parade in which the enemy was displayed before a shameful death." Therefore, according to Elmore, the sacrifice represents the victory of the Aryan invaders over the native Dravidian peoples and their "mad gods."

I must admit that this explanation seems very far-fetched and unlikely to me. It is completely out of step with everything we know about the origins and meanings of sacrifice and ritual among other cultures. Furthermore, it faces a major flaw: it forces us to assume that these buffalo sacrifices began at a relatively late date, long after the Aryan invasion of North India and after the Aryans moved into South India—a time when the conflict with the Dravidians was over and Aryan victory was certain. 

The stories I have shared in Chapter VII, and those Professor Elmore provides in his book to support his theory, clearly belong to a period when the <term>Pariahs</term> <note>original: "Pariahs"; a term historically used for the Dalit or outcaste communities of South India</note> of South India, who were originally a prominent clan among the Dravidians, had been removed from their high status and forced into a state of servitude.

<summary>Whitehead critiques Professor Elmore's theory that buffalo sacrifices represent the historical humiliation of Dravidian enemies by Aryan invaders, arguing that the theory is chronologically inconsistent and ignores broader anthropological patterns of ritual.</summary>
<keywords>Professor Elmore, buffalo sacrifice, grāma-devatās, Dravidian, Aryan invasion, Pariahs, totemism, ritual origin</keywords>

### INTRODUCTION 15

...and degradation by Brahman influence. However, it seems quite clear to me that the worship of the village deities <note>original: "grāma-devatās"</note> and the buffalo sacrifice belong to a much older period. They likely date back to a time long before the Aryan invasion <note>A historical theory regarding the migration of Indo-Aryan speaking peoples into the Indian subcontinent</note>, probably to when the Dravidian <note>The native peoples and language family of Southern India</note> clans first arrived in India and settled into an agricultural lifestyle. If this is true, it is impossible to interpret the meaning of rites and ceremonies that originated by at least 3000 or 4000 BCE using legends about historical events that took place three thousand years later.

Furthermore, considering that agricultural deities all over the world have been primarily female, and that many rituals associated with the worship of the village deities <note>original: "grāma-devatās"</note> are clearly linked to the harvest, I still maintain my opinion. I believe the reason these village deities are female is that they were originally goddesses of agriculture. Professor Elmore’s view—that Dravidian deities are female because Dravidian women were uniquely quarrelsome, vengeful, and jealous, and that their tempers and curses made people feel it was wise to propitiate <note>To appease or win the favor of a spirit or deity</note> female spirits—strikes me as a very improbable explanation. This remains true even if we could prove that Dravidian women six thousand years ago were as skilled in "using foul language and vigorous insults" as some are today.

# CHAPTER I

## LEADING FEATURES OF THE RELIGION

The worship of the village deity, or *grāma-devatā* <note>original: "grāma-devatā," a Sanskrit and Tamil term literally meaning "village deity"</note>, forms an important part of the collection of religious beliefs, customs, and ceremonies that are generally grouped together under the term Hinduism. In almost every village and town of South India, one can see a shrine or symbol of the village deity, and in every village, this deity is periodically worshipped and propitiated <note>appeased or won over through offerings and rituals</note>. 

As a rule, these shrines are far less grand than the Brahmanical <note>relating to the priestly Brahman caste or the mainstream, Sanskrit-based Hindu tradition</note> temples in the neighborhood. Very often, a shrine is nothing more than a small brick building three or four feet high, or a small enclosure with a few rough stones in the center. Often, there is no shrine at all. Nevertheless, when disaster strikes the village—when a plague, famine, or cattle disease appears—the entire body of villagers turns to the village deity for protection. Shiva <note>original: "Śiva," one of the principal deities of Hinduism, often associated with destruction and transformation</note> and Vishnu <note>original: "Viṣṇu," one of the principal deities of Hinduism, known as the Preserver</note> may be more majestic beings, but the village deity is regarded as a more immediate help in times of trouble and is more closely concerned with the happiness and prosperity of the villagers.

(*a*) *The origin of this form of Hinduism is lost in ancient times;* but it is certain that it represents a pre-Aryan <note>referring to the era before the migration of Indo-Aryan speaking peoples into the Indian subcontinent, roughly before 1500 BCE</note> cult of the Dravidian <note>the native peoples and language family of Southern India</note> peoples. This cult has been modified to varying degrees in different parts of South India by Brahmanical influence, and some details of the ceremonies seem to point back to a totemistic <note>a stage of religion involving the veneration of a sacred object or animal as a spiritual emblem of a group</note> stage of religion. The typical function of the village deity <note>original: "grāma-devatā"</note> is to guard the village, but many of them are believed to...

<summary>This page introduces the "grāma-devatā" or village deities of South India, explaining their central role in local life and their ancient, pre-Aryan Dravidian origins compared to more formal Hindu deities.</summary>

<keywords>grāma-devatā, Hinduism, South India, Brahmanical temples, Shiva, Vishnu, Dravidian culture, pre-Aryan, folk religion, village shrines</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the powers of village deities and begins a comparison between these local spirits and the major gods of the Hindu pantheon, such as Shiva and Vishnu.</meta>

->### LEADING FEATURES OF THE RELIGION<-

...have other powers, especially in relation to disease and calamity.

(*b*) The village deities and their worship are widely different from the *popular Hindu deities, Shiva and Vishnu, and the worship that centers in the great Hindu temples.*

1. Shiva <note>original: "Śiva"</note> and Vishnu <note>original: "Vishṇu"</note> represent forces of nature: Shiva symbolizes the power of destruction and the idea of life through death, while Vishnu represents the power of preservation and the idea of salvation. Both these deities and the religious system connected with them are the outcome of philosophic reflection on the universe as a whole. But the village deities, on the other hand, have no relation to the universe. *They symbolize only the facts of village life.* They are related, not to great world forces, but to such simple facts as cholera, smallpox, and cattle disease.

2. Then, in the second place, *village deities,* with very few exceptions, *are female.* Shiva and Vishnu, and the principal deities of the Hindu pantheon <note>the complete set of gods belonging to a particular religion or mythology</note>, are male. Their wives, it is true, play an important part in Hindu religious life—Kali <note>original: "Kālī"</note> especially, the "black one," the wife of Shiva, is the presiding deity of Calcutta, and is one of the chief deities of Bengal—but, speaking generally, in the Hindu pantheon the male deities are predominant and the female deities occupy a subordinate position. This is characteristic of the nature of the Aryan <note>referring here to the Indo-Aryan peoples who migrated into northern India in ancient times</note> religion, but in the old Dravidian <note>the people and cultures of southern India that predated the Aryan migration</note> cults, a leading feature was the worship of the female principle in nature. It is possible that this is due to the fact that the Aryan deities were the gods of a race of warriors, whereas the Dravidian deities were the goddesses of an agricultural people. All over the world, the gods of war are mostly male, while the agricultural deities are, for the most part, female; and this naturally arises from the fact that war is the business of men, whereas, among primitive peoples, the cultivation of the fields was largely left to the women, and also from the fact that the idea of fertility is naturally connected with the female. All over Southern India, therefore, the

<summary>This page distinguishes village deities from major Hindu gods, noting that village spirits focus on local crises like disease rather than universal philosophy, and are predominantly female, reflecting an ancient agricultural heritage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the local village deities and the major gods of the Hindu pantheon, focusing on the gender of the deities, the role of male attendants, and the nature of their worship.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

Village deities are almost entirely female. In the Tamil-speaking region <note>original: "Tamil country"</note>, it is true that most of them have male attendants who are supposed to guard the shrines and carry out the commands of the goddesses; however, their position is clearly subordinate and almost like that of a servant. 

One of these male deities, however, named Iyenar <note>a popular protective deity in Tamil Nadu, often associated with borders and protection</note>, holds an independent position. He generally has a shrine of his own and is regarded as the night-watchman of the village. The courtyard of his shrine is usually crowded with clay figures of horses, both large and small. It is believed that he rides these horses around the village during the watches of the night to keep away evil spirits. 

In the Telugu-speaking region <note>original: "Telugu country"</note> as well, there is a being called Potu-Razu. He appears sometimes as the brother, sometimes as the husband of the village goddess, and sometimes merely as an attendant. However, I have never encountered him as an independent deity, and I have always been told that sacrifices are never offered to him alone, but only in combination with one or more of the goddesses.

3. Thirdly, village deities are almost universally worshipped with animal sacrifices. Buffaloes, sheep, goats, pigs, and chickens <note>original: "fowls"</note> are freely offered to them, sometimes by the thousands. In the Tamil region, this custom is curiously modified by the influence of Brahminism <note>the traditional religious system led by the Brahmin caste</note>, which has convinced the villagers that the shedding of blood is low and irreligious. Because of this, it is remarkable that no animal sacrifices are ever offered to Iyenar. His male attendants accept them eagerly—and take palm wine <note>original: "toddy"</note> and cigars <note>original: "cheroots"</note> into the bargain—but Iyenar is considered much too refined a being to be pleased by the sight of bloodshed.

4. Furthermore, the *pujaris* <note>original: "pūjārīs"</note>—that is, the priestly ministers, the men who perform the *puja* <note>original: "pūjā"</note> or worship—are *not Brahmins* <note>the highest priestly caste in the Hindu hierarchy</note>, but are drawn from all the other castes.

---

<note>
¹ The entire Hindu population in North India may be compared to a great step-pyramid consisting of five stories. These are exclusive groups, separated from each other by deep distinctions in religious and social standing and in their ideal roles in society:
</note>

It also details the common practice of animal sacrifice and notes that the priests of these local shrines are drawn from non-Brahmin castes.</summary>

# LEADING FEATURES OF THE RELIGION

It is almost impossible to make any general statement about India without immediately encountering facts that seem to prove you wrong. Accordingly, I may mention that I have found cases where Brāhmans serve as <term>pūjārīs</term> <note>priests who perform the daily worship and rituals</note> at the shrines of village deities. I came across one such case at Negapatam; while, at Bangalore, I actually found a case where a Brāhman widow was the officiating priest. About three miles from Tanjore, too, there is a temple of Mariamma <note>a popular South Indian goddess associated with disease and protection</note> served by Brāhman priests. 

However, no animal sacrifices are offered at the central shrine where the Brāhmans serve. In one corner of the temple grounds, there is a separate shrine with an image of Mariamma where animals are regularly sacrificed; but at this shrine, no Brāhmans officiate. I believe that it is the only temple or shrine of Mariamma in South India where there are Brāhman priests. But then, in these cases, the Brāhman priest never has anything to do with animal sacrifices. These are always conducted entirely by men of lower castes; and even so, it is considered a degradation for a Brāhman to be connected as a priest with a shrine where such <note>original: "abominations"</note> <note>the author's term for animal sacrifices</note> take place. But, according to the Indian proverb, "For the sake of one’s stomach, one must play many parts." Setting aside these exceptional cases, it may be stated generally that no—

---

| Social Group | Role | Status and Origins |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Brāhmans** | Priests | In North India, these three groups are held to be of pure Aryan blood and are called <term>twice-born</term> <note>refers to the spiritual rebirth through the sacred thread ceremony</note>. |
| **Kshatriyas** | Kings and warriors | |
| **Vaiśyas** | Farmers and tradesmen | |
| **Śūdras** | Servants | Aboriginal people, considered pure and admitted to the temples. |
| **Outcastes** | <term>Pañchamas</term> <note>literally "fifth-class men"</note> | Unclean, untouchable aboriginal people. |

---

Foreigners are held to be unclean and are called <term>mlecchas</term> <note>a term for foreigners or non-Aryans who do not follow Vedic culture</note>. In South India, it should be noted, the farmers, artisans, and tradesmen are all classed as Śūdras, and the Kshatriyas are practically non-existent. The population, therefore, is divided into three main groups: the Brāhmans of Aryan blood; the Śūdras, who are Dravidians <note>the indigenous people of South India</note> admitted to the temples; and the Outcastes, who are partly Dravidians and partly descendants of even older inhabitants, and who are not admitted to the temples.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 19, completing the thought that while rare exceptions exist, high-caste priests generally do not officiate at village shrines. It transitions into how village deities and spirits are often local in origin, sometimes arising from recent tragic or remarkable events.</meta>

### THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

[no] <term>Brahmans</term> <note>original: "Brāhmans"; the highest priestly caste in Hindu society</note> are the priests of village deities, but that the <term>ritual priests</term> <note>original: "pūjārīs"; those who perform the daily offerings</note> are drawn from all other castes indiscriminately, while an important part in the worship, especially that connected with the buffalo-sacrifices, is even taken by <term>Outcastes</term> <note>historically marginalized communities outside the traditional four-tier caste system</note>. As will be seen later on, the buffalo-sacrifice has special features of its own, and seems to retain traces of a primitive form of worship, which may possibly have originated in <term>totemism</term> <note>a system of belief where a group has a spiritual connection with a particular animal or plant</note>.

In addition to the <term>village deities</term> <note>original: "grāma-devatās"</note>, who are in a special sense the deities of the village, there are a large number of spirits of all kinds, male and female, who are worshipped by the villagers. The worship of departed ancestors played an important part in the old <term>Dravidian religion</term> <note>the indigenous religious traditions of South India, distinct from the later Sanskrit-based traditions</note> and is still universal all over South India. So men and women, boys and girls, who have died violent or untimely deaths, or who have been notorious for their power or even their crimes, are frequently worshipped after death. It is probable that a large proportion of these gods have been reverenced for centuries, but many are of quite recent origin. Some were originally people who were murdered, or who during their lifetime were feared for their power or their crimes, or women who died in child-birth. It is easy to observe a deity in the making even at the present day.

A district superintendent of police in the <term>Telugu</term> <note>a major language and cultural region of South India, primarily in modern Andhra Pradesh</note> country told me that in 1904, at a village some twelve miles from <term>Ellore</term> <note>modern-day Eluru</note>, two little boys, minding cattle in the fields, thought they heard the sound of trumpets proceeding from an ant-hill. They told the story in the village, and at once the people turned out and performed *<term>worship</term>* <note>original: "pūjā"; ritual offerings and prayers</note> to the deity in the ant-hill. The fame of the deity’s presence spread like wild-fire far and wide, and the village became the centre of pilgrimages from all the country round about. Every Sunday as many as 5,000 people, men and women, assembled before the ant-hill, and might be seen prostrate on their faces, rapt in adoration. The incident illustrates the ease with which a local <term>cult</term> <note>a localized system of religious devotion centered on a specific figure or object</note> springs up in India and suddenly becomes popular over a large district.

<summary>This page explains that village worship is generally led by non-Brahman priests and Outcastes, and describes how new deities often emerge from local tragedies or mysterious events, such as a 1904 incident involving a "trumpet-sounding" ant-hill.</summary>
<keywords>caste roles, village priests, buffalo sacrifice, ancestor worship, Dravidian religion, deity formation, Eluru pilgrimage, Telugu country</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how local spirits are deified following tragic or violent deaths. It provides specific 19th and early 20th-century examples of new "deities" arising from local tragedies in the Andhra region of South India.</meta>

# LEADING FEATURES OF THE RELIGION

Another instance came to my notice a few years ago at Bezwada <note>now known as Vijayawada, a major city in Andhra Pradesh</note>. A small boy, the son of well-to-do parents, was murdered near the town for the sake of his ornaments, and thrown into the canal. The body was discovered and placed under a tree near the bank of the canal, at a place where three roads meet. A little after, a small shrine, about a foot and a half high, was built by the parents under a tree to the spirit of the murdered boy. Then some one declared that he had made a vow at the shrine and obtained his desire. The fame of the shrine at once spread, the spirit of the boy rose quite to the rank of a minor deity, and a local worship speedily sprang up and became popular. When I last saw the shrine it had been enlarged and had become about twice its original size.

About sixty years ago a Hindu widow, named Rāmamma, lived between Bezwada and Hyderabad, farming some land left her by her husband. After a time she contracted immoral relations with one of her servants, Buddha Sahib <note>the title "Sahib" suggests the servant was likely Muslim, indicating a relationship that crossed religious and social boundaries of the time</note>. Her brother was so angry that he murdered them both. Then the cattle-plague broke out; and the villagers connected it with the wrath of the murdered Rāmamma, and instituted special rites to pacify her spirit. And now, whenever there is cattle-plague in the district, two rough wooden images, about two feet high, are made to represent Maddha Rāmamma and Buddha Sahib, and, with two images of local goddesses as their attendants, are put on a small wooden cart and dragged in procession at night round all the principal streets of the village, accompanied by fireworks, music, and *nautch-girls* <note>original: "nautch-girls"; from the Hindi word 'nach' for dance. Historically, these were professional dancers, often associated with temples, though the author's description of "loose character" reflects a typical Victorian-era colonial prejudice</note> (*i.e.* dancing-girls of loose character connected with Hindu temples). Finally, the cart is dragged to the boundary of the village lands and thrown into the territory of the adjacent village, in order to transfer to it the angry spirit of Rāmamma.

Temples have been built to Plague-amma <note>The suffix "-amma" means "mother" or "lady" and is a standard title for goddesses in South India; "Plague-amma" is literally "Plague Mother," a deity created to personify and appease the disease</note> during the last ten years, as a result of the prevalence of plague.

Special reverence is paid to persons who come to

...an untimely end, for example, to the spirits of girls who die before marriage. When the circumstances of their death especially strike the imagination of the general public, the reverence which is ordinarily confined to the family expands into a regular <term>local cult</term> <note>a localized religious practice centered on a specific figure, site, or legend, often distinct from mainstream temple worship</note>.

Then, again, there is the spirit of the <term>boundary stone</term> <note>stones used to mark the edges of village lands or individual properties, often treated as sacred guardians of the territory</note>, the spirits of hills and rivers, forests and trees, and the deities of particular arts and crafts, who are worshipped by particular classes of the population. The practice of <term>serpent worship</term>, especially the deadly <term>cobra</term>, is common all over South India. In one village of the <term>Wynaad</term> <note>now known as Wayanad, a mountainous district in the state of Kerala</note>, I came across a Mission school which was visited almost daily by a large cobra, which glided undisturbed and harmless through the schoolroom. Neither teachers nor pupils would have dared to kill it. Constantly they fed it with milk. In many towns and villages, large slabs of stone with figures of cobras—often two cobras intertwined—carved in <term>bas-relief</term> <note>a style of sculpture where the figures are carved to stay close to the background surface</note> are seen on a platform under a large tree. They are worshipped especially by women who want children.

# CHAPTER II

## NAMES, CHARACTERS, AND FUNCTIONS OF THE VILLAGE GODS

(a) THE names of village deities are countless. Some of them have an obvious meaning, many are quite unintelligible to the people themselves, and I have often failed to get any clue to their origin, even from native <term>paṇḍits</term> <note>learned scholars or teachers, typically experts in Sanskrit, Hindu law, or philosophy</note>. They differ in almost every district, and often the deities worshipped in one village will be quite unknown in other villages five or six miles off. In Masulipatam <note>a historic port city in the state of Andhra Pradesh, now known as Machilipatnam</note> on the East Coast, in the Telugu country <note>the region where the Telugu language is spoken</note>, the following were given me as the names of the village deities worshipped in the district, *namely* <note>original: viz.</note> Mutyalamma, the pearl goddess (*amma* or *amman* is only a female termination <note>the suffix "-amma" literally translates to "mother" in many South Indian languages</note>); Chinnintamma, the goddess who is head of the house; Challalamma, the goddess presiding over buttermilk; Ghantalamma, the goddess who goes with bells; Yaparamma, the goddess who transacts business; Mamillamma, the goddess who sits under a mango tree; Gangamma, the water goddess, who in this district is the protectress against smallpox.

But, at a village about twenty miles from Masulipatam, I found that fifteen different goddesses were worshipped in the neighbourhood, of whom only four were identical with those of Masulipatam. Some were named after the villages from which they had been imported, *for example* <note>original: e.g.</note> Addankamma, the goddess from Addanki, and Pandilamma, the goddess from Pandil; others had names derived from common objects of country life, *for example* Wanamalamma, the goddess of the <term>tope</term> <note>a South Indian term for a grove or orchard of trees</note>, Balamma, the goddess of the cart, and Śitalamma, the water goddess.

In the Ellore district <note>now spelled Eluru, a city in Andhra Pradesh</note>, farther west, the deities

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

The deities worshipped are chiefly **Gaṅgamma**, who is sometimes called **Mahālakshmī** <note>the goddess of wealth and beauty, and the wife of the god Vishṇu</note>, and sometimes **Chamalamma** <note>another name for Kālī, the fierce consort of the god Śiva</note>, as well as **Poleramma**, the goddess of boundaries, and **Aṅkamma**, who is regarded as the goddess of cholera and disease in general.

Farther west than Ellore <note>now known as Eluru, a city in Andhra Pradesh</note>, across the hills in the Cuddapah and Kurnool districts, the village goddess is often known simply as **Peddamma** (the "Great Mother" or Great Goddess) or **Chinnamma** (the "Little Mother" or Little Goddess). In many villages of these districts, however, these general names are unknown, and the village deities are called **Gaṅgamma**, **Polamma**, and **Suṅkalamma**, and so on. In some villages, the local deities consist of **Potu-Razu** <note>a male deity often acting as a brother or guardian to the goddesses</note> and his seven sisters, who are known by various names. In one village, these were given to me as: **Peddamma**, **Isondamma**, **Maramma**, **Aṅkalamma**, **Nukalamma**, **Vasukota**, **Ellamma**, and **Arikamma**.

Again, **Kāliamma** or **Kālī** is said to be the only one of the village goddesses whose name is found in the *Vedas* <note>the oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism</note>. She is an <term>avatāra</term> <note>a Sanskrit word meaning "descent," referring to the incarnation of a deity on earth</note>, or an incarnation of the eight powers of the universe. The story told about her is that a demon named **Mahishāsura** (the "buffalo demon") gave great offence to Śiva and was condemned to death. But, owing to a privilege bestowed on him by Śiva himself, he could not be slain by the **Trimūrti**¹ nor by any male deity. So the task was given to **Kālī**, who successfully accomplished it, and so won a place among village deities.

At Cuddalore <note>a coastal city in Tamil Nadu</note>, I visited a shrine of the goddess **Mīnāchīamman** at the village of Devanampatnam. It stands on the seashore on a low ridge of sand. There is no building, but an oblong space about 20 by 12 feet is enclosed on three sides by rows of clay figures, the eastern end towards the sea being left open. On the western side of the oblong, facing the sea, there were—

***

¹ This word <note>original: "Trimūrti", meaning "Three Forms"</note> is used for an image with three heads, representing **Brahmā** (the Creator), **Vishṇu** (the Preserver), and **Śiva** (the Destroyer) as a triple manifestation of the divine nature.

<meta>This page continues the description of South Indian village shrines, moving from the general classification of goddesses in Andhra districts to a specific case study of a shrine layout in the Tamil-speaking region of South Arcot. <page-num>25</page-num>

### NAMES, CHARACTERS, AND FUNCTIONS

Two small clay figures, apparently a man and a woman, seated in the centre. They were about a foot high with the remains of old garlands on them. To the left and right of them were figures of **seven virgins** (or <term>Saptakannigais</term> <note>original: "Saptakannigais"; these are the "Seven Maidens," a group of goddesses widely worshipped in the Tamil region</note>), very well modelled in clay and about nine inches high. In front of them and beside them were the figures of male guardians and attendants. On each side of the images of the virgins was a figure of a large round fish, with open mouth and staring eyes, and seated on the back of each fish were the figures of a man and a woman. 

The <term>pūjārī</term> <note>a priest who performs the ritual offerings</note> of the shrine told me that the woman was **Mīnāchī** <note>literally "the fish-eyed one," the patron goddess of the city of Madurai</note> the fish-goddess, and the man **Madurai-Vīran** <note>literally "The Hero of Madurai," a popular guardian deity in Tamil folklore</note>. Beside each fish were figures of guardians and attendants. 

The north and south sides of the oblong, which are about twenty-one feet in length, are formed by clay figures of horses and elephants, some of them with men on their backs. The elephants are quaint creatures, very like horses with trunks. The horses are not in this case steeds of the god **Iyenar** <note>also known as Ayyanar, a prominent male guardian deity usually associated with terracotta horses</note>, but simply the attendants of **Mīnāchī** and the **seven virgins**. 

Animal sacrifices, consisting of goats, cocks, etc., are offered to these deities once a year at an annual festival. The people at the shrine gave the name of the fish as something like *ullai*; but the translator of the district and sessions court of South Arcot told me that the fish on which **Mīnāchī** and **Madurai-Vīran** are seated is the **ullan** fish <note>a species of sea-fish that migrates into freshwater rivers</note>, which is a sea-fish that runs up the river in flood-times, when the bar is open, and generally travels a considerable distance till it meets with an <term>anicut</term> <note>a dam or weir used for irrigation</note> or some similar obstacle. It gets very fat and is a favourite dish. 

The goddess **Mīnāchī**, who is seated on it, is commonly worshipped by fishermen, who swear by her name. She is the goddess worshipped in the great temple of Madura <note>now known as Madurai</note> together with the god **Śiva** <note>one of the supreme deities of Hinduism</note>. **Madurai-Vīran** is a male attendant of nearly all the village goddesses throughout the Tamil country, and he is generally represented by a small conical stone or the image of a man carved in bas-relief on a stone slab, standing outside the shrine.

-># THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA<-

The <term>Saptakannigais</term> <note>original: "Saptakannigais"; translated as "the seven virgins"</note>, or <term>Akasa-kannigais</term> <note>original: "Akasa-kannigais"; translated as "the heavenly virgins"</note>, are the tutelary <note>guardian or patron</note> deities of <term>tanks</term> <note>In India, "tanks" refers to artificial reservoirs or ponds used for irrigation and village water</note>, and the figures of these Virgins seated in a row are often carved on a small stone and placed on <term>tank bunds</term> <note>the embankments or dams that hold back the water</note>, especially at places where the tank has previously breached. In the North Arcot district, they are described as female creatures who are very quarrelsome; it is believed that when they fight, breaches are caused in the tanks by the stamping of their feet. At the same time, they are supposed to protect the tanks. When the flood rises to a dangerous point, it is said that one of the Virgins, in the shape of a little child, runs through the village knocking at the doors and calling up the villagers to come and protect the embankment. It is believed that people walking alone along a tank embankment have sometimes met the Seven Virgins going in procession with horses and torches, and that anyone who sees them invariably dies. The district judge told me that, in a case which came before him in the North Arcot district, a man who actually died from a fracture of his skull—because a cousin of his had hit him on the head with a thick piece of sugarcane—was reported to have died as the result of meeting a procession of the Seven Virgins on the tank bund. The village magistrate even excused himself for not reporting the man's death because he considered it to be a death by natural causes.

A male deity, called **Kuttandavar**, is worshipped in many parts of the Tamil country, especially in the South Arcot district. At the village of Devanampatnam, near Cuddalore, I saw an image of this god in a small shrine built of brick, with a rough <term>pandal</term> <note>a temporary shed or ritual pavilion</note> of bamboos, thatched with coconut leaves, in front of it. The image consisted of a head, like a big mask, about three feet high, with a rubicund <note>reddish or healthy-colored</note> face, strong features, moustaches turning up at the end, lion's teeth projecting downwards outside the mouth from the angles of the upper jaw, and a tall conical head-dress, called in Tamil a ***Krittam*** <note>original: "Krittam"; a tiered crown or ornamental headpiece</note>. Below this stone there was a small stone head about one and a half feet high, which was a miniature of the larger figure. It was finely chiselled and—

Both images had the mark of <term>Viṣṇu</term> <note>one of the principal deities of Hinduism, the Preserver</note> on their foreheads, so
also had the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>translated as "priest"</note> of the shrine. The priest said that
the images represented the god <term>Kuttandavar</term> <note>A deity, often identified with the mythological figure Aravan, who is a central figure in a major annual festival in Tamil Nadu.</note>, and he told
me the following legend about him. The god <term>Indra</term> <note>the king of the gods in Vedic mythology</note>, for
the crime of murdering a <term>Brāhman</term> <note>a member of the highest Hindu caste, traditionally priests</note>, became incarnated
in the form of Kuttandavar, and a curse was laid upon
him that his body should rot away, leaving only the
head; with the result that no one would give him his
daughter in marriage; because, if they were married in
the morning, his body would rot away before the even-
ing and so the bride would become a widow and the <term>tali</term> <note>original: "tali"; a sacred wedding thread or necklace symbolizing marriage</note>
be cut. <term>Śrī Kṛiṣhṇa</term> <note>the god Krishna, an avatar of Vishnu</note>, however, took pity on him,
assumed the female form of <term>Mohinī</term> <note>the female avatar of Vishnu</note>, and consented
to be married to him in the morning, and then, as he
vanished all but the head, the wedding thread was cut in the even-
ing. In memory of this event, during the festival,
which is celebrated in the month of *Chitrai* <note>original: "Chitrai"; the first month of the Tamil calendar, roughly April–May</note> (April), a
crowd of men dressed as women come to the shrine
with wedding threads on their necks. In the evening at sunset the
wedding thread is cut, because the god has died and all the people
dressed as women have become widows. The festival,
therefore, is necessarily limited to the day-time. Fowls
and goats are sacrificed to the god a little distance in
front of the shrine. The festival is attended by all non-
Brahman castes. The people who showed me the
shrines said that Kuttandavar is so named from an
<term>Asura</term> <note>a titan or demon figure in Hindu mythology</note>, or Demon or Kuttu, whom the god killed. But
as Kuttandavar is especially the god of the actors or
dancers, or <term>Kuttadis</term> <note>original: "Kuttadis"; translated as "actors" or "dancers"</note>, who are very numerous in South
Arcot and are a sub-division of the <term>Padaiyachi</term> <note>a sub-group of the Vanniyar caste</note> caste, it
seems likely that the name is derived from *Kuttadi*
(a dancer or actor). I was told that wherever the
<term>Vaniyars</term> <note>referring to the Vanniyar community</note> or Padaiyachis are in great numbers, for instance,
in the South Arcot, Coimbatore and Salem districts,
and in the city of Madras, one is sure to see a large
number of shrines of the god Kuttandavar. The worship
of this god is, however, not considered to be very
respectable. There is apparently no immorality con-

connected with his worship, but more respectable members of the caste do not like men dressing like women. The members of the <term>Padaiyachi</term> <note>a Tamil caste, historically associated with agriculture and military service</note> caste, therefore, who have been educated in recent years and have risen in the social scale, tend to give up the worship of <term>Kuttandavar</term> <note>a deity associated with the sacrifice of Aravan; the festival often involves devotees taking on female personas</note>.

I have often seen on the seashore of <term>Madras</term> <note>the city now known as Chennai</note> a conical heap of sand, about three inches high, standing on a small platform of sand, with camphor and incense in a small earthenware vessel or in a heap of old netting. The conical heap of sand represents the goddess <term>Kanniamma</term> <note>the "Virgin Mother," a local protective deity of the fishing communities</note>, the *<term>village deity</term>* <note>original: "grāma-devatā"; the presiding guardian spirit of a specific locality or village</note> of the fishing village. The fishermen have told me that she is the goddess who gives them fish and enables them to make a living, and that they make these offerings to her when fish are scarce and they have reason to think that she is angry. This illustrates the characteristic feature of all animistic <note>the belief that objects, places, and creatures all possess a distinct spiritual essence</note> worship. Its chief if not only motive is to propitiate the angry deity. Probably something of the same feeling lurks beneath the custom of Roman Catholic fishermen, when they bring holy water from the church and sprinkle it on their nets after they have toiled all the day and caught nothing. Probably the object of this custom is to exorcise a malignant spirit from the nets.

In the <term>Mysore</term> <note>a region and former kingdom in southern India, now part of Karnataka</note> country I came across quite a different set of names for the village goddesses. At one village, near <term>Bangalore</term> <note>the capital city of the state of Karnataka</note>, the name of the goddess was <term>Maheśvaramma</term> <note>literally "Great Goddess"</note> (great goddess), also called <term>Savaramma</term> <note>meaning "she who rides on horseback"</note>. Her sister, <term>Doddamma</term> <note>meaning "Senior Mother"</note>, and her brother, <term>Muneśvara</term> <note>a protective male deity, often considered a saint or a form of Shiva</note>, share in the worship paid to her. At another village a goddess, called <term>Pūjamma</term> <note>literally "she who is worshipped"</note>, was shown to me. She was said to be the local goddess of the <term>Mādigas</term> <note>a Dalit community historically associated with leatherwork and music</note>, the lowest section of the <term>Outcastes</term> <note>a term formerly used for Dalit communities who were excluded from the traditional four-tier caste system</note> in the <term>Telugu</term> <note>referring to the language and culture of the region of Andhra Pradesh and Telangana</note> country; but at the same time the <term>Śūdras</term>¹ <note>the fourth of the traditional caste categories, usually consisting of laborers and artisans</note> make vows to her, to induce her to ward off diseases from their homes, and then fulfil their vows by sacrificing buffaloes or thrusting

...silver pins through their cheeks. <term>Annamma</term> <note>the "Mother of the South," recognized as the presiding goddess of Bangalore</note> is the principal goddess at another shrine in Bangalore City, and in the same shrine are six other deities: <term>Chandeśvaramma</term>, <term>Māyeśvaramma</term>, <term>Maramma</term> (the goddess of <term>cholera</term> <note>an acute diarrheal infection; historically a major cause of death in India, leading to the creation of specific protective deities</note>), <term>Udalamma</term> (the goddess of the swollen neck), <term>Kokkalamma</term> (the goddess of coughs), and <term>Sukhajamma</term> (the goddess of measles and small-pox).

At some villages a little distance from Bangalore the deity was simply the *<term>village deity</term>* <note>original: "grāma-devatā"</note>. In Mysore City the village deity is known as <term>Bisal-Mariamma</term> (the word *Bisal* in the <term>Kannada</term> <note>original: "Canarese"; the Dravidian language spoken primarily in the state of Karnataka</note> language means "sunlight," and I was told that *Māri* means *<term>energy</term>* <note>original: "śakti"</note> or power). The deity seems to have been originally connected with sun-worship. I was told that her shrines are never covered with a roof, and one of the symbols representing the deity is a brass pot full of water with a small mirror leaning against it, called *<term>Kunna-Kannadi</term>*, which means "eye-mirror."

There are seven <term>Mari</term> deities, all sisters, who are worshipped in Mysore. All the seven sisters are regarded vaguely as wives or sisters of <term>Shiva</term> <note>one of the principal deities of Hinduism, associated with destruction and transformation</note>.

In Mysore villages, <term>Mahādeva-Amma</term> (the Great Goddess) and <term>Huliamma</term> (the tiger-goddess) are found; and doubtless there are countless other names in the Mysore State for the many deities who are worshipped as the guardians of the villages and the averters of epidemics and other misfortunes.

It is quite probable that, originally, in South India the village goddesses had all quite simple names, such as <term>Uramma</term> or *<term>village deity</term>* <note>original: "Grāma-devatā"</note>, both meaning village goddess, or <term>Peddamma</term> (Great Mother), and that the imagination of the villagers gradually invented special titles for their own guardian deities. But at the present time the village deities consist of a most miscellaneous collection of spirits, good, bad, and indifferent, who

Those Hindus who worship <term>Kali</term> <note>a powerful goddess associated with time, change, and destruction</note>, the wife of Shiva, are called *<term>Shaktas</term>* <note>original: "śāktas"; followers of the Shakti tradition</note>. baffle all attempts at classification, counting, or explanation. A few of them, like <term>Mariamma</term> <note>a widely worshipped goddess of rain and disease, especially smallpox</note> and <term>Iyenar</term> <note>a male guardian deity, often depicted on a horse, protecting village boundaries</note>, have won their way to general respect or fear among the Tamil people; and, where <term>Brahman</term> <note>the priestly caste of traditional Hindu society</note> influence is strong, there has been an obvious attempt, as we have seen, to connect the village goddesses with the popular worship of <term>Shiva</term> or <term>Vishnu</term>; but it is more than doubtful whether, originally, they had anything to do with either <term>Shaivism</term> or <term>Vaishnavism</term> <note>the two main traditions of Hinduism focused on Shiva and Vishnu, respectively</note>. The stories told about them in the folklore of the people, which represent them as <term>avatars</term> <note>original: "avatāras"; divine incarnations</note>, that is, incarnations of Shiva, were probably quite late inventions, to account for names and ceremonies whose meaning had long been lost.

(b) The characters of the goddesses vary considerably. The villagers do not regard them as evil spirits, but neither do they regard them as unmixed benefactors. They are rather looked upon as beings of uncertain temper, very human in their liability to take offence. At <term>Cocanada</term> <note>modern-day Kakinada in Andhra Pradesh</note> the <term>pujaris</term> <note>village priests who perform daily rituals</note> told me that the village goddess, who is significantly called <term>Nukalamma</term>—from a colloquial Tamil word meaning "to beat"—causes all sorts of trouble and is dreaded as an evil spirit. But when an epidemic of <term>cholera</term> <note>an acute diarrheal infection; historically a major cause of death in India, leading to the creation of specific protective deities</note> breaks out, they, curiously enough, install another goddess, called <term>Maridiamma</term>, in her place, and offer sacrifices to her instead of to Nukalamma, a proceeding calculated, one would have thought, to give dire offence.

<term>Mahakali</term> <note>literally "Great Kali"</note>, that is, great Kali, is another form or incarnation of the same goddess. She is supposed to be a deity of furious temper, and to be the cause of the prevalence of cholera. She is also known as <term>Vira-Mahakali</term>¹ or <term>Ugra-Mahakali</term>,² to denote her rage and fury.

Another deity of similarly violent temper is <term>Angalamma</term>, who is worshipped largely in the Coimbatore district. The idea seems to be that all who worship the <term>Ashta Shakti</term> <note>original: "Ashṭa Śakti"; the Eight Powers of the Universe</note>, or eight powers of the—

---

¹ <note>original: "Vira"; a Sanskrit word meaning heroic.</note> Heroic.
² <note>original: "Ugra"; a Sanskrit word meaning fierce.</note> Fierce.

universe, will attain to bliss, while the others will be destroyed by **Angalamma**. The people worship her to avoid falling victims to her unquenchable anger, since her main object is believed to be to devour and consume everything that comes in her way. She is said especially to have a great relish for bones!

Another deity of a very different disposition is **Kamachiamma** <note>original: "Kāmāchīamma"; derived from the Sanskrit *Kāmākshī*, meaning "the love-eyed one," an epithet of the goddess Kali, wife of Shiva</note>, whose name implies that she is full of good and gracious qualities. She is reported to have been born a **Brahman** <note>the priestly caste</note> girl, and then to have become the <term>avatar</term> <note>original: "avatāra"; a divine incarnation</note> of one of the <term>Ashta Shakti</term> <note>literally the "Eight Powers," a group of eight primary manifestations of the Mother Goddess</note>.

Another benevolent deity is **Thuropathiyamma** <note>a regional name for Draupadi, the heroine of the Hindu epic *Mahabharata*</note>, who is reported to have been the wife of a <term>Rishi</term> <note>a sacred sage or seer</note> and a very virtuous woman; so, in her next birth, she was allowed to be born a king's daughter. Accordingly when **Thurupatham**, King of **Panchala**, offered a <term>sacrifice for a child</term> <note>original: "puthrayāgam" or "putrayāga"; a Vedic ritual performed to ensure the birth of a son</note> she came forth from the fire. She afterwards became the wife of the **Pandavas**, the five brothers famous in the great Hindu epic, the *Mahabharata*, and is regarded as one of the <term>Ashta Shakti</term>.

(c) The functions of the different goddesses are not at all clearly marked in the **Telugu** country <note>modern-day Andhra Pradesh and Telangana</note>. The people often told me "They are only different names for the same goddess." In some places there is a special cholera goddess, *e.g.* **Ankamma**, and in others a special small-pox goddess, *e.g.* **Gangamma**; but as a rule the infliction and removal of epidemics and disasters is a general function of all goddesses alike. On the other hand, in the **Coimbatore**, **Tanjore**, and **Trichinopoly** districts of the **Tamil** country, where the people have been for many generations past far more influenced by civilization and <term>Brahmanism</term> <note>the religious system and social structure dominated by the Brahman caste</note> than in the **Telugu** country, I found that the functions of different deities were far more differentiated and that often elaborate stories were current as to their origin and characters. For example, one of the deities worshipped in almost

...every village in the Tamil country <note>the region of Tamil Nadu</note> is **Mariamma** or **Māri**, the goddess of small-pox.

It is noticeable that **Mariamma** is not found in any temples dedicated to one of the seven sisters, as she is considered superior to them in power and much worse in temper. The seven sisters are supposed to be kind and indulgent, while **Mariamma** is vindictive and inexorable and difficult to propitiate <note>to win the favor of or appease</note>. The boundary goddess is worshipped in the **Tanjore** <note>modern Thanjavur</note> district under the name of **Kāli**, and her special function is to prevent any evil coming from without into the village of which she is the guardian, while the seven sisters are supposed to guard against any evil arising within the village itself. Though **Mariamma** keeps herself aloof from the seven sisters, I came across, in the **South Arcot** district, a shrine dedicated to **Kanniamma** (who was said to be another form of **Mariamma** and to preside over small-pox), in which were clay images of seven brothers. The youngest, called **Muni** <note>literally "ghost" or "spirit"</note>, was the tallest and was represented by a larger clay figure seated on a raised platform, with his six smaller brothers standing beside him.

In the Tamil districts of **Tanjore**, **Trichinopoly** <note>modern Tiruchirappalli</note> and **Cuddalore**, the names of village deities most commonly met with are **Pidāri**, which is often used as a generic name of village deities, **Mariamma**, **Kāli**, **Seliamma**, **Draupati**,¹ and **Aṅgalamma**. **Mariamma** is the commonest of them all, and her function is always to inflict or ward off small-pox. **Pidāri** is supposed to act as guardian against evil spirits and epidemics, especially cholera. **Kāli** is often regarded as especially the protectress against evil spirits that haunt forests and desolate places, and against wild beasts. In some parts she is the special goddess of the bird-catchers. But in some villages she is also the guardian against cholera. Except, however, in the villages near **Tanjore**, I have not met with **Kāli** in the capacity of a boundary goddess. In other places there are curious ceremonies

¹ This is for **Draupadī**, the heroine of the *Mahābhārata* <note>one of the two major Sanskrit epics of ancient India</note>.

3

connected with the **boundary-stone** <note>original: "ellai-kal"</note>, as it is called, and I was told in one village that in the boundary-stone reside evil spirits, which it is the object of the ceremonies to propitiate <note>to win the favor of or appease</note>. In another village I found that there was a festival to a goddess called **Boundary-Pidāri** <note>original: "Ellai-Pidāri"; see below and cf. p. 101</note>.

(*d*) *Male deities.* Next to **Mariamma**, the deity that is most universally worshipped among the **Tamils** is **Iyenar** <note>also spelled Ayyanar; a protective male deity</note>, and, as already stated, he is the one village deity, largely worshipped in the Tamil country, who seems to be an exception to the general rule that the village deities are female. In almost every Tamil village there is a shrine of **Iyenar**, who is regarded as the watchman of the village, and is supposed to patrol it every night, mounted on a ghostly steed, a terrible sight to behold, scaring away the evil spirits. He has always a separate shrine, and is not, like **Munadian** and the **Hero of Madurai** <note>original: "Madurai-Vīran"; Vīran is the Tamil form of Vīra, meaning "hero"</note>, simply an attendant of a local goddess. His shrine may be known by the clay or concrete figures of horses ranged on either side of the image or piled about in the compound of the shrine in admired confusion. The horses are offered by devotees, and represent the steeds on which he rides in his nightly rounds. He is regarded by the villagers as a good and benevolent protector, of far higher character than the disreputable **Hero of Madurai**.

Another male deity, of much inferior character to **Iyenar**, who is sometimes worshipped separately, is **Karuppanna** <note>literally "Black Brother"</note>. As a rule he is simply one of the subordinate male attendants of the village goddess: but in some places I have met with separate shrines to **Karuppanna**, where he presides as the chief deity. At one of these shrines worship was offered exclusively by **Pariahs** <note>the chief group of Outcastes in the Tamil country, now generally referred to as Dalits</note>. At another place the evil spirit residing in the boundary-stone was called **Boundary-Karuppu** <note>original: "Ellai-Karuppu"</note>.

In one village in the **Trichinopoly** <note>modern Tiruchirappalli</note> district, I came

...across a male deity known as **King Father** <note>original: "Rāja Vayan"</note>, who was represented by four or five stakes, about five or six feet high, with iron spear-heads on top. The spears were stuck on one side of a stone platform under a tamarind and an areca tree, and reminded me of the wooden stakes representing **Potu-Razu** <note>a male deity in the Telugu-speaking regions, often considered a brother or companion to the village goddesses</note> in the **Telugu country** <note>the region comprising modern-day Andhra Pradesh and Telangana</note>. Whether these independent and semi-independent male deities have in all cases developed out of the subordinate male attendants of the village deities, or whether they belong to another **Dravidian cult** <note>referring to the indigenous religious traditions of South India that predate or exist alongside Brahmanical Hinduism</note>, it is difficult to say.

**Key Terms on this Page:**
*   **Village Deity** <note>original: "Grāma-devatā"</note>: The protective spirit or divinity of a specific village.
*   **Shrine**: The physical structure or "house" of the deity.
*   **Stones**: Used as symbols or "aniconic" representations, meaning the deity is honored through a physical object that does not have a human or animal form.

# CHAPTER II

## THE CULT

### SHRINES, SYMBOLS, MINISTRANTS, FESTIVALS

*Shrines.* The shrines of the village deities, lacking in uniformity or beauty, are characteristic of this whole system of religion. They represent the dwelling-places of minor local deities <note>original: "petty local deities"</note> concerned with the affairs of a small local community. They express the humble nature <note>original: "meanness"</note> of a religion rooted in fear. There is nothing about them to suggest feelings of adoration or love. Some of the shrines, especially in the Tamil country <note>the region of South India where Tamil is spoken</note>, are fairly large buildings, ornamented with stylized <note>original: "grotesque"</note> figures, almost rivaling in size and architectural features the local temples of **Shiva** <note>original: "Śiva"; one of the principal deities of Hinduism, often associated with transformation</note> and **Vishnu** <note>one of the principal deities of Hinduism, known as the Preserver</note>. The shrines of **Ayyanar** <note>original: "Iyenar"</note> are distinguished by figures of horses great and small, on which he is supposed to ride round the village every night to chase away the evil spirits. But the majority of the shrines are humble little brick buildings of various shapes and sizes, often no more than four or five feet high, with a rough figure of the deity inside, carved in low relief <note>original: "bas-relief"</note> on a small stone. In many villages the shrine is simply a rough stone platform under a tree, with stones or iron spears stuck on it to represent the deity. Often a large rough stone with no carving on it is stuck up in a field or under a tree, and serves for shrine and image alike. The boundary-stone of the village lands is very commonly regarded as a habitation of a local deity, and might be called a shrine or symbol with equal propriety.¹ In many villages of

---

¹ See above, p. 33.

<summary>This page describes the architectural variety and humble nature of village deity shrines in South India, noting the use of stone platforms, boundary markers, and the iconic terracotta horses of Ayyanar.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of local shrines from the previous page, shifting focus from the permanent structures of the Tamil-speaking regions to the temporary practices found in the Telugu-speaking regions. It transitions from describing the buildings themselves to the specific symbols and idols used to represent the village deities.</meta>

the Telugu country <note>the region of South India where the Telugu language is spoken, primarily modern-day Andhra Pradesh and Telangana</note> there is no permanent shrine at all, but a temporary one is put up made of bamboo and cloth to accommodate the deity whenever a festival is held. It seems probable that this “tent of meeting” <note>original: "tent of meeting"; a reference to the portable dwelling place of the divine, similar to the biblical Tabernacle described in the Bible</note> represents the primitive use, and that the permanent shrine was a later development, when individual worshippers began to make offerings in times of domestic trouble, and when the village community as a whole realized more fully the need of help and protection in the ordinary affairs of daily life.

*Symbols.* The images or symbols, by which the village deities are represented, are almost as diverse as their names. In some of the more primitive villages there is no permanent image or symbol of the deity at all; but a clay figure of the goddess is made by the potter, or the goldsmith, for each festival and then cast away beyond the boundaries of the village when the festival is ended. In other villages the deity is represented simply by a stone pillar standing in a field, or on a stone platform under a tree, or in a small enclosure surrounded by a stone wall. Often the stones, which represent the different deities, are simply small conical stones not more than five or six inches high, blackened with the anointing oil <note>the practice of pouring oil or clarified butter over a sacred object as an act of devotion</note>. It is difficult to see anything at all peculiar in them which in any way fits them to be symbols of the goddesses or their male attendants. In more civilized parts a slab of stone has the figure of a woman roughly carved upon it, sometimes with four, six, or eight arms, holding various implements in her hands, sometimes with only two arms, and sometimes with none at all.

Here is the description of a typical image which I saw in the Trichinopoly district <note>now known as Tiruchirappalli, a city and district in the state of Tamil Nadu</note>. It was a stone figure of a woman, about two and a half feet high, with eight arms, and in her hands a knife, a shield, a bell, a devil’s head <note>likely representing the severed head of a demon or "asura"</note>, a drum, a three-pronged fork <note>original: "three-pronged fork"; a trident or "trishula," often associated with the god Shiva</note>, a goad, and a piece of rope:¹ truly a collection of articles worthy of a schoolboy’s pocket! Another image of the goddess

---

¹ Most of these objects appear in the hands of images of **Shiva** <note>original: "Śiva"; one of the principal deities of Hinduism, often associated with destruction and transformation</note> or of his wife **Kali** <note>original: "Kālī"; a powerful goddess associated with time, change, and empowerment</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of physical representations of deities, moving from the temporary structures of the Telugu regions described on the previous page to the portable metal images and ritual pots used in the Tamil districts. # THE CULT

A statue made of the five metals (gold, silver, brass, copper, and lead) <term>panchaloha: a traditional five-metal alloy used for Hindu icons, believed to possess sacred properties</term> was kept, strangely enough, in the temple of Shiva <note>one of the principal deities of Hinduism, often associated with transformation and destruction</note>, about two hundred yards away, for use in processions. It is very common in the Tamil districts <note>regions in South India where the Tamil language is spoken, primarily the modern state of Tamil Nadu</note> to find a stone image fixed in the shrine, and a small portable metal image, which is used in processions during the festival.¹

Very often, too, the goddess is represented in processions by a brass pot filled with water and decorated with margosa² leaves. I saw one of these brass pots in a shrine of Kaliamma <note>a local village form of the goddess Kali</note> at Shiyali <note>modern-day Sirkazhi</note>, in the Tanjore <note>modern Thanjavur</note> district. It was about a foot high and a foot in diameter at the base, and had four tubes sticking out just below the neck. In other Tamil villages, where the image is fixed in the shrine and there is no metal image to carry in procession, an earthenware pot is used, filled with water and decorated with margosa leaves.

At Irungalur, in the Trichinopoly <note>modern Tiruchirappalli</note> district, I found a small enclosure sacred to Kurumbaiamma <note>a local village goddess</note>, outside the village, without any image or sacred stones in it at all, and I was told that during the festival a small *pandal* <term>pandal: a temporary booth or canopy made of leaves and poles</term> is erected in the enclosure, under which a small earthen pot, curiously decorated, is placed to represent the goddess. The pot is filled with water, and has a silver two-anna piece <note>an old Indian coin; "2d." refers to its equivalent value of two pence in British currency at the time</note> put inside it. Some coconut and oleander flowers are stuck in the mouth of the pot, surrounded and concealed by a sheaf of mango leaves, tied together by tender shoots of the banana tree. This bunch of mango leaves is then decorated with flowers, a small pointed stick of bamboo, with a lime stuck on the end, is inserted at the top of the bunch, and by the side of the lime a small silver umbrella with a silver handle. The decoration varies locally. This decorated pot is placed on a small platform of sand, and about eight measures of rice are heaped round the base of it. It is called *karagam* <term>karagam: a ritual vessel used in South Indian folk religion to represent a deity</term>, and is carefully prepared at the chief local shrine of Kurumbaiamma,

<margin>² The margosa or neem tree is an evergreen bearing white flowers, *Melia Azadirachta* <note>original Latin botanical name: "Melia Azadirachta"</note>, and is frequently associated with village divinities.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the description of rituals involving the karagam (sacred pot) used in the Tamil districts of South India.
