about a mile outside the village, and during the festival is treated exactly like the goddess. It is taken round in procession on the head of a <term>pūjārī: a priest who performs ritual worship</term> to the sound of <term>tom-toms: traditional barrel-shaped drums</term>¹ and pipes; offerings of fruit and flowers are made to it; a lamb is sacrificed before it, and it is worshipped with the orthodox prostrations.

The use made of the <term>karagam: a ritual pot, often made of brass or clay, filled with water and decorated with leaves</term> is also worth notice. The following is from an article by Mr. F. J. Richards, of the Indian Civil Service:

> “The cholera goddess <note>a deity believed to control or cause outbreaks of cholera, a common and deadly disease in historical South India</note> is popularly believed to be the mother of the washerman <note>the village official responsible for cleaning clothes; in many South Indian village traditions, specific castes hold hereditary ritual roles</note>. He is therefore chosen to officiate as the priest, as the son alone can hope to succeed in propitiating <note>appeasing or calming</note> such a fierce divinity.
> 
> “A sacred pot is prepared; and the village washerman bathes early in the morning and places it on his head. Then, holding a sickle in one hand and <term>margosa: leaves from the Neem tree, known for their medicinal and purifying properties</term> in the other, he goes through the village dancing. Before the procession takes place, all the villagers pour large quantities of <term>ragi gruel: a porridge made from finger millet, a staple grain in South India</term> into the big iron buckets used for baling water. When two or three of such buckets are filled, the poor people of the village are fed. The washerman dances at the place where the food is distributed. After dusk, when the procession passes through the village, sheep are sacrificed at the important centres in the village, and the blood collected in a mud vessel. The washerman, with the sacred pot on his head, goes on dancing through the limits of the village, preceded by the village musicians. At the point where his village borders on the adjoining village he places the sacred pot and the blood which had been collected at the different places of sacrifice, and returns home after taking a bath on his way. The goddess is believed to be propitiated by this, and any further attacks of cholera are attributed to the perfunctory <note>careless or half-hearted</note> discharge of this duty by the washerman. The sacrificial victims are sheep only, and the method of sacrifice is decapitation. The deity is thus propitiated and

---

¹ A tom-tom is a native drum. It is usually shaped like a small barrel, and beaten at both ends with the hands and fingers.

# THE CULT

carried beyond the village limits. The villagers of the adjacent villages in their turn carry the <term>karagam</term> <note>a decorated sacred pot representing the goddess</note> to the border of the next village, and in this way the <term>karagam</term> traverses many miles of country, and the baleful influence of the goddess is transferred to a safe distance."¹

At another village I found that <term>Kālīamma</term> <note>a local village manifestation of the goddess Kali</note> was represented by seven brass pots, without any water in them, one above the other, with <term>margosa</term> <note>also known as Neem, a tree sacred to the goddess and known for its medicinal properties</note> leaves stuck into the mouth of the topmost pot, as well as by an earthenware pot filled with water and also adorned with <term>margosa</term> leaves. It is possible that the seven brass pots represent seven sisters, or the seven virgins sometimes found in <term>Tamil</term> <note>relating to the people and language of the South Indian state of Tamil Nadu</note> shrines. The people themselves have no idea what they mean, but can only say that it is <term>custom</term> <note>original: "Mamul"</note>.

At Mysore City, in the <term>Canarese</term> <note>referring to the Kannada-speaking region of Karnataka</note> country, I found, as stated above,² that the goddess was represented by a small metal pot full of water with a small mirror leaning against it. In the mouth of the pot two, four, or six <term>betel</term>³ leaves are placed, always an even number, and the pot is decorated with a bunch of cocoanut flowers. The pot is called <term>Eye-mirror</term> <note>original: "Kunna-Kannadi"</note>, or <term>vessel</term> <note>original: "Kalsa"</note>, and is used, I was told, as a symbol of deity in the preliminary ceremonies of all the <term>Brāhmans</term> <note>the priestly class of Hindu society</note>. It is evidently connected with sun-worship, which in Mysore seems to have strongly influenced the cult of the village deities.

Another curious symbol much used in Mysore is called <term>ārati</term>.⁴ It consists of a lamp made of rice flour about six or eight inches high, with the image of a face

---

¹ *Quarterly Journal of the Mythic Society*, Jan., 1920, p. 108.
² P. 29.
³ <term>Betel</term> is a pepper plant, the leaf of which is wrapped round the nut of the areca palm and eaten by Indians as a digestive.
⁴ The waving of a lamp in front of an image of a god is an orthodox Hindu custom. It is also frequently observed in the case of kings and other great personages. The object is to ward off the evil eye and other harmful influences. It is performed only by married women or <term>professional dancers</term> <note>original: "nautch-girls"</note>. The name of the lamp and of the act of waving is <term>ārati</term>. See Dubois, *Hindu Manners and Customs*, p. 148. Hence the symbol described in the text.

roughly represented on one side of it by pieces of silver and blotches of <term>kunkuma</term> <note>original: "kunkuṁa"; a sacred red powder made from turmeric and lime, used for making ritual marks</note>, red paste, stuck on to represent the eyes, nose, mouth, and so on. Sticks of incense were stuck in the lamp all round, and on the top were about four <term>betel</term> <note>leaves from the piper betle plant, often used in rituals and as a mild stimulant</note> leaves stuck upright and forming a sort of cup with a wreath of white flowers below them. An *<term>arati</term>* <note>a ritual lamp or the act of waving a flame before a deity</note> was brought to me at Mysore by the *<term>pujaris</term>* <note>village priests</note> for my inspection. It was a quaint object, and seemed like the relic of some harvest festival of bygone days.

A common symbol of the village deities is simply a stick or a spear. It is very common in the <term>Tamil</term> <note>referring to the people and language of the state of Tamil Nadu</note> country to see one or more iron spears stuck in the ground under a tree, to represent some village deity. The idea seems to be that the deity is represented by his weapons. In the <term>Telugu</term> <note>referring to the people and language of the states of Andhra Pradesh and Telangana</note> country <term>Potu-Razu</term> <note>a male deity, typically the brother or husband of the village goddess, serving as a guardian</note>, the brother or husband of the village goddess, is sometimes represented by a stone, sometimes by a thin wooden stake, like an attenuated post, about four or five feet high and roughly carved at the top. It faintly resembles a spear, and is called *<term>shulam</term>* <note>original: "śūlam"</note>, which in <term>Telugu</term> means a spear.² Sometimes this stake stands beside a slab of stone representing <term>Potu-Razu</term>. At one village the symbol of <term>Potu-Razu</term> is a painted image made of wood, about three feet high, representing a warrior, sitting down with a sword in his hand, and carrying a lime and nine glass bangles belonging to his sister <term>Ellamma</term> <note>a prominent mother goddess in South Indian tradition</note>. Beside each foot is the figure of a cock, and in the shrine is kept a large painted mask for the *<term>pujari</term>* to wear at festivals, as he dances round the image of <term>Potu-Razu</term>. But elaborate images of <term>Potu-Razu</term> of this kind are not very often found.

Another symbol akin to these stakes and spears is the <term>Nattan Kal</term> in the <term>Tamil</term> country. <term>Nattan</term> means “planted” and <term>Kal</term> means “a stone” or “post.” The <term>Nattan Kal</term> is the first post of a nuptial booth, set up at an auspicious moment, painted red and white, adorned

it must be admitted that all explanations can only be
regarded as more or less probable hypotheses.

figures of serpents. Within the shrine there is no
image of any kind, but on the left-hand side of the door
is a platform, covered with garlands of white flowers,

with a small earthenware lamp upon it, which is kept burning day and night as a symbol of the goddess.

To the right of this shrine stands a smaller one dedicated to a goddess called Dalamma. No one in the village could tell me who the goddess was nor what her name meant. There was no image nor lamp nor symbol of any kind in her shrine. An old picture frame, hung up on the wall to the left, without any picture in it, was the only attempt at decoration or symbolism. Just within the doorway was a shallow trough about one and a half feet long, one foot broad, and two inches deep, where the worshippers break their cocoanuts.

In front of the larger shrine stood an enclosure about five or six yards square, enclosed by a stone wall, with four slabs of stone in the centre, on which a platform is erected, covered by a canopy of cloth and leaves, during the annual festival. The lighted lamp is then brought out from the shrine, placed under the canopy, and worshipped as the symbol of the goddess. Apparently cattle are tethered in the enclosure at other times, and, when I saw it, there were no obvious marks of sanctity about it. About twenty yards off stands the Cattle Stone, a slab of rough stone about five feet high and three feet broad, set upon a stone platform about one and a half feet high. When the cattle get sore feet, their owners pour curds <note>thickened yogurt or dahi, commonly used in rituals to "cool" a deity's fever or bring healing</note> over the Cattle Stone for their recovery.

Near the Cattle Stone, in a field on the outskirts of the houses, stands a square stone pillar, about five feet high and half a foot in thickness, without any carving or ornament on it whatever. It represents Maramma, the goddess of small-pox and other epidemics, a most malignant spirit. Apparently she had been brought to this village by some people who had migrated from another village called Hethana; whence she is called Maramma-Hethana. Buffaloes and sheep are offered to her whenever epidemics break out.

The <term>grāma-devatā</term> <note>original: "grāma-devatā"; the tutelary "village deity" who protects the community from harm and disease</note> herself—she has no other name—has in this village no permanent image. The gold-

One striking feature of this festival is that on the first day of the festival a woman comes from every household to the place of worship with a lighted lamp made of rice flour, called the <term>waving of lights</term> <note>original: "āratī"</note>; and they all together wave their lamps in a circle from left to right above their heads and from right to left below.¹ When the festival is over, the washerman of the village, who acts as the <term>priest</term> <note>original: "pūjārī"; a non-Brahman who performs ritual offerings</note>, accompanied by all the villagers, takes the image to the tank <note>a ritual pond or reservoir</note>, walks into the water, and leaves it there. In some villages in the Mysore State the <term>waving of lights</term> <note>original: "āratī"</note> is presented by the men, the heads of the households, and not by the women. But in all the annual festivals in these parts the presentation of the <term>waving of lights</term>, which seems often to be regarded as a symbol of the deity herself, forms an important part of the ceremonial.

## *Ministrants.*

One of the most striking features of the worship of the village deities is the absence of anything like a <term>sacerdotal caste</term> <note>a hereditary class of priests, such as the Brahmans</note> in connexion with it. Every other department of village work belongs to a special caste, and in the ordinary worship of <term>Vishnu</term> <note>the Hindu god of preservation</note> and <term>Śiva</term> <note>the Hindu god of destruction and transformation</note> the priestly caste of the <term>Brahmans</term> <note>the highest social class in traditional Hindu society, responsible for religious rites</note> is supreme. But in the worship of the village deities the <term>priests</term> <note>original: "pūjārīs"</note> are drawn from all the lower castes indiscriminately, though in any one village the <term>priests</term> of a particular goddess nearly always belong to one particular caste.

I have occasionally found a <term>Brahman</term> in charge of a <term>village deity</term> <note>original: "grāma-devatā"</note> shrine in the Tamil country. But then, as I have noted above, the <term>Brahman priest</term> never takes any part in the animal sacrifices, and, even so, is degraded by his connexion with the shrine. In the Telugu country the potters and the washermen, who are <term>laborers</term> <note>original: "Śūdras"; the fourth social class in the varna system</note> of low caste, often officiate as priests, and

---

...an important part, especially in the buffalo sacrifices, is taken by the *Mālās* and *Mādigās*. <note>The *Mālās* and *Mādigās* are the primary Dalit communities in the Telugu-speaking regions. Though historically marginalized as "Outcastes," they hold indispensable roles in village religious life.</note>

A *Mādiga* nearly always kills the buffalo and performs the unpleasant ceremonies connected with the sprinkling of the blood, and there are certain families among the *Mālās*, called *Asādis*, who are the nearest approach to a priestly caste in connection with the village deities. They have the hereditary right to assist at the sacrifices, to chant the praises of the goddess while the sacrifices are being offered, and to perform certain ceremonies. But in the more primitive villages, where, it may be presumed, primitive customs prevail, it is remarkable how great a variety of people take an official part in the worship: the potter, the carpenter, the <term>toddy-drawer</term> <note>a worker who extracts sap from palm trees to produce fermented liquor</note>, the washerman, *Mālās* and *Mādigās*, and even the <term>Brāhman village accountant</term> <note>original: "Karṇam"</note>, have all their parts to play.

In the Tamil country <note>the region of modern-day Tamil Nadu</note> this is not so marked, and the details of the worship are left far more to the regular <term>priest</term> <note>original: "pūjārī"</note>. It is noticeable that the office of <term>priest</term> is by no means an honourable one, and this is especially the case among the Tamils, where <term>Brāhman</term> <note>the highest priestly caste in the Hindu hierarchy</note> influence is strong and the shedding of blood is regarded with aversion. And even among the <term>Brāhmans</term> themselves, though they owe their influence to the fact that they are the priestly caste, the men who serve the temples are regarded as having a lower position in the caste than those <term>Brāhmans</term> engaged in secular pursuits.

Among the <term>Canarese</term> <note>speakers of the Kannada language</note> in the Bellary district, the *Asādis* take a similar part in the worship to the *Asādis* in the Telugu country. In the whole of the Bellary district there are about sixty families of them living in three separate villages. They form practically a separate caste or section of the Outcastes. They eat food given them by the *Mādigās* and take their girls in marriage. The *Asādi* girls, however, never marry, but

-># THE CULT<-

are made <term>Basavīs</term>¹ <note>women dedicated to a temple or deity, historically often living as prostitutes</note>, that is, they are consecrated to the goddess and become prostitutes. Certainly the degradation of religion in India is seen only too plainly in the degradation of the priesthood.

*Festivals.* There is no act of uniformity and no ecclesiastical calendar regulating the festivals or forms of worship of village deities, and no universal custom as to the appointment of ministrants. In some villages, where there is a permanent shrine, offerings of rice, fruit, and flowers, with incense and camphor, are made every day by the villagers, who have made vows to the goddess, through the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>a village priest who conducts the ritual offerings</note>. Often offerings are made once or twice a week, on fixed days, consisting chiefly of grain, fruit, and flowers and occasionally of goats, sheep, and fowls. In many places there is a fixed annual festival, which sometimes takes place after harvest, when the people are at leisure and well off for food; but there is no regular rule as to the time, and the custom varies widely in different districts. In most places, however, there is no regular annual festival, but sacrifices are offered whenever an epidemic or any other calamity occurs which may make it expedient to <term>propitiate</term> <note>to win the favor of a god or spirit by offering a sacrifice or performing a ritual</note> the goddess. In some villages old men complained to me that, whereas formerly sacrifices were offered yearly, now, owing to the decay of religion, they are only offered once in four or five years. So, again, there is no uniformity as to the duration of a festival. Generally it lasts about a week, but in the <term>Tamil</term> <note>referring to the people and language of southeastern India</note> country it is sometimes a very elaborate affair, lasting for a fortnight, three weeks, or even a whole month; so too in some parts of the <term>Canarese</term> <note>an older term for the Kannada-speaking people and region of Karnataka</note> country the <term>Māri</term> <note>referring to Mariamman, a popular goddess of rain and infectious diseases</note> festival, which is held in February, lasts for about four weeks. But a long festival is an expensive luxury, which only a large town or a well-to-do village is able to afford. Speaking generally, the object of the festival is simply to propitiate the goddess—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "The Cult," specifically detailing the motivation behind the worship of village deities. It transitions from the frequency of sacrifices to the underlying fear of evil spirits that drives these rituals.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

->### THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA<-

and to avert epidemics and other calamities from the village, and to ward off the attacks of evil spirits.

Every village in South India is believed by the people to be surrounded by evil spirits, who are always on the watch to inflict diseases and misfortunes of all kinds on the unhappy villagers. They lurk everywhere, on the tops of <term>palmyra trees</term> <note>the *Borassus flabellifer*, a tall fan palm tree common in the region, often associated in folklore with spirits</note>, in caves and rocks, in ravines and chasms. They fly about in the air, like birds of prey, ready to pounce down upon any unprotected victim, and the Indian villagers pass through life in constant dread of these invisible enemies. So the poor people turn for protection to the <term>guardian deities</term> <note>local goddesses, usually known as *Gramadevata*, who protect a specific boundary or community</note> of their village, whose function it is to ward off these evil spirits and protect the village from epidemics of cholera, small-pox, or fever, from cattle disease, failure of crops, childlessness, fires, and all the manifold ills that flesh is heir to in an Indian village.

The sole object, then, of the worship of these village deities is to <term>propitiate</term> <note>to appease or win the favor of a spirit, usually through sacrifice, to prevent them from causing harm</note> them and to avert their wrath. There is no idea of praise and thanksgiving, no expression of gratitude or love, no desire for any spiritual or moral blessings. The one object is to get rid of cholera, small-pox, cattle disease, or drought, or to avert some of the minor evils of life. The worship, therefore, in most of the villages, only takes place occasionally. Sometimes, as I have stated above, there are daily offerings made to the deity; but, as a rule, the worship is confined to one big sacrifice, which takes place once a year, or on the occasion of some special disaster or outbreak of disease. The general attitude of the villager towards his village deity is “Let sleeping dogs lie.” So long as everything goes on well and there is no disease afflicting man or beast, and no drought nor other great calamity, it seems safest to let her alone. But, when misfortune comes, it is a sign that she is out of temper, and it is time to take steps to appease her wrath.

I have dignified the periodical sacrifices to the village goddesses by the name of festivals. But the term is a misnomer. There is really nothing of a

<summary>This page explains that the primary motivation for worshipping village deities in South India is the fear of malevolent spirits and the need to appease goddesses to prevent disaster. It characterizes the religion as one of propitiation rather than devotion, often triggered only by the onset of misfortune.</summary>
<keywords>evil spirits, propitiation, village deities, South India, epidemics, sacrifice, religious psychology, guardian goddesses, palmyra trees</keywords>

->### THE CULT<-

festal character about them. They are only gloomy and weird rites for the <term>propitiation</term> <note>the act of appeasing a god or spirit, often through ritual or sacrifice, to prevent them from causing harm</note> of angry <term>deities</term> <note>in this context, the local goddesses or "Gramadevata" who oversee specific villages</note> or the driving away of evil spirits, and it is very difficult to detect any traces of a spirit of thankfulness or praise. Even the term worship is hardly correct. The object of all the various rites and ceremonies is not to worship the deity in any true sense of the word, but simply to propitiate it and avert its wrath.

A brief description of the sacrifices and offerings themselves will make this clear. But I must premise that, as with the names and images and shrines, so with the offerings and sacrifices, there is no law of uniformity: the variations of local use and custom are innumerable. Still, the accounts here given will give a fair idea of the general type of rites and ceremonies prevalent throughout South India, in the propitiation of village deities.

<summary>This page explains that rituals for South Indian village deities are driven by the need to appease angry spirits rather than by gratitude. It notes that while these sacrificial practices vary greatly by location, they share a common goal of averting divine or supernatural wrath.</summary>

# CHAPTER IV

## MODES OF WORSHIP IN THE TELUGU COUNTRY

LET us suppose that an attack of cholera or small-pox has broken out in a village of South India. We will take a village in the <term>Telugu country</term> <note>the region in South India where the Telugu language is spoken, primarily modern-day Andhra Pradesh and Telangana</note>, in one of the more backward districts, where life is lived under more primitive conditions than in places where large towns and railways and the influence of the <term>Brāhmans</term> <note>the priestly and scholarly class in the Hindu social hierarchy</note> have tended to change old-fashioned ideas and customs.

*A Telugu Village.* The village deity, in this particular village, is called <term>Peddamma</term> <note>literally "the great mother" in the Telugu language</note>. The epidemic is a sign that she is angry and requires to be <term>propitiated</term> <note>appeased or calmed through ritual action</note>. So a collection is made for the expenses of a festival, or a rich man offers to pay all expenses, and a propitious day is selected, which in this village may be any day except Sunday or Thursday. Then the potter of the village is instructed to make a clay image of the great mother, and the carpenter to make a small wooden cart, and a buffalo is chosen as the chief victim for the sacrifice.

When the appointed day arrives, the buffalo is sprinkled all over with yellow <term>turmeric</term> <note>original: "Curcuma longa"</note>, while garlands of <term>margosa</term> <note>leaves from the neem tree, which are considered sacred and possess cooling, medicinal properties</note> leaves are hung round its neck and tied to its horns. At about two p.m. it is conducted round the village in procession to the sound of music and the beating of <term>tom-toms</term> <note>traditional drums</note>. The two sections of the <term>Outcastes</term> <note>historically marginalized groups living outside the main village social structure, now often referred to as Dalits</note>, the <term>Mālās</term> and the <term>Mādigās</term> <note>specific communities in the Telugu-speaking region who hold traditional and essential roles in village festivals</note>, take the leading

---

¹ <note>original: "Curcuma longa"</note> Turmeric is an Indian plant from the rootstock of which a powder called turmeric is extracted. This powder is used as a dye and also as one of the ingredients of curry-powder.

<summary>This page illustrates the specific ritual response of a Telugu village to an epidemic, focusing on the preparations to appease the goddess Peddamma. It details the communal effort involving various tradespeople and the beginning of a ceremonial procession featuring a sacrificial buffalo.</summary>

<keywords>Telugu country, Peddamma, South Indian ritual, epidemic, goddess propitiation, buffalo sacrifice, Mālās, Mādigās, turmeric, margosa</keywords>

->49<-
->4<-

part in the sacrifice, and conduct the buffalo from house to house. One <term>Mādigā</term> <note>a member of a South Indian community traditionally tasked with leatherwork and ritual drumming</note> goes on ahead, with a <term>tom-tom</term> <note>a traditional hand drum</note>, to announce that “the buffalo devoted to the goddess is coming.” The people then come out from their houses, bow down to worship the buffalo, and pour water over his feet, and also give some food to the <term>Mālās</term> <note>a community traditionally working as agricultural laborers and musicians</note> and Mādigās, who form the procession. By about eight p.m. this ceremony is finished, and the buffalo is brought to an open spot in the village and tied up near a small canopy of cloths supported on bamboo poles, which has been set up for the reception of the goddess.

All the villagers then assemble at the same place, and at about ten p.m. they go in procession, with music and tom-toms and torches, to the house of the potter, where the clay image is ready prepared. On arriving at his house, they pour about two and a half measures of rice on the ground and put the image on the top of it, adorned with a new cloth and jewels. All who are present then worship the image, and a ram is killed, its head being cut off with a large chopper, and the blood sprinkled on the top of the image, as a kind of consecration.

The potter then takes up the idol and carries it out of the house for a little distance, and gives it to a <term>washerman</term> <note>in this ritual context, the washerman acts as a ceremonial carrier of the deity</note>, who carries it to the place where the canopy has been set up to receive it. During the procession the people flourish sticks and swords and spears to keep off the evil spirits, and, for the same purpose, cut limes in half and throw them up in the air. The idea is that the greedy demons will clutch at the golden limes and carry them off, and so be diverted from any attack on the man who carries the image.

When the idol has been duly deposited under the canopy, another procession is made to the house of the <term>toddy-drawer</term> <note>a person whose occupation is to climb palm trees and collect the sap used for fermentation</note>. He is the man who climbs the palm trees and draws off the juice which is made into <term>toddy</term> <note>a traditional alcoholic beverage made from fermented palm sap</note>. At his house some rice is cooked, and a pot of toddy and a bottle of <term>arrack</term>¹ <note>a potent distilled alcoholic spirit</note> are produced and duly smeared with yellow <term>turmeric</term> <note>a bright yellow spice used for purification and ritual blessing</note> and a red paste, constantly used in religious

---

¹ Arrack is a native intoxicant.

->50<-

...worship among the Hindus and called *<term>kunkuma</term>* <note>original: "kunkuma"; a red powder made of turmeric mixed with lime, used for ritual markings</note>. The cooked rice is put in front of the pot of palm wine <note>original: "toddy"</note> and the bottle of distilled liquor <note>original: "arrack"</note>, a ram is killed in sacrifice, and then the palm-wine drawer worships the pot and the bottle. The village officials pay him his fee—three-eighths of a measure of rice, three-eighths of a measure of *<term>cholam</term>* <note>original: "cholam"; a coarse grain or sorghum that is a staple food in the region</note>, and four <term>annas</term> <note>a former currency unit of India, equal to one-sixteenth of a rupee</note>—and then he carries the pot and bottle in procession and places them under the canopy near the image of the goddess <term>Peddamma</term>. Then comes yet another procession. The people go off to the house of the chief official, the <term>Reddy</term> <note>the village headman or a member of the local landowning caste</note>, and bring from it some cooked rice in a large earthenware pot, some sweet cakes, and a lamb. A large quantity of <term>margosa</term> <note>neem tree</note> leaves are spread on the ground in front of the image; the rice from the Reddy’s house is placed upon them in a heap, and a large heap of rice—from one hundred to three hundred measures, according to the amount of the subscriptions—is poured in a heap a little farther away.

All these elaborate proceedings form only the preparations for the great sacrifice, which is now about to begin. The lamb is first worshipped and then sacrificed by having its throat cut and its head cut off. A ram is next brought and stood over the first large heap of rice, and is there cut in two, through the back, with a heavy chopper, by one of the village <term>washermen</term> <note>in this ritual context, the washerman performs specific ceremonial duties including this sacrifice</note>. The blood pours out over the rice and soaks it through. One half of the ram is then taken up and carried to a spot a few yards off, where a body of <term>Asādis</term> <note>ritual singers and storytellers from the Mādigā community</note> are standing ready to begin their part in the ceremonies. The other half of the ram is left lying on the rice. The Asādis then begin to sing a long chant in honor of the deity. Meanwhile, the chief sacrifice is made. The buffalo is brought forward, and the <term>Mādigās</term> <note>a community traditionally tasked with ritual drumming and leatherwork</note> kill it by cutting its throat (in some villages its head is cut off). Some water is first poured over the blood, and then the pool of blood and water is covered up carefully with earth, lest any outsider from another

->BUFFALO SACRIFICE<-

...worship among the Hindus and called *<term>ritual red powder</term>* <note>original: "kunkuma"; a brilliant red powder made of turmeric mixed with lime, used to mark sacred objects or the foreheads of devotees</note>. The village officials pay him his fee—three-eighths of a measure of rice, three-eighths of a measure of <term>sorghum</term> <note>original: "cholam"; a hardy grain that is a staple food in South India</note>, and four <term>coins</term> <note>original: "annas"; a former currency unit of India, equal to one-sixteenth of a rupee</note>—and then he carries the pot and bottle in procession and places them under the canopy near the image of the goddess <term>Peddamma</term>. 

Then comes yet another procession. The people go off to the house of the chief official, the <term>village headman</term> <note>original: "Reddy"; the headman or a member of the local landowning caste</note>, and bring from it some cooked rice in a large earthenware pot, some sweet cakes, and a lamb. A large quantity of <term>neem</term> <note>original: "margosa"; leaves from the neem tree, which are considered sacred and have antiseptic properties</note> leaves are spread on the ground in front of the image; the rice from the headman's house is placed upon them in a heap, and a large heap of rice—from one hundred to three hundred measures, according to the amount of the donations—is poured in a heap a little farther away.

All these elaborate proceedings form only the preparations for the great sacrifice, which is now about to begin. The lamb is first worshipped and then sacrificed by having its throat cut and its head cut off. A ram is next ...

<meta>This page continues the description of the festival for the goddess Peddamma in the Telugu-speaking regions. # WORSHIP IN THE <term>TELUGU COUNTRY</term> <note>Referring to the regions of Southern India where the Telugu language is spoken, primarily modern-day Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.</note>
->51<-

[...any man from another] village should come and steal it. The idea is that if any man from another village should take away and carry home even a small part of the blood, that village would get the benefit of the sacrifice. The head of the buffalo is then cut off and placed before the image, with a layer of fat from its entrails smeared over the forehead and face, so as to cover entirely the eyes and nose. The right foreleg is cut off and placed crosswise in the mouth, some boiled rice is placed upon the fat on the forehead, and on it an earthenware lamp, which is kept alight during the whole of the festival. Why the right foreleg should be cut off and placed in the mouth, and what the meaning of it is, I have never been able to discover nor can I conjecture. When I have asked the villagers, they only reply, “It is the custom.” But I have found the custom prevailing in all parts of South India, among <term>Tamils</term> <note>The people and language of the modern state of Tamil Nadu.</note>, <term>Telugus</term>, and <term>Canarese</term> <note>An older term for the Kannada-speaking people of modern-day Karnataka.</note> alike, and I have been informed that exactly the same custom prevails in the Southern <term>Marāṭhā country</term> <note>The region in western-central India, primarily modern-day Maharashtra.</note>. It seems to be a very ancient part of the ritual of sacrifice prevailing in South India.¹ This completes the presentation of the sacrifice to the goddess, who is supposed to delight in the food offered, and especially in the blood. A great deal of the food offered is, as a matter of fact, taken—

---

¹ <term>Maharaja Sir V. S. Raṅga Rao Bahadur</term> <note>The Zamindar of Bobbili, a prominent noble and political figure in the Madras Presidency.</note>, <term>G.C.I.E., C.B.E.</term> <note>British colonial honors: Knight Grand Commander of the Order of the Indian Empire and Commander of the Order of the British Empire.</note>, writes in *The Asiatic Review* for January, 1919: “The Lord Bishop wishes to know why the leg of an animal is put crosswise in its mouth after it has been sacrificed before the village goddess. Among the menial castes of a village there is the practice of a guilty man putting a piece of dry grass crosswise in his mouth when he goes to the head of his village to ask his pardon. It denotes that he has committed a wrong act, as a beast. In places where grass is not available, the person in question puts the first finger of his right hand crosswise in his mouth with the same idea or purpose. Here the animals are sacrificed before the village gods and goddesses by the people in the expectation, or rather with the firm belief, that their sins will be forgiven by those deities, and that their consequences will be thus averted by means of those sacrifices. Instead of putting their fingers in their mouths, as stated before, they put the animal’s leg (generally the right leg) crosswise in its mouth. Though I am not sure that this is the explanation of this practice, I presume that it must be along these lines, as no other ground is traceable.”

It includes a scholarly contribution from the Maharaja of Bobbili explaining this act as a symbol of seeking pardon for sins.</summary>

carries the basket sprinkles the rice soaked in blood over
the houses to protect them from evil spirits. As he
walks along, he shouts out, at intervals, that he sees the
evil spirits, and falls down in a faint. Then lambs have
to be sacrificed on the spot and limes thrown into the
air and cocoanuts broken, to drive away the demons and
bring the man to his senses. And so the procession
moves through the village, amid frantic excitement,
till, as the day dawns, they return to the canopy, where
the great mother <note>The goddess Peddamma, who is being honored in this festival.</note> is peacefully reposing.

called. They are all covered over with a large cloth,

and eat the food entirely concealed from view, probably to prevent the evil spirits from seeing them, or the **evil eye** <note>A widespread belief across many cultures that a malevolent glare, often fueled by envy, can cause misfortune or injury to those being watched, especially while eating.</note> from striking them. And then some more food is served to the *<term>Asādis</term>* <note>A class of hereditary ritual singers and storytellers from the Dalit community who chant the history and praises of the goddess during festivals.</note>, who have been for many hours, during the ceremonies of the night, chanting the praises of the goddess. After this the villagers bring their offerings. The *<term>Brāhmans</term>* <note>The highest-ranking group in the traditional Hindu social hierarchy, who historically followed a strictly vegetarian diet and avoided the killing of animals.</note>, who may not kill animals, bring rice and cocoanuts, and other castes bring lambs, goats, sheep, fowls, and buffaloes, which are all killed by the washermen, by cutting their throats, except the buffaloes, which are always killed by the *<term>Mādigās</term>* <note>A South Indian social group traditionally involved in leatherwork and manual labor; they play a central role in village goddess rituals, particularly in the sacrifice of buffaloes.</note>, the lowest class of Outcastes. The heads are all cut off and presented to the goddess. This lasts till about three p.m., when the people go off to the house of the village carpenter, who has got ready a small wooden cart. On their arrival some cooked rice is offered to the cart, and a lamb sacrificed before it, and a new cloth and eight *<term>annas</term>* <note>A unit of currency used in colonial India; sixteen annas made up one rupee.</note> are given to the carpenter as his fee. The cart is then dragged by the washermen, to the sound of horns and tom-toms, to the place of sacrifice. The heads and carcasses of the animals already sacrificed are first removed by the *Mālās* and *Mādigās*, except the head of the buffalo first offered, which remains in its place till all the ceremonies are finished, when the shrine is removed.

At about seven p.m. another series of ceremonies begins. First a lamb is sacrificed before the goddess, and its blood mixed with some cooked rice, and at the same time a pig is buried up to the neck in a pit at the entrance of the village, with its head projecting above the earth. The villagers go in procession to the spot, while one of the *Mādigās* carries the rice, soaked in the blood of the lamb, in a basket. All the cattle of the village are then brought to the place and driven over the head of the unhappy pig,¹ which is, of course, trampled to death; and, as they pass over the pig, the blood and rice are sprinkled upon them to preserve them from disease. Then, after this, follows the final ceremony.

<meta>This page continues the account of a major village festival in the Telugu-speaking region, moving from the sacrificial rites to the final procession intended to remove the deity's potential anger from the village. It then shifts to a specific case study of worship in Gudivada.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA
->54<-

The image of the goddess is taken from the canopy by the washerman, and a *<term>Mādigā</term>* <note>A member of a South Indian community traditionally tasked with leatherwork and essential ritual roles in village festivals, particularly the handling of sacrificial buffaloes.</note> takes the head of the buffalo with its foreleg in the mouth, the forehead and nostrils all smeared over with fat, and the earthen lamp still lighted on the top. They then all go in procession to the boundary of the village, first the men carrying the buffalo’s head, next the washerman with the image, and last the small wooden cart. When the procession arrives at the extreme limit of the village lands, they go on, for about a furlong, into the lands of the neighbouring village. There the *<term>Asādis</term>* <note>Hereditary ritual singers and storytellers who chant the history and praises of the goddess.</note> first chant the praises of the goddess, then some turmeric is distributed to all the people, and finally the image is divested of all its ornaments and solemnly placed upon the ground and left there. The light on the head of the buffalo is extinguished, and the head itself carried off by the *Mādigā*, who takes it for a feast to his own house. The object of transporting the goddess to the lands of the next village is to transfer to that village the wrath of the deity, a precaution which does not show much faith in the temper of the goddess, nor much charity towards their neighbours!

*Gudivada, near Masulipatam.* A somewhat different form of ceremonial prevails in some of the villages of the **Telugu** <note>A major Dravidian language and the ethnic group residing primarily in the modern-day state of Andhra Pradesh.</note> country nearer the coast. The village of **Gudivada**, about twenty miles from the important town of **Masulipatam**, may be taken as a good specimen of a well-to-do village in a prosperous district, and the ceremonies prevailing there are a fair sample of the cult of the village deities in these parts.

The name of the village deity at **Gudivada** is **Pallalamma**. Her image is the figure of a woman with four arms, and a leopard’s head under her right foot, carved in bas-relief on a flat stone about three feet high, standing in an open compound, surrounded by a low stone wall. The *<term>pūjārī</term>* <note>A priest who performs the rites of worship (puja) and maintains the shrine.</note>, who is a *<term>Śūdra</term>* <note>A broad social classification within the traditional Hindu hierarchy, typically associated with agriculture and various trades; in village worship, they often serve as priests instead of Brahmans.</note>, gave me a full account of the rites and ceremonies. Weekly offerings are made every Sunday, when the *pūjārī* washes the image with water and soap-nut seeds <note>The fruit of the Sapindus tree, containing natural saponins used as a traditional cleanser for the body and for cleaning sacred icons.</note> early in the morning, and smears it with turmeric

...and **<term>kunkuma</term>** <note>A red powder, often made from turmeric and lime, used for ritual marking in Hindu worship.</note>, offers incense, breaks a coconut, and cooks and presents to the image about a **<term>seer</term>** <note>A traditional unit of weight in India, approximately equivalent to two pounds or one kilogram.</note> of rice, which he afterwards eats himself. The rice is provided daily by the villagers. Occasionally fowls and sheep are offered on the Sunday by villagers who have made vows in time of sickness or other misfortunes. When a sheep is sacrificed, it is first purified by washing. The animal is simply killed in front of the image by a **<term>Mādigā</term>** <note>A member of a community traditionally tasked with leatherwork and specific ritual duties, such as performing sacrifices.</note>, who cuts off its head with a large chopper. The blood is allowed to flow on the ground and nothing special is done with it. The head becomes the perquisite <note>A customary right or legal perquisite of a position.</note> of the **<term>pūjārī</term>** <note>A village priest who conducts the rites of worship.</note>, and the offerer takes away the carcass for a feast in his house. In many villages, both in the **Telugu** and **Tamil** districts, water is poured over the sheep’s back to see whether it shivers. If it shivers, it is a sign that the goddess has accepted it.<gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: "1 For this widespread superstition see Sir Alfred Lyall, Asiatic Studies, i, 19. Compare pages 63, 68, 69, 73, 99, below."</note> Where the people are economical, they keep on pouring water till it does shiver, to avoid the expense of providing a second victim, but, where they are more scrupulous, if it does not shiver, it is taken as a sign that the goddess will not accept it and it is taken away.

A public festival is held whenever an epidemic breaks out. The headman of the village then gets a new earthenware pot, besmears it with turmeric and kunkuma and puts inside some clay bracelets, some necklaces, and ear-rings, three pieces of charcoal, three pieces of turmeric, three pieces of incense, a piece of dried coconut, a woman’s cloth, and two **<term>annas’</term>** <note>A former currency unit; sixteen annas equaled one rupee.</note> worth of copper coins—a strange collection of miscellaneous charms and offerings. The pot is then hung up in a tree near the image, as a pledge that, if the epidemic disappears, the people will celebrate a festival.

When it does disappear, a thatched shed of **<term>palmyra</term>** <note>A type of fan palm tree common in the region, whose leaves are frequently used for thatching and writing material.</note> leaves is built near the image, and a special image of clay, adorned with turmeric and kunkuma, is put inside, and beneath it an earthen pot filled with buttermilk and boiled rice. This pot is also smeared with turmeric and...

<meta>This page continues the description of ritual worship in South Indian villages. While the previous page discussed individual offerings and the ritual of checking for a sheep's "shiver" to confirm the goddess's acceptance, this page details the communal festival rituals, including the procession of a sacred pot and the large-scale sacrifice of a buffalo to protect the entire village.</meta>

### THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA
->56<-

...**<term>kunkuma</term>** <note>A red ritual powder, usually made from turmeric and alum.</note>, adorned with **<term>margosa</term>** <note>The Neem tree (Azadirachta indica), considered sacred and used for its medicinal and protective properties in rituals.</note> leaves, covered with an earthenware saucer, and carried in procession through the village during the day, to the exhilarating sound of pipes, horns, and **<term>tom-toms</term>** <note>Traditional drums commonly used in village festivals and announcements.</note>, by the village potter, who takes the rice and buttermilk for his perquisite <note>A traditional payment or benefit given to a specific worker for their ritual service.</note> and renews it every morning of the festival at the public expense. The duration of the festival depends on the amount of the subscriptions, but it always lasts for an odd number of days, excluding all numbers with a seven in them, *e.g.* 7, 17, 27, etc. During the night the barbers of the village chant the praises of the goddess, and the **<term>Mādigās</term>** <note>A community traditionally tasked with leatherwork and specific ritual duties, including the performance of animal sacrifices.</note> beat tom-toms near the image.

On the night before the day appointed for the offering of animal sacrifices by the villagers, a male buffalo, called **<term>Devara Potu</term>** <note>original: "Devara Potu"; literally "God’s animal," a buffalo specifically consecrated for sacrifice.</note>, *i.e.* devoted to the deity, is sacrificed on behalf of the whole village. First, the buffalo is washed with water, smeared with yellow turmeric and red kunkuma, and then garlanded with flowers and the leaves of the sacred margosa tree. It is brought before the image; and a Mādigā cuts off its head, if possible at one blow, over a heap of boiled rice, which becomes soaked with the blood. The right foreleg is then cut off and placed crosswise in its mouth, according to the widespread custom prevailing in South India, the fat of the entrails is smeared over the eyes and forehead, and the head is placed in front of the image. A lighted lamp is placed, not as in the other villages on the head itself, but on the heap of rice soaked with blood. This rice is then put into a basket; and a Mādigā, the village ***vetti*** <note>A village servant or sweeper who performs various communal tasks.</note> or sweeper, carries it round the site of the village, sprinkling it on the ground as he goes. The whole village goes with him, but there is no music or tom-toms. The people shout out as they go “**<term>Poli! Poli!</term>**” <note>original: "Poli! Poli!"; meaning "Food! Food!" or "Bounty! Bounty!"—a ritual cry to ensure prosperity and appease spirits.</note> *i.e.* “**Food! Food!**” and clap their hands and wave their sticks above their heads to keep off the evil spirits. The rice offered to the goddess, but not soaked with blood, is then distributed to the people. What spirits the rice soaked in blood is supposed to feed is not clear, but the object of sprinkling the blood is evidently to ward off evil spirits.

<summary>This page describes the communal ritual of buffalo sacrifice in South Indian village festivals, detailing the preparation of the victim and the process of sprinkling blood-soaked rice (Poli) around the village boundaries to ward off evil spirits.</summary>
<keywords>South India, buffalo sacrifice, Devara Potu, Mādigā, margosa, kunkuma, Poli ritual, evil spirits, village festivals, ritual slaughter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of a multi-day village festival in the Telugu-speaking region of South India. Having established the initial offerings and the consecration of the sacrificial buffalo on the previous page, the narrative now shifts to the communal offerings of rice and the large-scale sacrifices of sheep and buffalo intended to appease the goddess and protect the village from harm.</meta>

# WORSHIP IN THE TELUGU COUNTRY
->57<-

and prevent them from coming near the village, and apparently the present idea is that they will be satiated with rice and blood and not want to do any mischief. The original idea was possibly quite different; but this seems to be the intention of the ceremony in modern times.

On the next day, early in the morning, the clay image and the pot are washed and smeared afresh with **<term>turmeric</term>** <note>A bright yellow spice used in rituals for its purifying and auspicious properties.</note> and **<term>kunkuma</term>** <note>A red ritual powder, usually made from turmeric and alum.</note>. Incense and boiled rice are then offered as on other days, and the pot is taken in procession round the village. When this has been done, about midday, each householder brings his offering of boiled rice, cakes, fruits and flowers, and, in addition, the village as a whole contributes about two hundred or more **<term>seers</term>** <note>A traditional Indian unit of weight, roughly equivalent to two pounds or one kilogram.</note> of rice, which is boiled near the **<term>pandal</term>** <note>A temporary ritual pavilion or shed, often decorated with leaves and flowers.</note>. All these offerings are placed in a heap before the image. 

Then, first, a sheep or a buffalo is offered on behalf of the whole village. Having been duly washed, and smeared with turmeric and kunkuma, and decorated with **<term>margosa</term>** <note>The Neem tree (Azadirachta indica), considered sacred and used for its medicinal and protective properties in rituals.</note> leaves, its head is cut off by a **<term>Mādigā</term>** <note>A community traditionally tasked with leatherwork and specific ritual duties, including animal sacrifices.</note>. The blood is allowed to flow on the ground, and some loose earth is thrown upon it to cover it up. The head is offered to the image by the headman of the village. After this various householders, even **<term>Brāhmans</term>** <note>Members of the highest priestly caste in the traditional Hindu social hierarchy.</note> and **<term>Bunniahs</term>** <note>Also known as Banias, a community traditionally involved in trade and moneylending.</note>, bring animals for sacrifice. All are killed by a Mādigā, and then the heads are all presented and placed in a heap before the goddess. 

Sometimes an extraordinary number of animals is sacrificed on occasions of this kind, as many as a thousand sheep on a single day. In a village like Gudivada the number of victims is, of course, far less. The question of precedence in the offering of victims constantly gives rise to quarrels among the leading villagers. When I was once visiting Gudivada, there was a case pending before the **<term>tahsildar</term>** <note>A local administrative officer and tax collector.</note>, *that is*, the sub-divisional magistrate, between a **<term>zamindar</term>** <note>A significant landowner or local lord who collected land revenues.</note>, landowner, and a village **<term>munsiff</term>** <note>A village official or magistrate with local judicial authority.</note>, *that is*, a village magistrate, about this knotty point. The heads are taken away by the **<term>pūjārīs</term>** <note>Priests who perform the ritual worship and care for the deity.</note>, potters, washermen, barbers, **<term>Mālās</term>** <note>A community in South India with specific roles in village life and religious rituals.</note> and Mādigās, and others who take any official part in the sacrifice. <meta>This page continues the description of a multi-day village festival in the Telugu-speaking region of South India. Following the previous page’s focus on communal rice offerings and buffalo sacrifices, the text now details the distribution of the sacrificial remains and a specific, violent ritual involving a ritual cart and the impaling of animals.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA
->58<-

The remains of private sacrifices are taken away by those who offered them, while the remains of the public victim belong to the headman of the village. The rice, fruit, and other offerings are distributed among the various officials. The ceremony lasts from about ten in the morning until five in the afternoon.

In the evening, a cart is brought to the image with nine pointed stakes standing upright in it—two at each of the four corners and one in the center. On each stake, a young pig, a lamb, or a fowl is impaled alive. A **<term>Māla</term>** <note>A member of a South Indian community, often performing essential ritual and priestly roles in village festivals.</note>, called a **<term>Pambala</term>** <note>A hereditary priest, usually from the Māla community, who specializes in the rituals of village goddesses.</note> (*that is*, a hereditary priest), then sits in the cart dressed in female clothing, holding in his hand the clay image of the goddess which was made for the festival. The cart is dragged with ropes to the furthest boundary of the village lands, and both the cart and the ropes are left beyond that boundary. The Pambalas take away the animals, which all die during the procession, as their share of the offerings.

*Living animals impaled in many villages.* This cruel ceremony of impaling live animals is quite common in the eastern part of the **<term>Telugu</term>** <note>The primary language and people of the region now comprising Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.</note> country,¹ and I have come across it in many villages that I have visited. The Reverend F. N. Alexander, the veteran missionary of the **<term>C.M.S.</term>** <note>The Church Missionary Society, a British Anglican missionary organization.</note> who lived for over fifty years at **<term>Ellore</term>** <note>Now known as Eluru, a city in Andhra Pradesh.</note>, told me that he witnessed it in the town of Ellore during the first year he went there, and wrote a letter to the *Madras Mail* describing it. As a result of his letter, the practice was forbidden by the Government. So now at Ellore the animals are tied onto the stakes without being impaled; but in many villages near Ellore, the custom still survives of impaling the unfortunate animals alive. Sometimes there are only four stakes on the cart, sometimes five, and sometimes more. It is not often that there are as many as nine. In one of the villages of the **<term>Kurnool district</term>** <note>A district in the southern part of the Telugu-speaking region.</note>, I found that a similar barbarity was practiced in connection with the **<term>hook-swinging ceremony</term>** <note>A traditional ritual where devotees were suspended from a tall pole by hooks inserted into the muscles of their backs as an act of devotion or penance.</note>. On the fifth day of the festival in honor of **<term>Ankalamma</term>** <note>A fierce village goddess popular in South India, often sought for protection against disease.</note>, a large car is constructed, with an arrangement of poles projecting—

...about 20 feet in the air. A sheep is then suspended from the pole by iron hooks fastened through the muscles of its back and a band round its middle, and swung round and round. Two or three of the older men in the village said that they had often seen men swing like this with iron hooks fastened into their backs,¹ and that it did not hurt. As soon as the sheep is swung up, buffaloes, sheep and goats are sacrificed, and the car <note>A ritual chariot or cart used to carry idols or sacrificial items.</note> is then dragged in procession through the village.

*A cruel pig sacrifice.* Sometimes, when there is cattle disease, a pig is buried up to its neck at the boundary of the village, a heap of boiled rice is deposited near the spot, and then all the cattle of the village are driven over the unhappy pig.² It is not the custom at Gudivada <note>A town in the Krishna district of Andhra Pradesh.</note> to sprinkle anything on the cattle as they pass over the poor animal, as is done elsewhere.

There is a remarkable parallel to this form of sacrifice in a description quoted by Mr. E. Thurston <note>Edgar Thurston (1855–1935), a British administrator and ethnographer famous for his study of the tribes and castes of Southern India.</note>, in his *Ethnographical Notes in Southern India*,³ of an ancient custom among the **<term>Lambadis</term>** <note>Also known as Banjara, a historically nomadic community found across India.</note>, a wandering tribe of South India:

> "In former times, the Lambadis, before setting out on a journey, used to procure a little child and bury it in the ground up to its shoulders, and then drive their loaded bullocks over the unfortunate victim. In proportion to the bullocks thoroughly trampling the child to death, so their belief in a successful journey increased."

It is possible that this custom of driving the cattle over the head of a buried pig may be connected with the worship of an agricultural goddess, since in ancient Greece the pig was sacred to agricultural deities, <note>original: "e.g."</note> for example **<term>Aphrodite</term>** <note>Greek goddess of love and fertility.</note>, **<term>Adonis</term>** <note>A figure in Greek mythology associated with vegetation and rebirth.</note>, and **<term>Demeter</term>** <note>Greek goddess of the harvest and agriculture.</note>; but it may also be a survival of some former custom of infanticide or human sacrifice such as prevailed among the Lambadis.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual sacrifices in South India, specifically moving from the description of Lambadi customs to the general ceremonies performed when founding a new village and the underlying logic of animal substitution for human sacrifice.</meta>

### THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

...fathers of the ceremonies observed when founding a new village. An auspicious site is selected and an auspicious day, and then in the centre of the site is dug a large hole, in which are placed different kinds of grain, small pieces of the five metals, gold, silver, copper, iron, and lead, and a large stone, called **<term>boddu-rayee</term>** <note>Telugu: 'navel-stone', a ritual stone representing the center of a new settlement.</note>, *that is* <note>original Latin: *i.e.*</note> navel-stone, standing about three and a half feet above the ground, very like the ordinary boundary stones seen in the fields. And then, at the entrance of the village, in the centre of the main street, where most of the cattle pass in and out on their way to and from the fields, they dig another hole and bury a pig alive. This ceremony would be quite consistent with either of the explanations suggested as to the origin of pig-burying. The pig may be buried at the entrance to the village as the emblem of fertility and strength, to secure the prosperity of the agricultural community, the fertility of the fields, and the health and fecundity of the cattle. Or it may equally be a substitute for an original human sacrifice. 

The idea that a new building or institution must be inaugurated by the sacrifice of a human life is very common all over India. To this day there is often a panic among the villagers who live near the banks of a river where a bridge is about to be built, because they think that one or more of their babies are sure to be required to bury under the foundations of the first pier. On one of my visits to Kalasapad <note>A village in the Cuddapah district, now part of the Prakasam district of Andhra Pradesh.</note>, in the Cuddapah district, the missionary told me that, when a new ward was opened for their local mission dispensary, no one would go into it, because the people imagined that the first to go in would be the needful sacrifice. Their fears were allayed by a religious service at the opening of the ward; but had it been a Hindu hospital, probably a goat or a sheep would have been killed as a substitute for the human victim.

The idea of substitution, too, is quite common in India. In the hook-swinging ceremony described above,¹

# WORSHIP IN THE TELUGU COUNTRY

It is common both in the Telugu and Tamil districts to substitute a sheep for a man, and to fasten the iron hooks in the muscles of its back <note>This refers to "hook-swinging" rituals, where a devotee was originally suspended by hooks through their skin as an act of devotion or to ensure village prosperity.</note>.

## *Alleged infanticide among Todas.* 

I have been told that, among the Todas of the Nilgiri Hills <note>A mountain range in Tamil Nadu, home to the indigenous Toda people, known for their distinct culture and buffalo-centered economy.</note>, it was formerly the custom to place female children, whom it was not desired to rear, on the ground at the entrance of the mund, <note>original Latin: *i.e.*</note> that is, a group of huts, and drive buffaloes over them. If they survived this ordeal, they were allowed to live.

It is only fair to add that the Todas themselves deny that this custom ever existed. To quote Mr. Thurston <note>Edgar Thurston (1855–1935), a prominent British administrator and ethnographer who documented the cultures of Southern India.</note> again:¹

> “The practice of infanticide, as it prevailed among the Todas of the Nilgiris, is best summed up in the words of an aged Toda during an interview with Colonel Marshall (*A Phrenologist amongst the Todas*, 1873): ‘I was a little boy when Mr. Sullivan (the first English pioneer of the Nilgiris) visited these mountains. In those days it was the custom to kill children, but the practice has long died out, and now one never hears of it. I don’t know whether it was wrong or not to kill them, but we were very poor, and could not support our children. Now every one has a mantle (<term>putkūli</term> <note>A traditional, heavily embroidered cotton shawl unique to the Toda people.</note>), but formerly there was only one for the whole family. We did not kill them to please any god, but because it was our custom. The mother never nursed the child, and the parents did not kill it. Do you think we could kill it ourselves? Those tell lies who say we laid it before the open buffalo-pen so that it might be run over and killed by the animals. We never did such things, and it is all nonsense that we drowned it in buffalo’s milk. Boys were never killed—only girls; not those who were sickly and deformed—that would be a sin; but, when we had one girl, or in some families two girls, those that followed were killed. An old woman (<term>kelachi</term> <note>A Toda term for an elderly woman or midwife.</note>) used to take the child immediately it was born, and close its nostrils, ears and mouth with a cloth thus (here pantomimic action). It would shortly droop its head, and go to sleep. We then buried it in the ground. The <term>kelachi</term> got a present of four annas (4d.) <note>Four annas was a small denomination of currency in British India, equivalent to about four pence at the time.</note> for the deed.’ The old man’s remark about the cattle-pen refers to the Malagasy <note>Pertaining to the people of Madagascar.</note> custom of placing a new-born child at the entrance of a cattle-pen, and then driving the cattle over it, to see whether they would trample on it or not.”

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

...during an epidemic, a washerman carries the earthenware pot, half full of buttermilk and adorned with neem leaves <note>original: "margosa leaves." The neem tree is sacred in South India and associated with healing and the goddess Mariamman.</note>, round the village to the sound of local drums <note>original: "tom-toms."</note>. As it goes round, the washerman stops at each house and the wife comes out and pours water beside the pot on the ground and does reverence to the pot, imploring the goddess not to let any evil spirit come to the house; and then she puts more rice and buttermilk into it. When it is full, it is taken back to the shrine and another brought in its place. As this procession continues for fifteen days, the accumulation of rice and buttermilk must be considerable. It is ultimately consumed by the washermen, potters, *Mālās*, and *Mādigās* <note>The Mālās and Mādigās are Dalit communities who historically held specific, essential roles in village administration and religious rituals.</note>, who take part in the festival. 

The real sacrifice begins on the sixteenth day and lasts for a month. Cotton-thread and all the rice and buttermilk collected from the villagers are offered to the image. The images themselves are smeared with turmeric, and dots of vermilion <note>original: "kunkuma," a bright red powder used for social and religious markings.</note> are put on them, and finally on the last day a male buffalo, called *Devara-Potu*, <note>original Latin: *i.e.*</note> that is, "one devoted to the goddess," is brought before the image and its head cut off by the head *Mādigā* of the town. 

The blood is caught in a vessel and sprinkled over some boiled rice, and then the head, with the right foreleg in the mouth, is placed before the shrine on a flat wicker basket, with the rice and blood on another basket just below it. A lighted lamp is placed on the head, and then another *Mādigā* carries it on his own head round the village, with a new cloth dipped in the blood of the victim tied round his neck. This is regarded here and elsewhere as a very inauspicious and dangerous office; and the headman of the village has to offer considerable inducements to persuade a *Mādigā* to undertake it. Ropes are tied round his body and arms and held fast by men walking behind him, as he goes round, to prevent his being carried off by evil spirits, and limes are cut in half and thrown into the air, so that the demons may catch at them instead of at the man. It is believed that gigantic demons sit on the tops of tall trees ready to swoop down and carry him away, in...

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of village rituals in South India, specifically focusing on the Telugu-speaking regions. ...order to get the rice and the buffalo's head. The idea of carrying the head and rice around a village, so the people said, is to draw a kind of protective boundary <note>original: "cordon."</note> on every side of it and prevent the entrance of the evil spirits. Should any one in the town refuse to contribute <note>original: "subscribe."</note> to the festival, his house is passed over by the procession and left to the mercy of the devils. This procession is called *bali haranam*¹; and in this district, *inams*—rent-free lands—are held from the Government by certain families of the Mādigā community <note>The Mādigās are a Dalit community who historically held specific, essential roles in village administration and religious rituals.</note> for performing it. Besides the buffalo, large numbers of sheep, goats, and fowls are sacrificed, with each householder giving at least one animal. The head Mādigā who kills the animals takes the carcass and distributes the meat among the members of his family. Often, legal cases come before the courts to decide who has the right to perform the slaughter. As the sacrifice cannot wait for the slow processes of the law, the village elders settle the matter immediately, while an appeal to the courts remains pending. However, in the town of Masulipatam <note>Modern-day Machilipatnam.</note>, a Mādigā is specifically licensed by the Municipality for this purpose, and all disputes are avoided.

### *Cocanada*
<note>Modern-day Kakinada.</note>

At Cocanada there is only one Village Goddess <note>original: "Grāma-Devatā."</note>, Nukalamma (the name comes from *Nuku*, a Tamil word meaning "to beat"); but she is very ill-tempered, they told me, and causes much trouble. Curiously enough, the current priestess <note>original: "pūjārī."</note> is a woman of the fisherman caste. The office was hereditary in her family and she is the only surviving member. A male relative acts as the deputy priest. Offerings are made to Nukalamma every day, undoubtedly because of her temper. One custom I found observed here is common in these regions: when a victim's head has been cut off, it is placed before the shrine and water is poured on it. The person making the offering then waits to see if the mouth opens. If it does, it is seen as a sign that the sacrifice is accepted.² Another ceremony observed here is significant and, undoubtedly, a remnant of the primitive idea—

The annual festival of this goddess lasts for a whole month, ending on the New Year’s day of the Telugu calendar <note>This holiday, known as Ugadi, typically falls in March or April.</note>. During this festival, the procession of pots is observed with special ceremony. Six brass pots, each about two feet high, featuring the figure of a cobra springing from below the neck and rising over the mouth of the pot, are draped in women’s clothing <note>Saris or similar traditional cloths.</note> and carried around the town on the heads of men. Nothing is placed inside them, but as they make their rounds, the women of each house come out, pour water on the feet of the bearers, and make offerings of rice and fruit. These are solemnly presented to the pots by the bearers, and a colored powder is applied to the two small feet that project at the base of each pot, which form a sort of frame fitting on the bearer’s head. The bearer then takes a little of the turmeric powder—which is already on the foot of the pot—and places it into the dish in which the offering was brought, along with a few <term>neem <note>original: "margosa," a tree known for its medicinal and sacred properties in India.</note></term> leaves from a bundle that he carries with him. The dish is then returned to the woman who offered the gifts, while the gifts themselves become the property of the <term>priest <note>original: "pūjārī"</note></term>. The women and children of the family mark their foreheads with the turmeric and place the neem leaves in their hair. This is called *The Mother's Grace-Gift* <note>original: "Amma-vari-Prasādam"</note>.¹ As the procession moves along, the priests dance to the rhythmic sound of tom-toms <note>Traditional hand drums.</note>.

On the last day of the festival, when a buffalo is sacrificed, a curious ceremony takes place which is said to be very common in the villages of this district. The doors of the shrine are kept shut for three days. On the fourth day, the doors are opened, and the thickened mass of blood, grain, and pulses <note>original: "gram," a term for various legumes used in India.</note> is carefully washed. The grain and pulses are then separated on the ground behind the shrine to see which of the various kinds of grain has sprouted. All the <term>peasant farmers <note>original: "ryots"</note></term> eagerly gather to watch the result. Whichever grain is found to have sprouted is regarded as being marked out by the goddess as the right kind of grain to sow that year. This method of determining which crop to sow is common in both the Godavari and Masulipatam districts.

In these sacrifices to the goddess Nukalamma, the application of the blood is particularly significant. As soon as the animal is killed, a small quantity of the blood is smeared on the sides of the door-posts of the shrine. The assistant <term>priest <note>original: "pūjārī"</note></term> dips his finger in the blood and applies it to his own forehead; then all the other people present do the same. Afterward, some boiled rice and turmeric powder are mixed with the blood, and a little of this mixture is sprinkled on the head of the <term>Madiga <note>A member of a local community who traditionally performed essential ritual tasks, such as drumming or handling sacrificial elements.</note></term> who holds the basin to catch the blood.

When an epidemic of cholera breaks out, another goddess, called Maridiamma, is installed in the place of Nukalamma. A log of <term>neem <note>original: "margosa," a tree highly valued in India for its medicinal properties and its sacred association with village goddesses.</note></term> wood, about three feet high and six inches thick, is cut and roughly carved at the top into the shape of a head. This is then fixed in the ground with a <term>canopy <note>original: "pandal," a temporary shelter or pavilion made of leaves and cloth.</note></term> over it. 

Then, a procession featuring an earthen pot half-filled with buttermilk and rice is conducted every day until the epidemic subsides, in much the same way as is done in the town of Masulipatam <note>Referring to a description on page 62.</note>. After that, some ten or twelve small carts are made, about six feet square, with three pointed stakes standing up on each side. Live animals are impaled on these stakes, a practice also seen in other parts of the Telugu country <note>Referring to a description on page 58.</note>. The carts are partly filled with boiled rice and curry prepared at the shrine, and the blood of the sacrificed victims is poured over the rice. I was told that live animals were only impaled if a cart did...

### *Ellore*
The number of victims slaughtered at some of these festivals is enormous. At *Ellore* <note>Modern Eluru, a city in Andhra Pradesh.</note>, which is a town of considerable size and importance, I was told that at the annual festival of **Mahālakshmī** about a thousand animals are killed in one day, rich people sending as many as twenty or thirty. The blood then flows down into the fields behind the place of sacrifice in a regular flood, and carts full of sand are brought to cover up what remains on the spot. The heads are piled up in a heap about fifteen feet high in front of the shrine, and a large earthen basin about one-and-a-half feet in diameter is then filled with <term>sesame oil <note>original: "gingelly oil," a term commonly used in India for oil derived from sesame seeds.</note></term> and put on the top of the heap, a thick cotton wick being placed in the basin and lighted. The animals are all worshipped with the usual **respectful salutation** <note>original: *namaskāram*</note>, that is, folded hands raised to the forehead, before they are killed. This slaughter of victims goes on all day.

At midnight about twenty or twenty-five buffaloes are sacrificed. Their heads are cut off by a <term>Madiga <note>A member of a local community who traditionally performed essential ritual tasks, such as leatherwork or handling sacrificial elements.</note></term> **priest** <note>original: *pūjārī*</note> and with their carcasses are thrown upon large heaps of rice which have been presented to the goddess, till the rice is soaked with blood.

The subsequent ceremonies illustrate again the varieties of local custom. The rice is collected in about ten or fifteen large baskets, and, instead of being carried by a **Madiga**, is carried on a large cart drawn by buffaloes or bullocks, with the **Madiga priest** seated on it. As the cart moves along, the **Madigas** sprinkle the rice on the streets and on the walls of the houses shouting “Bounty! Bounty!” <note>original: "Poli! Poli!"</note>. A large body of men of different castes, including **Sudras**, **Kommas**, and **Outcastes**, go with the procession: but only the **Madigas** and **Malas** (the two sections of the Outcastes) shout “Bounty,” the rest following in silence. They have only two or three torches to show them the way, and no **drums** <note>original: "tom-toms"</note> nor music. Apparently the idea is that, if they make a noise or display a blaze of light, they will attract the evil spirits,

<summary>This page details the large-scale sacrificial rituals for the goddess Mahalakshmi in Ellore, where thousands of animals are killed and blood-soaked rice is distributed through the streets in a silent midnight procession.</summary>
<keywords>Mahalakshmi, Ellore, animal sacrifice, Madiga, Mala, Poli, ritual procession, village deities, South India</keywords>
