## WORSHIP IN THE TELUGU COUNTRY

who will swoop down on them and do them some injury; though in other villages it is supposed that a great deal of noise and flourishing of sticks will keep the evil spirits at bay. Before this procession starts, the heads of the buffaloes are placed in front of the shrine, with the right foreleg in the mouth, the fat from the entrails smeared about half an inch thick over the whole face, and a large earthen lamp on the top of each head. The Pambalas<gloss>1</gloss> <note>A community of hereditary musicians and priests who specialize in reciting the legends of village goddesses.</note> play **tom-toms** <note>Traditional drums used in South Asian religious and festive contexts.</note> and chant a long story about **Gaṅgamma** <note>A fierce and powerful village goddess (Gramadevata) worshipped across Andhra Pradesh.</note> till daybreak. About eight a.m. they put the buffalo heads with the lighted lamps upon them into separate baskets; and these are carried in procession through the town to the sound of tom-toms. All castes follow, shouting and singing. In former times, I was told, there was a good deal of fighting and disturbance during this procession, but now the police maintain order. When the procession arrives at the municipal limits, the heads are thrown over the boundary, and left there. The people then all bathe in the canal and return home.

On the last day of the festival, which, I may remark, lasts for about three months, a small cart is made of **margosa wood** <note>Wood from the Neem tree (*Azadirachta indica*), which is considered sacred to the goddess and possesses medicinal properties.</note> and a stake is fixed at each of the four corners. A pig and a fowl are tied to each stake, while a fruit, called *dubakaya* <note>A type of large citrus fruit, such as a citron or pomelo.</note>, is impaled on it instead of the animal. A yellow cloth, sprinkled with the blood of the buffaloes, is tied round the sides of the cart, and some margosa leaves are tied round the cloth. A Pambala sits on the cart, to which are fastened two large ropes, each about 200 yards long. Then men of all castes, without distinction, lay hold of the ropes and drag the cart round the town to the sound of tom-toms and music. Finally it is brought outside the municipal limits and left there, the **Outcastes** <note>A historical term for the Dalit community, who traditionally performed essential ritual tasks and were entitled to the remains of the offerings.</note> taking away the animals and fruits.

Sometimes, I was told, animals are sacrificed to **Gaṅgamma** by the people in **Ellore** in the courtyards of their own houses. They then clean the wall of

<image-desc>A religious diagram consisting of two distinct marks. On the left is a representation of three stacked horizontal lines with a single dot centered above and another centered below. On the right is a crescent or semi-circle opening upwards, with a single dot centered within the curve.</image-desc>

The symbol on the right represents the sun and moon; that on the left is the **sectarian mark of the followers of Shiva** <note>original: Śaivite sectarian mark; also known as the Tripundra, representing the three-fold nature of the divine.</note>. They sacrifice sheep, goats, and fowls to these symbols. It is curious that, in these private sacrifices at home, they pour water on the sheep and goats to see whether they shiver, which is taken as a sign of the deity's acceptance,¹ though this is not done in the public sacrifices at **Eluru** <note>original: Ellore.</note>.

*Dharmaja-Gudem, near Eluru.* At a village called Dharmaja-Gudem, about sixteen miles from Eluru, while the main features of the festivals are the same as those found elsewhere, there are two or three peculiarities which deserve notice. The ordinary **village deities** <note>original: grāma-devatās.</note> of the village are **Ellaramma**, **Gangamma**, **Mutyalamma**, and **Ravelamma**, who are represented by four stone pillars about six feet high, with figures of women carved on them, standing in an open field on the outskirts of the village; but when an epidemic breaks out, Mutyalamma, Gangamma, **Ankamma**, and **Mahalakshmiamma** are the deities propitiated, and special images are made of them. Those of the first three are made of clay, but that of Mahalakshmiamma is made of turmeric kneaded into a paste. Then, again, it is noticeable that a **Brahmin** <note>The highest priestly caste.</note> acts as the **priest** <note>original: pūjārī.</note> of Mahalakshmi, a washerman as the priest of Gangamma, and a potter as the priest of Ankamma. The Brahmin priest presides over the worship for the greater part of the festival, which lasts for about three months, and during that time the people come almost every day and offer flowers, fruits, coconuts, camphor, and incense, but no animal sacrifices. All this time, too, some **dancing girls**— <note>original: nautch; referring to professional dancers who performed at religious and social events.</note>

girls come and dance in a booth erected in front of the image and work themselves up into a state of frenzy, during which they are supposed to be inspired by the deities, and utter oracles to the worshippers. When the epidemic begins to abate, the <term>Brahman priest <note>original: Brāhman pūjārī</note></term> closes his part of the proceedings and departs.

Then, on that afternoon and evening, animal sacrifices are offered under the booth. On the first animal killed, which is generally a goat, water is poured from a brass vessel, to see if it shivers.<gloss>¹ See page 55.</gloss> If it does, it is taken as a good omen that the goddess is propitiated and the disease will disappear. Then other animals are brought and, in accordance with a very common division of functions in the Telugu country, a washerman kills the sheep, goats, and fowls, and a <term>Madiga <note>original: Mādigā; a member of a community traditionally responsible for leatherwork and specific ritual duties during village festivals.</note></term> the buffaloes. The heads of the sheep and goats, as well as of the buffaloes, have the right forelegs put crosswise in the mouths, the faces smeared with fat from the entrails, and a lighted lamp placed above them. The blood is caught in a basket full of boiled rice, and the rice and blood are sprinkled round the village, while a Madiga carries on his own head the head of a buffalo exactly as is done elsewhere. Here, too, great care is taken to prevent any person from another village taking away any of the rice and blood, lest the other village should get all the benefit of the sacrifice, and evils of all kinds descend on the unhappy villagers who have offered it. The ceremony of impaling live animals on stakes fixed round a wooden car,<gloss>² See p. 58.</gloss> and dragging them off to the boundary of the village is also practised here.

*Bhīmadole, near <term>Eluru <note>original: Ellore.</note></term>.* At another village, called Bhīmadole, about twenty miles from Eluru, I came across one of the few instances I have met with of any direct connexion between the harvest and the worship of a village goddess. There is an annual festival held there about harvest time, in November or December, lasting one day, which is always a Tuesday. About half a ton of rice is boiled in the middle of the village, taken

to the shrine and presented in a heap before the image, with a lighted lamp on the top of it, made of rice flour kneaded into a paste, and holding about one pint of oil.<sup>1</sup> Some <term>liquor <note>original: toddy; a fermented beverage made from the sap of palm trees.</note></term> is poured on the ground to the east of the rice by the washerman; incense and camphor are burnt; while the people make a <term>respectful salutation <note>original: namaskāram; a traditional gesture of folding the hands and bowing the head in reverence.</note></term> to the image. As many as two hundred sheep and goats are then killed, and fowls are brought by the poorer people. In this festival, the rice soaked in the blood of the victims is not sprinkled on the streets of the village nor over the houses, but each <term>farmer <note>original: ryot; a tenant farmer or small-scale landholder.</note></term> gives a handful of it to one of his field servants (an <term>Outcaste <note>A historical term referring to communities categorized outside the traditional four-tier caste system, today generally referred to as Dalits.</note></term>), who takes and sprinkles it over his master's fields. Three handfuls of the crop are cut on the same day to inaugurate the harvest. No buffaloes are sacrificed during this festival.

On the other hand, when an epidemic breaks out, there is a special festival, in which five or six buffaloes are sacrificed as well as about three hundred sheep and goats. The buffaloes are killed last of all. One special buffalo, called the **"great sacrifice"** <note>original: Pedda-Veta</note>, is reserved to the end, and killed at about ten p.m. Nothing special is done with the blood of the other buffaloes nor with that of the sheep and goats, but the blood of the **"great sacrifice"** is allowed to flow on to some of the rice, as soon as the head is severed, and both head and carcass are placed upon the rice heap. The head, as usual, has the right foreleg put in the mouth, with fat smeared over the face and a lighted lamp above it.

At about eleven p.m. the head is carried by a <term>Mala <note>original: Mālā; a Telugu-speaking community traditionally involved in weaving and agricultural labor.</note></term>, not by a <term>Madiga <note>original: Mādigā; a community traditionally responsible for leatherwork and playing ritual drums.</note></term> in this village, on his own head three times round the boundaries of the village site, and the rice soaked in blood is sprinkled by the <term>Malas</term> on the ground, as they go, and on any cattle they happen to meet, accompanied by the same weird and excited procession as elsewhere.

## CHAPTER V

### MODES OF WORSHIP IN THE KANNADA <note>original: "Canarese"</note> COUNTRY

The Kannada people are closely related in their physical and cultural traits <note>original: "ethnologically"</note> to the Telugu people, and we should naturally expect, therefore, to find a close connection between the ceremonies used by the two peoples in the worship of their village goddesses. A brief account of the ceremonies used in different parts of the Kannada country will show how far this is actually the case.

**Bellary District.** In the Bellary district, Durga <note>original: "Durgamma"</note>, Sunkalamma, and Uramma are very commonly worshipped. Uramma means simply the "village goddess," and is equivalent to the general term <term>village deity <note>original: "grāma-devatā"</note></term>. Her festival is not celebrated annually, but when there is a specially good crop, or when cholera or plague break out. The following account of it was given to me by an <term>Asadi <note>A member of a specific priestly group who performs songs and rituals during village goddess festivals.</note></term> of a village near Bellary, and may be taken as describing fairly the general type of such festivals and sacrifices throughout the district.

We will suppose that cholera has broken out in the village. The villagers then make vows to offer the sacrifice if the epidemic ceases. The day appointed for the festival is invariably a Tuesday, and on the previous Tuesday a basin-shaped earthen lamp, filled with oil and furnished with a stout cotton wick, is placed in the house of the <term>village magistrate <note>original: "Reddy"</note></term> and kept lighted until the festival and all the ceremonies are ended. The carpenter, also, prepares beforehand a wooden image of the goddess and a small cart, while

---

^1 Durga <note>original: "Durgā"</note> is one of the many names of Kali <note>original: "Kālī"</note>, the wife of Shiva <note>original: "Śiva"</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the specific religious rituals of the Kannada-speaking people in the Bellary district, detailing how festivals for village goddesses like Uramma are triggered by harvests or disease outbreaks.</summary>
<keywords>Kannada country, Bellary district, village goddesses, Uramma, Durga, ritual sacrifice, Asadi, village headman, cholera rituals</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of a festival for a village goddess in the Bellary district of the Kannada-speaking region. The previous page concluded with the placement of a sacred lamp in the house of the Reddy (the village magistrate or headman).</meta>

->THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA<-

A <term>pandal <note>A temporary booth or pavilion made of natural materials like leaves and cloth.</note></term> of leaves and cloths, with a raised platform inside and festoons of flowers hung in front, is made ready in an open space in the village. On the appointed Tuesday, a sheep or goat is first sacrificed at the carpenter’s house, and the carcass is given to the <term>taliaris <note>Village servants or watchmen, usually belonging to the Boya community.</note></term>.

The image <note>The physical representation of the goddess.</note> is then put on a cart about sunset and taken by the villagers in procession to the booth. In some villages, the washerman lays clean cloths on the ground so that the men who carry the image from the cart to the booth may not tread on the bare earth. Then the people proceed to the house of the flower-seller, who is by caste a Gira and generally a <term>Lingāyat <note>A member of a specific religious sect that worships the god Śiva.</note></term> by religion, and bring from there a kind of cradle made of pith and flowers, together with a pot of <term>toddy <note>An alcoholic drink made from the sap of palm trees.</note></term>, a looking-glass, some limes, and other articles used in worship. The cradle and looking-glass are hung up in front of the booth, and the other things are placed in front of the image. A looking-glass, I was told, is considered very auspicious, and is used by all castes in various religious ceremonies. Next, the lighted lamp is brought in procession from the Reddy’s house and placed before the image by a man belonging to the Reddy’s family. Four measures of boiled rice are then poured in a heap before the image, while flowers, betel leaves, nuts, plantains <note>A type of firm banana.</note>, and coconuts are offered, and camphor and incense are burnt.

When the preliminaries have been duly performed, the buffalo—which, since the close of the last festival, has been dedicated to the goddess and reserved for sacrifice—is brought from the Outcaste quarters <note>Historically, the segregated residential area for Dalits (then referred to as 'Outcastes' or 'Untouchables') who lived on the outskirts of the main village.</note> to the booth in a solemn procession. The <term>Asādis <note>A specific group of hereditary priests and singers who perform during these festivals.</note></term>, some ten or twelve in number, dance before it and sing songs in honor of the goddess. The animal has been kept the whole day without food or water and is garlanded with flowers and smeared with turmeric and red <term>kunkuma <note>A red mineral powder used for social and religious markings.</note></term>. This—

---

¹ A South Indian Śivaite <note>Follower of the god Śiva.</note> sect, named Lingāyats because each wears a small <term>Linga <note>The symbolic representation of the god Śiva, often interpreted as a phallic emblem.</note></term> hung round his neck in a reliquary <note>A small container for a sacred object.</note>.

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the festival for the goddess Uramma in the Kannada-speaking regions. The previous page ended with the placement of a heap of boiled rice before the image of the goddess.</meta>

# WORSHIP IN THE CANARESE COUNTRY

The buffalo is called the *husband-buffalo* <note>original: *Gauda-Kona*</note>, and, according to the traditional story, represents the <term>out-caste <note>A historical term for groups outside the four main social classes of Hindu society, now often referred to as Dalits.</note></term> husband who pretended to be a <term>Brahman <note>A member of the highest social class in the traditional Hindu system, historically associated with priesthood and scholarship.</note></term> and married the Brahman girl, who is now worshipped as the goddess Uramma. A fresh buffalo is always dedicated immediately after the festival, lest the goddess should be left a widow. When it arrives at the <term>pandal <note>A temporary pavilion or booth made of natural materials like leaves and cloth.</note></term>, it is laid on its side upon the ground and its head is cut off by one of the <term>Madigas <note>A specific community traditionally involved in leatherwork and essential ritual tasks.</note></term> with the sacrificial chopper. Its neck is placed over a small pit, which has been dug to receive the blood, and the entrails are taken out and placed in the pit with the blood. The right leg is then cut off below the knee and put cross-wise in the mouth, some fat from the entrails is placed on the forehead and a small earthenware lamp, about as large as a man’s two hands, with a wick as thick as his thumb, is placed on the fat and kept there lighted, till the festival is over. Some of the blood and entrails are then mixed with some boiled rice and placed in a new basket, which a Madiga, stripped naked, places on his head and takes round the boundary of the village fields, accompanied by a washerman carrying a torch, and followed by a few of the villagers. He sprinkles the rice, blood, and entrails all round the boundary. The greatest care is taken to see that none of the blood from the pit in front of the pavilion, where the buffalo was killed, is taken away by any one from another village, as they believe that in that case all the benefits of the sacrifice would be transferred to the other village. In former days men who stealthily took away the blood were chased and murdered. As this cannot be done under <term>British rule <note>Referring to the period of British colonial governance in India, which imposed different legal standards on local practices.</note></term>, a strict patrol is kept all round the place where the blood lies, and no one from any other village is allowed to loiter near the spot.

Next day, Wednesday, about four p.m., villagers, who have made vows, bring sheep for sacrifice and offerings of boiled rice, fruits, coconuts, etc., with incense and camphor. I was told that fowls were not offered to Uramma. After the sheep has been killed, the head is cut off and water is poured on the nose; if the mouth opens, it is regarded as a good omen. The

<summary>This page details the central sacrifice of the "husband-buffalo" to the goddess Uramma, including the ritual distribution of blood along village boundaries to secure divine protection. It also notes the historical shift in how these rituals were defended following the imposition of British colonial law.</summary>

<keywords>Uramma, buffalo sacrifice, Madigas, village boundaries, Gauda-Kona, ritual omens, British Raj, South Indian folk religion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the festival for the goddess Uramma. It details the distribution of the sacrificial buffalo meat and the conclusion of the ritual, before transitioning to the worship of other village deities in the town of Bellary.</meta>

->THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA<-

carcasses are taken away by the offerers to their own homes as a feast for the family. The heads are all put together and distributed to those of the village artisans and officials who are meat-eaters.

On Thursday, about four p. m., the flesh of the buffalo, which was sacrificed on Tuesday evening and must be by this time rather high <note>In this context, "high" refers to meat that has begun to age or ferment, developing a strong, pungent odor.</note>, is cooked in front of the <term>pandal <note>A temporary ritual pavilion or booth made of greenery and cloth.</note></term>, and part of it is first offered to the goddess, with some boiled rice, on five separate leaves. The <term>Asādis <note>A group of ritual performers, usually from the Madiga community, who specialize in singing the stories of the goddesses.</note></term> make the offering with songs and dances, the breaking of coconuts, and burning of incense and camphor, and prostrations on the ground, *<term>shasthāngam <note>original: "shasthāngam"; a "full-body" prostration where eight parts of the body touch the floor, signifying total submission to the deity.</note></term>*. For this part of their service they receive twenty <term>pies <note>A historical unit of currency in British India; 192 pies made up one rupee. This was a very small amount, even then.</note></term> (about 1¾d.), four pies for each leaf, not an extravagant sum. Then they take the five leaves away and eat the flesh and rice at some distance from the pandal, where it was cooked. These offerings to the goddess must be eaten on the spot, and are not allowed to be taken home. The rest of the flesh is given to the <term>Outcastes <note>A historical term for groups outside the traditional four-tier social system, now often called Dalits.</note></term> and <term>taliāris <note>Village watchmen or local administrative assistants.</note></term>, who cook and eat some of it on the spot and take away the remainder. After sunset the goddess is put on the wooden cart and dragged in procession to the boundary of the village, an Asādi walking in the front and carrying on his head the head of the buffalo. When they come to the limit of the village lands, they leave the image on their own side of the boundary and there it stays. This ceremony ends the festival.

### Bellary Town

Somewhat similar festivals are held periodically to propitiate *Suṅkalamma*, the goddess of small-pox and measles, and *Maramma*, the goddess of cholera. In the town of Bellary there is a shrine of *Durgamma*¹ which consists only of an ant-hill, with a plain stone shrine about thirty feet long, six deep and eight or ten high built over it. The story goes that an old woman many years ago was worshipping an image of *Durgamma* on this spot, when the goddess appeared to her and said that she was *Durgamma* of Bellary, that

---

¹ See p. 71, n. 1, above.

<summary>This page describes the final stages of the Uramma festival, involving the ritual consumption of the buffalo sacrifice, and introduces the shrines of disease-averting goddesses in Bellary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of ritual practices in the Bellary (modern-day Ballari) district, moving from the general festival of Uramma to the specific origins and sacrificial customs of a shrine where the goddess is believed to reside in an ant-hill.</meta>

### WORSHIP IN THE KANNADA <note>original: "CANARESE"; referring to the region and language of Karnataka.</note> COUNTRY

she lived in the ant-hill, and ought to be worshipped there. The ant-hill grew in size in the course of years and a shrine was built. The present <term>priest <note>original: "pūjārī"; a person who performs the ritual acts of worship and tends to the deity.</note></term>, who is a <term>milkman <note>original: "Golla"; a member of a pastoral caste traditionally involved in cattle-herding.</note></term> by caste, says that in the time of his father, about forty years ago, a large snake lived in the ruined wall behind the shrine, and used to come out and eat eggs and milk placed for it before the shrine. Apparently it very rarely makes its appearance now.

There is an annual festival to this goddess in Bellary, when male buffaloes, sheep, goats, and fowls are offered in sacrifice. When a buffalo is sacrificed, the right leg is, as usual, cut off and placed in its mouth, and fat is smeared over its forehead, with a lighted lamp on the top. Then the offerer stands with folded hands in front of the goddess asking for a boon; and, if at that time the mouth of the buffalo opens, he thinks that his prayer has been granted; otherwise he goes away disappointed. The <term>local administrator <note>original: "tahsildar"; a government official in charge of a sub-district or "tehsil".</note></term> of Bellary conjectured that the practice of putting the right foreleg in the mouth was originally connected with this last ceremony, its object being to prevent <term>the stiffness of death <note>original: "rigor mortis"; the natural stiffening of a body's muscles after death.</note></term> setting in at once, and to keep the mouth open and the jaws twitching, so as to deceive the superstitious. But this does not seem to be a likely explanation of so widespread a custom. The skins of the buffaloes offered in sacrifice are used for the drums employed in worship, and the carcasses are given to the <term>Outcastes <note>A term used historically for Dalit communities living outside the traditional four-tier caste system.</note></term> and <term>village watchmen <note>original: "taliāris"; local officers responsible for guarding the village and assisting officials.</note></term> in the vicinity of the shrine. People who do not approve of the slaughter of animals cut off the right ear of a goat or sheep and, after carrying it round the temple, offer it to the priest. The blood of animals offered in sacrifice in Bellary is not sprinkled round either the shrine or the town. People who offer animal sacrifices also offer boiled rice with them. The rice is heaped on leaves in front of the shrine, <term>yellow turmeric <note>A bright yellow spice used for purification and as a symbol of auspiciousness.</note></term> and <term>red vermilion <note>original: "kunkuma"; a sacred red powder used for marking the forehead and ritual objects.</note></term> are sprinkled over it, and then it is distributed to the people present. Tuesdays and Fridays are regarded as specially suitable days for the worship of this deity and are observed as days of fasting by the priests of the shrine.

<meta>This page continues the description of regional religious practices in South India, moving from the Bellary district (discussed on the previous page) into the Mysore State and the city of Bangalore. It details specific physical penances, including the evolution of "hook-swinging" from a human ritual to a symbolic one, and the use of sacred foliage in worship.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

About February every year, the hook-swinging festival is celebrated in connection with the worship of <term>Durgamma <note>A regional village goddess in South India; her name combines "Durga" (a major Hindu deity) with "Amma," a respectful suffix meaning "mother" in several South Indian languages.</note></term>.¹ Originally, devotees swung from the top of a high pole by hooks fastened through the muscles of their backs as an act of extreme devotion or vow-fulfillment; but in these days, only an effigy <note>A model or representation of a person, used here to replace the human participant in the ritual.</note> is swung from the pole. It is quite common, however, for devotees to come to the shrine with silver pins fastened through their cheeks. These pins are about six inches long and rectangular in shape. They are thrust through both cheeks and then fastened, just like a safety-pin. The devotee comes to the temple with his cheeks pierced in this fashion, and with a lighted lamp in a brass dish on his head. On his arrival before the shrine, the lamp is placed on the ground, and the pin is removed and offered to the goddess. I was told that the object of this ceremony is to enable the devotee to come to the shrine with a concentrated mind!

It was also formerly the custom for women to come to the shrine clad only in twigs of the <term>margosa tree <note>Also known as the Neem tree (*Azadirachta indica*), it is considered sacred to village goddesses and is famous for its cooling, medicinal, and purifying properties.</note></term>, prostrate themselves <note>To lay stretched out on the ground, face downward, in a gesture of total submission and adoration.</note> before the goddess, and then resume their normal clothing. But this is now only done by children, with grown-up women putting the margosa branches over a cloth wrapped around their loins.

The ceremonies performed in the <term>Mysore State <note>A historical princely state in South India, which forms the bulk of the modern state of Karnataka.</note></term>, further south, do not materially differ from those already described, though they seem in some places to have been greatly influenced by sun-worship.

*Bangalore.* In Bangalore <note>The modern-day city of Bengaluru, the capital of Karnataka.</note> there is a shrine of <term>Maheśvaramma <note>A village goddess whose name signifies the "Great Goddess-Mother." The "sh" sound in the middle is denoted by the accent on the "s".</note></term>, at a village near the <term>Maharajah’s <note>The title for the "Great King" or ruler of the region.</note></term> palace. The popularity of the shrine seems to have declined in recent years, but daily offerings of fruit and flowers, camphor and incense are still made, and on Tuesdays and Fridays people sometimes bring fowls and sheep to offer to the goddess. When there has been illness in a house, or when, for some other reason, special vows have been made, women often come to the shrine with a silver safety-pin thrust through their cheeks, as is

---

¹ See page 59, note 1, above.

They offer fruit and flowers, prostrate themselves on the ground before the image, then take out the pin and present it to the goddess.

In front of the shrine, in an open space across the road, about fifteen yards off, stands a block of granite like a thick milestone rounded above, with a small hollow on the top, and a female figure without arms, representing <term>Doddamma <note>literally "Big Mother" or "Elder Mother" in Kannada.</note></term>, the sister and companion of <term>Maheśvaramma <note>A local manifestation of the Great Goddess (Mahadevi), often associated with protection and village welfare.</note></term>. Doddamma seems to be treated as a younger sister of the goddess, whom it is politic to propitiate, though with inferior honours.

An annual festival is held in this village after harvest. A special clay image is made by the goldsmith from the mud of the village tank and a canopy is erected in a spot where four lanes meet, and decorated with tinsel and flowers. The goldsmith takes the image from his house, and deposits it beneath the canopy. The festival lasts three days. On the first day the proceedings begin at about two p.m., the washerman acting as <term>pūjārī</term>. He is given about two <term>seers <note>a traditional Indian unit of measurement; one seer is approximately equivalent to one kilogram or one liter.</note></term> of rice, which he boils, and at about five p.m. brings and spreads before the image. Then he pours curds and turmeric over the image, probably to avert the evil eye, and prostrates himself. The villagers next bring rice, fruits, flowers, incense and camphor, and <term>āratī <note>a ritual involving the waving of light; here referring specifically to lamps made from rice-flour paste.</note></term>, *i.e.* small lamps made of rice-flour paste, each with oil in it and a lighted wick. These are very commonly used in the Canarese country. One <term>āratī</term> is waved by the head of each household before the clay image, another before the shrine of Maheśvaramma, another before a shrine of <term>Muneśvara <note>A deity often worshipped as a protector, saint, or guardian of the village boundaries.</note></term> about two furlongs off, and a fourth at home to his own household deity. During these ceremonies music is played, and tom-toms are sounded without ceasing. After this ceremony any <term>Śūdras <note>the laboring or artisan class within the traditional four-fold social structure of India.</note></term>, who have made vows, kill sheep and fowls in their own...

### WORSHIP IN THE CANARESE COUNTRY <note>Referring to the Kannada-speaking regions of southern India, primarily the modern-day state of Karnataka.</note>

<page-num>78</page-num>

...homes and then feast on them, while the women pierce their cheeks with silver pins, and go to worship at the shrine of Maheśvaramma <note>A manifestation of the village goddess, often seen as a protector and a form of the Great Mother.</note>. At about nine p.m. the Mādigās <note>original: "Mādigās"; a community historically tasked with leatherwork and specific village ritual roles.</note>, who are esteemed the left-hand section of the Outcastes <note>A reference to the historical "Right-hand" and "Left-hand" social divisions in South India.</note>, come and sacrifice a male buffalo, called the god's buffalo <note>original: "devara kona"</note>, *i.e.* consecrated buffalo, which has been bought by subscription and left to roam free about the village under the charge of the village watchman <note>original: "Toti"</note>. On the day of the sacrifice it is brought before the image, and the watchman cuts off its head with the sacrificial chopper. The right foreleg is also cut off and put crosswise in the mouth, and the head is then put before the image with an earthen lamp alight on the top of it. The blood is cleaned up by the sweepers at once, to allow the other villagers to approach the spot; but the head remains there facing the image till the festival is over. The Mādigās take away the carcass and hold a feast in their quarter of the village.

On the second day there are no public offerings, but each household makes a feast and feeds as many people as it can. On the third day there is, first, a procession of the image of Maheśvaramma, seated on her wooden horse, and that of Muneśvara <note>A male deity often worshipped as a guardian or an ancestral spirit.</note> from the neighbouring shrine, round the village. They stop at each house, and the people offer fruits and flowers but no animals.

At about five p.m. the washerman takes up the clay image of the <term>village deity <note>original: "grāma-devatā"</note></term>, goes with it in procession to the tank <note>A large, man-made reservoir or pond used for water storage and ritual purposes.</note>, accompanied by all the people, to the sound of pipes and tom-toms <note>Traditional drums.</note>, walks into the tank about knee-deep, and there deposits the image and leaves it.

**Kempapura Agrahāra.** <note>An "Agrahāra" was a village historically granted to the Brahmin community for their maintenance.</note> This is the common type of festival held in honour of the <term>village deity <note>original: "grāma-devatā"</note></term> in all the villages round about Bangalore <note>The major city of the region, now known as Bengaluru.</note>, whatever special deity may be worshipped, allowing, of course, for the variations of detail which are found everywhere. <meta>This page continues the description of the festival rituals following the buffalo sacrifice mentioned on page 78. <page-num>79</page-num>

The blood <note>Though the OCR begins with "is mixed," the context from the previous page indicates this refers to the blood of the sacrificed buffalo.</note> is mixed with some boiled rice in a large earthen pot, and taken at night round the village by the village watchman <term>Toti</term> <note>A village official or watchman responsible for various communal tasks.</note>, and sprinkled on the ground. The members of the leather-working community <term>Mādīgās</term> <note>A community historically tasked with specific ritual roles, including drumming and leatherwork.</note> go with him carrying torches and beating traditional drums <term>tom-toms</term>. The object of this ceremony is, as usual, to keep off evil spirits.

**Yelahanka.** The goddess Mother of Worship <term>Pūjamma</term> is especially the goddess of the <term>Mādīgās</term> in these parts, and the buffalo sacrifice forms an important part of the annual festival whenever she is worshipped. At a group of villages some ten miles from Bangalore, near Yelahanka, I found that she was represented by no image, but by a small earthen lamp, which is always kept lighted.

**Shrine near Bangalore.** At one shrine on the outskirts of Bangalore, where there are seven goddesses, namely <term>Anṇamma</term>, the presiding goddess, <term>Chaṇḍeśvaramma</term>, <term>Māyēśvaramma</term>, <term>Maramma</term> (the goddess of cholera), <term>Uḍalamma</term> (goddess of swollen necks), <term>Kokkalamma</term> (goddess of coughs), and <term>Sukhajamma</term> (goddess of small-pox and measles), the fire-walking ceremony forms an important part of the annual festival, which lasts for ten days. 

A trench is dug in front of the shrine about thirty feet long, five feet wide and one-and-a-half feet deep, and washed with a solution of cow-dung, to purify it. About thirty measures <term>seers</term> <note>A traditional unit of volume; one seer is roughly equivalent to one liter or two pounds.</note> of boiled rice are then brought on the fifth day of the festival, and offered to the goddess before the trench. It is all put into the trench and some ten measures <term>seers</term> of yogurt <term>curds</term> are poured over it and then distributed to the people, who eat some on the spot and some at home. 

A cart-load of firewood is then spread over the trench, set alight and left to burn for about three hours, till the wood becomes a mass of red-hot embers. When all is ready, the people assemble, and the priest <term>pūjārī</term>, whose turn it is to conduct the worship, first bathes to purify himself, and then, amid the deafening din of trumpets, drums <term>tom-toms</term>, and cymbals, and the clapping of hands, walks with bare feet slowly and deliberately over the glowing embers the whole length of the trench towards the shrine of the seven goddesses. After him about thirty or forty women walk over the red-hot embers.

with lighted <term>āratīs</term> <note>Sacred lamps or torches often carried in procession as an act of devotion.</note> on their heads. Such is the power of the goddess, the people told me, that no one is injured. The <term>pūjārī</term> <note>The officiating priest of the shrine.</note> of the shrine declared positively that the people put no oil nor anything else on their feet when they walk over.

*Mysore City.* At Mysore City, where the fire-walking ceremony is also performed, I asked three men who had walked over the trench why they were not hurt, and their reply was that people who were without sin were never hurt! I can only say that in this case their faces sadly belied their characters.

The following account of the worship of village deities in the City of Mysore, and the note on the worship of village deities in the <term>Canarese</term> <note>Referring to the Kannada-speaking region of Karnataka.</note> country generally, was kindly given to me by the late Mr. Ramakrishna Rao, then palace officer at Mysore:

The <term>Maris</term> <note>A group of mother goddesses often associated with both the protection from and the cause of diseases like cholera and smallpox.</note> of Mysore are said to be seven in number, and all the seven are sisters:
(1) <term>Bisal Mari</term> (the sun);
(2) <term>Goonal Mari</term>;
(3) <term>Kel Mari</term> (the earthen pot);
(4) <term>Yeeranagere Mari</term>;
(5) <term>Hiridevathi</term> (the eldest sister);
(6) <term>Chammandamma</term>;
(7) <term>Uttahnahaliamma</term>.

Of the seven <term>Maris</term>, <term>Hiridevathi</term> is said to be the eldest. Every year the <term>Mari Jatra</term> <note>The annual festival or fair dedicated to the goddess Mari.</note> is held, generally in the month of February. It lasts for about four weeks, and consists of the following:
(1) <term>Mari Saru</term> <note>Literally "Mari’s broth," a ritual involving the distribution of sacrificial food.</note>;
(2) <term>Mari Made</term> <note>A ritual food offering.</note>;
(3) <term>Mari Sidi</term> <note>A ritual involving a ceremonial pole, historically related to hook-swinging rites.</note>;
(4) <term>Kelammana Habba</term> <note>The festival of the Mother of the Pot.</note>;
each taking nearly a week's time.

After offering the rice to the Bisal Mari <note>The "Sun-Heat" goddess, a local form of the mother goddess Mariamma associated with protection and disease</note> they take it, with the carcass of the goat, to the south fort gate and westwards, going round the fort in the inner circle, dragging the carcass of the goat on the ground, and all the way sprinkling the red rice <note>Rice stained with the blood of the sacrifice, used for ritual purification</note> over the streets (this is said to purify the place lying inside the circle traced in their course), till they arrive at the point whence they started. They then convey the carcass and the remaining rice to a spot near the shrine of Madeśvara <note>A popular saint-deity of the region, often associated with the hills</note>, situated in the quarters where they live. Then the entrails of the goat are roasted and, with the rice, divided into three equal parts, and made into three balls, which are given away to the <term>Chakras</term> <note>original: "Chakras" - a historical term for a section of the Dalit or "Outcaste" community</note> for their services in tom-toming <note>Traditional drumming used to announce ceremonies and drive away spirits</note> during the rice-sprinkling ceremony.

(2) **Mari Made.** On Monday of the second week the <term>Toreyars</term> <note>A local caste traditionally associated with fishing and agriculture</note> throw away all their old earthen pots, used for cooking, and get their houses whitewashed. They get new pots, prepare <term>Kitchadi</term> <note>A ritual dish made of flour and buttermilk</note> in them, cover them with earthen lids and put <term>ārātīs</term> <note>Vessels containing ritual lamps or offerings of light</note> on them. At about six p.m. the <term>ārātīs</term> are carried by females to a consecrated pial (platform) known as the <term>Gaddige</term> <note>A sacred throne or altar</note>, and placed in front of a <term>Kunna Kannadi</term> <note>A small mirror used as a symbolic representation of the goddess</note>. Two sheep or goats are killed in sacrifice on the spot, and all the flesh is distributed amongst the families of Toreyars. This done, the <term>Kitchadi</term> pots are carried by females in procession to the Bisal Mari shrine, cloths about four feet wide being spread all along the way on which the procession walks, that they may not tread on the earth <note>A sign of extreme reverence and ritual purity, ensuring the participants remain untainted by the ground during the holy procession</note>. The <term>Kitchadi</term> in all the pots is offered to the Bisal Mari, and heaped up on a cloth in front of the Bisal Mari image. The females return home with the empty pots, which will henceforth be used for cooking in their families. The heap of <term>Kitchadi</term> then becomes the property of the washerman <term>Pūjārī</term> <note>A priest; here, a member of the washerman caste who performs priestly duties for the community</note>, who distributes it amongst his friends and relatives. (3) *Mari Sidi.* This occupies the third week of the <term>Jatra</term> <note>original: "Jatra" — a religious fair or festival</note>. On the Sunday before the Monday on which the <term>Sidi</term> takes place, the Human Sacrifice Ceremony called <term>Bali</term> <note>original: "Bali" — a Sanskrit word for "offering" or "oblation," often used for animal or symbolic sacrifice</note> is performed. It begins at midnight, and lasts till dawn. The man appointed for the <term>Bali</term> is made to lie down, a piece of cloth fully covering his body. This takes place on the same spot where the rice for the *Mari Saru* <note>original: "Mari Saru" — literally "Mari soup," the ritual mixture of rice and sacrificial blood described on page 81</note> (already explained) was prepared. A carpenter begins the ceremony by touching the man lying down with a cluster of flowers of the cocoanut tree. The <term>Chakras</term>¹ <note>Drummers from a specific local community</note> keep tom-toming, while the carpenter dances round the victim, singing songs. Fires are lit all round. The carpenter closes his dance by touching the victim again with his cluster of flowers about daybreak. The people present carry the victim (the <term>Bali</term> man) to the Mane Manchi shrine, where he takes rest and walks straight home.

On Monday the carpenter who performed the <term>Bali</term> ceremony the previous day gets the <term>Sidi Car</term> <note>A ceremonial cart equipped with a pivoting pole for the swinging ritual</note> fitted up. It is ready about five p.m. for the swing. The men to swing² on the <term>Sidi</term> are kept without food. They take a cold bath, dress themselves on the <term>pial</term> <note>original: "pial" — a raised stone platform or veranda at the front of a house</note> of <term>Gaddige</term> <note>original: "Gaddige" — a throne-like seat or the tomb of a saint; here referring to the shrine of Madeśvara mentioned on page 81</note> (mentioned in connexion with *Made*³) and then go to the palace, where they get a pre-

The buffaloes are smeared with turmeric (yellow powder) and <term>kunkuma</term> <note>A sacred red powder used in rituals, usually made from turmeric and alum.</note>, and are also garlanded with flowers and <term>margosa</term> <note>The neem tree, considered sacred and antiseptic in South Indian tradition.</note> leaves. They remain with the <term>Sidi</term>, but, before the men are allowed by the carpenter to swing on the <term>Sidi</term>, the carpenter tests his fittings, and offers the goat in sacrifice. Its blood is taken and sprinkled over all the joints of the car and the wheels of the <term>Sidi</term>. The goat sacrificed is given away to the coolies that work at the car. Then the <term>Sidi</term> procession begins. The two men who are to swing go with the buffaloes to the <term>Hiridevathi</term> <note>The "Great Goddess" or a primary local deity.</note> shrine, where another <term>Sidi</term> party from <term>Yeerangere</term> <note>A neighborhood or district in the northern part of the city.</note>, the northern part of the city, meets them with another <term>Sidi</term>, one buffalo, and one man to swing. One at a time mounts on each <term>Sidi</term>. After mounting, each lightly strikes the other as the <term>Sidis</term> cross. Then each swings suspended by a band round his waist on his <term>Sidi</term>. It is at this time that the buffaloes are all killed one after another. It is attempted to cut off the head of each victim with one blow, but actually more blows are used before the buffaloes' heads are severed. When this is over, the men on the <term>Sidis</term> get down and return to the <term>Hiridevathi</term> shrine. There they offer <term>pūjā</term> <note>The performance of devotional rites and offerings.</note>, after which the parties return home. The party from the Bisal Mari shrine go to the Mane Manchi shrine, take rest, dine, and spend the night there, offering prayers, etc. The following morning they walk home.

(4) *Kelammana Habba* <note>The festival (Habba) of the goddess Kelamma.</note>. The faces are smeared with fat, turmeric, and <term>kunkuma</term> <note>A sacred red powder used in rituals, usually made from turmeric and lime.</note>. The right foreleg of each animal is cut off and stuck into its mouth. The flesh, etc., of the buffaloes is cooked and eaten by the <term>Chakras</term> <note>A community traditionally associated with leatherworking.</note> as well as by their friends and relatives. For one week the heads are kept in the aforementioned sheds and worshipped every day. On the next Monday the <term>Chakras</term> and <term>Holeyars</term> <note>A prominent laboring community.</note>, called also the <term>Balagai</term> <note>Literally "Right-hand"; referring to one of the two historical supra-caste groupings in South India.</note> caste, carry the heads of the two buffaloes in grand procession to their quarters and eat them up, if they are not very putrid.

A legend is prevalent regarding this <term>Kel Mari</term> <note>Likely "Lower Mari" or "Younger Mari."</note>. <term>Hiridevathi</term> <note>The "Great Goddess" or the eldest of the Mari sisters.</note>, the eldest of the Mari sisters, is said to have ordered one of her younger sisters, <term>Kel Mari</term>, to bring fire. The latter went, and in her search for fire she found a lot of low-caste men cooking the flesh of a buffalo and eating the same. It was a curious sight for her to see them do so. She sat there observing what was going on, and lost time. As she was late, the eldest sister was very angry and excommunicated her with a curse, saying that she should only be worshipped by the lowest class of people. Hence the heads of the buffaloes are worshipped in the name of <term>Kel Mari</term>.

The following legend is believed by the common people. Once upon a time there lived a <term>Rishi</term> <note>A holy sage or forest-dwelling ascetic.</note> who had a fair daughter. A <term>Chaṇḍāla</term> <note>A historical term for an outcaste, often associated with ritually impure tasks like leatherwork or handling the dead.</note>, *i.e.* an Outcaste, desired to marry her. He went to <term>Kāśī</term> <note>The sacred city of Benares, modern Varanasi.</note> in the disguise of a <term>Brāhman</term> <note>A member of the highest priestly caste.</note>, where, under the tuition of a learned <term>Brāhman</term>, he became well versed in the <term>śāstras</term> <note>The sacred Sanskrit scriptures and treatises on law and religion.</note>, and learnt the <term>Brāhman</term> modes of life. On his return he passed himself off for a <term>Brāhman</term>, and after some time made offers to the <term>Rishi</term> lady, and somehow succeeded in prevailing upon her to marry him. She did so, her father also consenting to the match. They lived a married life for some time, and had children. One day it so happened that one of the children noticed the father stitch an old shoe previous to going out for a bath. This seemed curious, and the child drew the mother's attention to it. Then the mother, by virtue of her <term>tapas</term> <note>Spiritual power or heat generated through intense asceticism and devotion.</note>,

<summary>This page describes the ritual processing and consumption of sacrificed buffaloes by specific caste groups and recounts two foundational myths: one explaining the "lower" status of the goddess Kel Mari, and another concerning a high-caste woman unknowingly married to a disguised outcaste.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the ethnographic description of village rituals and mythologies in South India, specifically regarding the origin of the goddess Mari and the specific customs of animal sacrifice among different social classes.</meta>

->WORSHIP IN THE CANARESE COUNTRY<-

85

...came to know the base trick that had been played upon her by her husband, and cursed him and herself. The curse on herself was that she should be born a <term>Mari</term> <note>A common name for mother goddesses in South India, often associated with the protection of villages and the management of diseases like smallpox or cholera.</note>, to be worshipped only by low-caste men. The curse on him was that he should be born a buffalo, fit to be sacrificed to her, and that her children should be born as sheep and chickens. Therefore, during the periodical <term>Mari</term> festivals, buffaloes, sheep, and chickens are used as victims, and the right leg of the male buffalo is cut off and stuck in his mouth, in memory of his having stitched the shoes in his disguise as a <term>Brāhman</term> <note>The highest social class in traditional Hindu society, historically associated with the priesthood. The legend suggests the husband was a lower-caste man who pretended to be a Brahman to marry the goddess.</note>.

Animal sacrifices are generally offered by <term>Vaiśyas</term> <note>The third social class in the Hindu hierarchy, traditionally composed of merchants, traders, and landowners.</note> and <term>Śūdras</term> <note>The fourth social class, traditionally consisting of laborers and service providers.</note>, the victims being usually buffaloes, sheep or goats, and fowls. These sacrifices are usually propitiatory <note>Sacrifices intended to appease a deity or spirit to prevent their anger or to gain favor.</note>. Sometimes they are thank-offerings, but there is no sin-offering. When, owing to sickness, any one's life is despaired of, a vow to sacrifice the life of an animal on the recovery of the sick person is made and carried out by the convalescent as soon as possible after restoration to health. Should any misfortune happen to a personal enemy, an animal is at once sacrificed as a thank-offering!

In all these cases, the victim is taken before the altar, and there decapitated by a stroke of a sword, the blood being sprinkled on the object before which the sacrifice is offered, or on the ground in the vicinity. In no case is the blood ever sprinkled on the persons offering the sacrifice. Before a building is finished or occupied, the same kind of sacrifice is made, to propitiate the spirit supposed to have already entered there, and the blood of the victim is sprinkled over the materials of which the building is constructed.

Similarly, when a well is sunk, or a tank built, or a new tool or agricultural implement used, all of which from their nature might be the means of causing death, a sacrifice is offered to the evil spirit to prevent accidents, and, in the case of sharp-edged tools, blood is poured on that part which would cause the hurt. A partial sacrifice is made in the case of tools and implements which from their nature would not be likely to

cause death, and in these cases only a slight cut is made, usually in the nose or ear of the animal, sufficient to draw a few drops of blood, which are smeared on the tool, as already mentioned. In cases of epidemics, blood is poured over the image of the deity supposed to be responsible for the disease.

**Coorg.** The relic of human sacrifice described above, in Mr. Ramakrishna Row’s memorandum, would serve to show that in Mysore such sacrifices, at one time, formed a regular part of the worship of the village deities; and this is confirmed by the account given in the *Mysore and Coorg Manual* by Mr. Lewis Rice<sup>1</sup> of the worship of the <term>grāma-devatā</term> <note>Sanskrit for "village deity." These are local guardian spirits, usually female, who protect the community from disease and misfortune.</note> in Coorg, which is a hill country to the west of the Mysore State inhabited by a mixed population consisting of aboriginal tribes, a hundred and twenty thousand cultivators and artisans, who were formerly serfs but are now freemen, and a ruling class of **Kodagas** or **Coorgs**, who probably migrated into the country about the third century A.D. He writes:

> “The essential features of the religion of the Coorgs are anti-Brāhmanical <note>Practices that exist outside of or in opposition to the traditional priestly Hindu (Brahmanical) rituals, often involving local spirits and blood sacrifices.</note>, and consist of ancestral and demon-worship. As among other Dravidian mountain tribes, so also in Coorg, tradition relates that human sacrifices were offered in former times to secure the favour of their <term>grāma-devatās</term>, **Mariamma**, **Durgā**, and **Bhadra-Kālī**,<sup>2</sup> the tutelary goddesses <note>Protective or guardian goddesses.</note> of the <term>Śakti</term><sup>3</sup> <note>The "Śakti" line refers to the tradition focused on the worship of the supreme female divine power, often in her more fierce or potent forms.</note> line, who are supposed to protect the villages or <term>Nāds</term> <note>Traditional administrative districts or territorial units in the Coorg region.</note> from all evil influences. In Kirindadu and Koniucheri-Grāma in Katiyet Nād, once every three years, in December and June, a human sacrifice used to be brought to **Bhadra-Kālī**, and during the offering by the <term>panikas</term> <note>A specific class of religious mendicants or performers who act as mediums or priests during these rituals.</note>, the people exclaimed ‘*Al Amma!*’—‘A man, oh mother!’—but once a devotee shouted ‘*Al All Amma, Adu!*’—‘Not a man, oh mother! a goat’; and since that time a he-goat without blemish

has been sacrificed. Similarly in Bellur in Tavaligeri-Murnad of Kiggatnad <term>taluq</term> <note>An administrative district or sub-division.</note>, once a year, by turns from each house, a man was sacrificed by cutting off his head at the temple; but when the turn came to a certain home, the devoted victim made his escape into the jungle. The villagers, after an unsuccessful search, returned to the temple, and said to the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A temple priest or one who performs ritual offerings.</note> ‘Kalak Adu,’ which has a double meaning, *viz.* <note>original: "videlicet," meaning "namely."</note> *Kalak*, next year, *Adu*, we will give, or *Adu*, a goat, and thenceforth only scapegoats were offered. The devotees fast during the day. The he-goat is killed in the afternoon; the blood is sprinkled upon a stone, and the flesh eaten. At night the <term>Panikas</term> <note>A community of ritual performers often acting as mediums or demon-dancers.</note>, dressed in red and white striped cotton cloths, and their faces covered with metal or bark masks, perform their demoniacal dances. In Mercara taluq in Ippanivolavade, and in Kadikeri in Halerinad, the villagers sacrifice a <term>Kona</term> <note>A male buffalo.</note> or male buffalo instead of a man. Tied to a tree in a gloomy grove near the temple, the beast is killed by a <term>Mēda</term> <note>A member of a wandering community traditionally known for basket and mat making.</note>, who cuts off its head with a large knife, but no **Coorgs** <note>Also known as Kodavas, the dominant land-owning community of the region.</note> are present at the time. The blood is spilled on a stone under a tree, and the flesh eaten by the **Mēdas**. In connexion with this sacrifice there are peculiar dances performed by the **Coorgs** around the temple, the <term>kombata</term> or horn dance, each man wearing the horns of a spotted deer or stag on his head; the <term>pili-ata</term> or peacock’s feather dance, the performers being ornamented with peacock’s feathers, and the <term>chauri-ata</term> or yak-tail dance, during which the dancers, keeping time, swing yak-tails. These ornaments belong to the temple, where they are kept.

<meta>This page continues the description of ritual practices in the Coorg region, specifically moving from animal substitutions for human sacrifice to complex magical rituals involving confinement and village-wide offerings to appease the goddess of disease.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

<header>THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA</header>

of his fraternity, the <term>Panikas</term> <note>A community of ritual performers, mediums, and traditional healers in the Coorg region.</note> or <term>Bannus</term>, and they set to work. A pit is dug in the middle room of the house, or in the yard or the stable, or the field, as the occasion may require. Into this one of the magicians descends. He sits down in Hindu fashion muttering <term>mantrams</term> <note>Sacred ritual chants or formulas.</note>. Pieces of wood are laid across the pit, and covered with earth a foot or two deep. Upon this platform a fire of jack wood <note>Wood from the jackfruit tree, commonly used in sacred fires.</note> is kindled, into which butter, sugar, different kinds of grain, etc., are thrown. This sacrifice continues all night, the <term>Panika</term> sacrificer above, and his immured colleague below, repeating their incantations all the while. In the morning the pit is opened, and the man returns to the light of day. These sacrifices are called *<term>maranada bali</term>* <note>original: "maranada bali," meaning "death sacrifice" or "death atonement."</note>, or death atonements. They cost from ten to fifteen rupees. Instead of a human being, a cock is sometimes shut up in the pit and killed afterwards.

> "In cases of sore affliction befalling a whole <term>Grāma</term> <note>A village or rural settlement.</note> or <term>Nād</term> <note>A territorial district or regional division.</note> (village), such as small-pox, cholera, or cattle disease, the <term>ryots</term> <note>Peasant farmers or cultivators.</note> combine to appease the wrath of <term>Mariamma</term> <note>The goddess of smallpox and contagious diseases, widely worshipped as a protector or bringer of epidemics.</note> by collecting contributions of pigs, fowls, rice, cocoanuts, bread, and plantains from the different houses, and depositing them at the *<term>Mandu</term>* <note>The open village common or ceremonial meeting place.</note> : whence they are carried in procession with <term>tom-toms</term> <note>Traditional drums used in religious and social ceremonies.</note>. In one basket there is some rice, and the members of each house on coming out bring a little rice in the hand, and waving it round the head, throw it into the basket, with the belief that the dreaded evil will depart with the rice. At last the offerings are put down on the <term>Nād</term> boundary, the animals are killed, their blood is offered on a stone, the rice and basket are left, and the rest of the provisions are consumed by the persons composing the procession. The people of adjoining <term>Grāmas</term> or <term>Nāds</term> repeat the same ceremony, and thus the epidemic is supposed to be banished from the country. In still greater calamities, a flock of sheep is driven from <term>Nād</term> to <term>Nād</term>, and at last expelled from the country."

<summary>This page describes two types of propitiatory rituals: a "death atonement" involving a magician buried alive under a ritual fire, and communal village offerings intended to drive epidemics across territorial boundaries.</summary>

# CHAPTER VI

## MODES OF WORSHIP IN THE TAMIL COUNTRY

The ceremonies observed in the worship of village deities in the Tamil districts of Tanjore <note>Modern-day Thanjavur, famous for its grand temples.</note>, Trichinopoly <note>Modern-day Tiruchirappalli.</note>, and Cuddalore closely resemble those prevailing in the Telugu <note>Referring to the region of modern Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.</note> and Canarese <note>Referring to the region of modern Karnataka, where Kannada is spoken.</note> countries; but there are striking differences, which seem largely due to the influence of <term>Brāhmanical</term> <note>Relating to the religious traditions and social structure led by the Brahmin priestly class, often involving an emphasis on ritual purity and Sanskrit scriptures.</note> ideas and forms of worship. 

In the first place the ceremonial washing of the images and the processions during the festivals are much more elaborate in these districts than among the Telugus and Canarese. Then, again, the male deities connected with the goddesses are much more prominent, and tend much more to assume an independent position. <term>Iyenar</term> <note>A protective guardian deity (also spelled Aiyanar), often depicted as a warrior riding a white horse or elephant, stationed at the borders of villages.</note> is entirely independent and has a separate shrine and often a separate festival, while in many cases special sacrifices are made to the male attendants, <term>Madurai-Vīran</term> <note>A popular folk hero and "Great Warrior" of the city of Madurai who became a protective deity.</note> and <term>Munadian</term> <note>Likely a regional variant of Muniswaran, a fierce guardian spirit or deity.</note>. 

And then, in the third place, there is a widespread idea that animal sacrifices are distasteful to good and respectable deities, both male and female, so that no animal sacrifices are ever offered to Iyenar or to the good and kind goddesses. The ancient sacrifices of fowls, sheep, goats, and buffaloes are, indeed, still offered, but only to the male attendants, Madurai-Vīran and Munadian, and not the goddesses themselves; and while the animals are being killed a curtain is often drawn in front of the image of the goddess, or else the door of her shrine is shut, lest she should be shocked at the sight of the shedding of blood.

An account of the modes of worship and festivals in some typical villages will clearly show both the

<summary>This page introduces the specific religious customs of the Tamil region, noting how the influence of Brahmanical traditions has created more elaborate rituals and a physical separation between "refined" goddesses and the blood sacrifices offered to their fierce male attendants.</summary>

resemblances to the Telugu and Canarese <note>Referring to the cultural and linguistic traditions of the modern-day states of Andhra Pradesh and Karnataka.</note> uses, and also the striking differences.

*Vandipaliam, Cuddalore District.* In the district of Cuddalore, at a village called Vandipaliam, three deities are worshipped, Mariamman <note>The primary mother goddess of the Tamil countryside, often associated with rain and the prevention of diseases like smallpox.</note>, Draupati <note>The heroine of the epic Mahābhārata, who in South India is frequently worshipped as a village goddess, often associated with fire-walking rituals.</note> and Iyenar <note>Also spelled Aiyanar; a protective male deity who guards the village boundaries.</note>, each of whom has a separate shrine. Mariamman’s is the largest, about twelve feet high, twenty-five feet long, and twelve or fifteen feet broad. Draupati’s is less imposing, being only about six feet high, ten feet long, and eight feet broad.

Iyenar stands in the open, under a tree, with clay images of horses, elephants, dogs, and warriors (or <term>Vīrans</term> <note>Literally "Heroes"; these are guardian figures or minor deities who serve as the attendants or soldiers of a higher god.</note>) on either side. The <term>Vīrans</term> are supposed to keep watch over their master, while the animals serve as his *<term>vahanams</term>*, <note>original: "vahanams"</note> vehicles, on which he rides in his nightly chase after evil spirits. Individual villagers, both men and women, constantly offer private sacrifices consisting of boiled rice, fruit, sugar, incense, and camphor, or fowls and sheep to the Viran of Iyenar, and then the victim is brought before the image of the Viran. Water is sprinkled over it, a wreath of flowers is put round its neck by the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A non-Brahmin priest responsible for the rituals and upkeep of the village shrine.</note>, and turmeric and <term>kunkuma</term> <note>A sacred red powder, usually made from turmeric and lime, used for ritual markings on the forehead.</note> are smeared on its forehead. Then a bottle of <term>arrack</term> <note>A strong distilled alcoholic spirit made from fermented fruit or grain.</note>, a pot of <term>toddy</term> <note>An alcoholic drink made by fermenting the sap of palm trees.</note>, two or three cheroots, some <term>ganja</term> (Indian hemp) and opium, and dried fish are presented to the <term>Vīran</term>, afterwards to be consumed by the <term>pūjārī</term>. Camphor is burnt between the animal and the <term>Vīran</term>, and finally the head of the victim is cut off with a large chopper by a <term>pūjārī</term>, specially appointed for the purpose. Nothing special is done with the blood. The carcass is taken away by the offerer, and the head belongs to the <term>pūjārī</term> who cuts it off.

Once a year a public sacrifice is offered to Iyenar by the whole village, some time in April or May. On this occasion the image of Iyenar, which is made of granite and stands about one-and-a-half feet high, is first washed with gingelly oil,¹ lime-juice, milk and curds, with

---

¹ <term>Gingelly</term> <note>original: "Gingelly"</note> is an Indian name for *Sesame* <note>original: "Sesamum Indicum"</note> and *Oriental Sesame* <note>original: "Sesamum Orientale"</note>.

coconut, plantains, sugar, and some aromatic spices all mixed together. <note>These ingredients form a traditional offering known as Panchamrita, often used in Hindu ritual bathing (abhisheka).</note> Then coconut milk and sandalwood paste are put on the forehead, and a cloth is tied round its waist. The villagers bring boiled rice, coconuts, plantains, betel leaves and betel nut, sweet cakes of rice, flour, sugar and coconut in large quantities, and spread them all on leaves upon the ground before the image. The <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A priest or ritual specialist who performs the worship ceremonies.</note> burns incense and camphor, and finally the offerings are all distributed among the people present. After these offerings have been duly made, a curtain is drawn in front of the image of <term>Iyenar</term> <note>A protective male deity, often considered the guardian of the village boundaries.</note>, and sheep and fowls are sacrificed to the <term>Vīran</term> <note>Literally "Hero"; these are subordinate warrior deities who act as guardians or attendants to the main god.</note>, in the same way as at private sacrifices.

<term>Mariamman</term> <note>The primary goddess of the Tamil countryside, associated with rain and the curing of contagious diseases.</note> and <term>Draupati</term> <note>The heroine of the Mahabharata, worshipped in South India as a village goddess often linked to fire-walking rituals.</note> each have one annual festival, which lasts for ten days, but no animal sacrifices are ever offered during these festivals, or on any other occasions at the shrines of these goddesses. The festival begins with the hoisting of a flag, and then for eight days there are processions morning and evening, when a metal image of the goddess is carried in a palanquin through all the streets of the village. On the ninth day there is a car procession, when the image is put on a large car <note>A "car" or Ratha is a massive, towering wooden chariot pulled by hundreds of devotees.</note>, about twenty feet high, and dragged round the village, while on the night of the tenth day the image is put on a raft and dragged round the tank <note>The temple reservoir or ceremonial pond.</note> with torches, pipes, and <term>tom-toms</term> <note>A common term used by colonial-era writers for traditional South Indian drums like the thavil or parai.</note>. Offerings of boiled rice, fruits and flowers, incense and camphor, are made every day, and especially on the ninth day, when a large crowd usually assembles.

*<term>Shiyali</term>, Tanjore District.* At a large village in the Tanjore <note>Modern-day Thanjavur, a region famous for its high-culture Brahmanical temples and agricultural wealth.</note> district, named <term>Shiyali</term> <note>Modern Sirkazhi.</note>, where <term>Brāhmanism</term> <note>The orthodox, priestly form of Hinduism based on the Vedas and managed by the Brahman caste.</note> is very strong, <term>Iyenar</term>, <term>Pidāri</term>, <term>Mariamman</term>, <term>Aṅgalamman</term>, and <term>Kālīamman</term> are all worshipped with typical rites; but in this village, though no animal sacrifices are offered to <term>Kālīamman</term>, <term>Mariamman</term>, <term>Pidāri</term> or <term>Aṅgalamman</term>, yet they are offered to the subordinate male deities,

1 These ablutions <note>Ritual washings of a deity's image.</note> are copied from the great temples.

<summary>This page describes the ritual offerings and ten-day festivals for village deities in Tamil Nadu, highlighting how "vegetarian" offerings are made to goddesses while animal sacrifices are reserved for their male attendants. It also notes how village worship in areas like Shiyali has been influenced by the formal practices of larger Brahmanical temples.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of ritual practices in the Tamil-speaking regions of South India. It follows a discussion of the physical shrines of Mariamman, Draupati, and Iyenar in Cuddalore, moving here into the specific sequence of offerings and the structure of their annual festivals.</meta>

### THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

Madurai-Vīran <note>The "Hero of Madurai," a popular guardian deity often depicted as a warrior</note> and Munadian, who act as guardians of their shrines. Apparently, however, Piḍāri <note>A fierce form of the village goddess who protects against evil spirits</note> is regarded as slightly less squeamish in the matter of bloodshed, as curtains are drawn before the other three <note>referring to the more "pure" deities mentioned on the previous page</note> when animals are sacrificed to Madurai-Vīran and Munadian, but not before Piḍāri. No festival is held for Kāliamman <note>A local manifestation of the goddess Kali</note>, who seems to be a rather inert deity, of no great account in practical affairs.

During the festivals of Māriamman <note>The goddess of rain and smallpox</note>, Piḍāri, and Aṅgālamman <note>A fierce guardian goddess often associated with crematoriums</note> the ablutions are particularly elaborate. The image is washed twice every day, morning and evening, with water, oil, milk, coconut milk, a solution of turmeric, rosewater, a solution of sandalwood, honey, sugar, limes, and a solution of the bark of certain trees, separately in a regular order. This ceremonial washing is called in the Tamil country <term>Abhisheka</term> <note>original: "Abishegam"; the Sanskrit word for the ceremonial anointing of a king or a god</note>, and certainly deserves an imposing name. The <term>priest</term> <note>pūjārī</note> next repeats certain <term>sacred chants</term> <note>mantrams</note> before the image, after the example of <term>Brahmin</term> <note>The traditional priestly class of India</note> priests, and the offerings of the people—boiled rice, fruit, flowers, cakes, sugar, and so on—are presented; incense and camphor are burnt, and prostrations are made to the deity. Every evening, after sunset, a metal image of the goddess, placed on a small wooden platform decorated with tinsel and flowers, is carried in procession on the shoulders of the people through the main streets of the village, accompanied by fireworks, torches, and the inspiriting sounds of the <term>traditional drum</term> <note>referred to here as a "tom-tom"</note>. After the procession, camphor is burnt, a coconut is broken, and the image is replaced in the shrine.

On the tenth day of the festival, in the evening, animal sacrifices are offered, consisting of fowls and sheep, to Madurai-Vīran and Munadian. People who have made vows, in times of sickness or distress, or in order to secure some boon, bring their victims to the shrine. If the animal is a sheep or goat, it is then seized by the offerer and his friends, some of whom catch hold of its hind legs, while others hold fast to a rope fastened round its neck, and its head is cut off with one stroke of the chopper by one of the <term>priests</term> <note>original: "pūjārīs"</note>. The head is placed in front of the image of the <term>Hero of Madurai</term> <note>original: "Madurai-Vīran"; a popular guardian deity often depicted as a warrior</note> with its right foreleg in its mouth. During the killing of the victim a curtain is drawn in front of the <term>Goddess of Rain</term> <note>original: "Mariamman"</note> and the <term>Guardian Mother</term> <note>original: "Aṅgalamman"</note>, but not before the <term>Protective Goddess</term> <note>original: "Pidāri"</note>.

At the festival of the Goddess of Rain <note>original: "Mariamman"</note> two special ceremonies are performed, which are not performed at the other festivals in this village, but are quite common elsewhere. When sheep are sacrificed, the blood is collected in earthen vessels, mixed with boiled rice, and then sprinkled in the enclosure of the shrine and in the four corners of the main streets, through which the procession passes. What remains over is taken and thrown away in some field at a little distance from the village.

Then, after the animals have been sacrificed, the fire-walking ceremony <note>See p. 79 above.</note> takes place. A trench is dug inside the enclosure of the shrine and filled with logs of wood, which are set alight and reduced to glowing embers. In the evening the metal image <note>See pp 36-37 above.</note> of the Goddess of Rain <note>original: "Mariamman"</note> is brought out and held in front of the fire, while a short <term>ritual offering</term> <note>original: "pūjā"</note> is performed by burning camphor. Then the <term>priest</term> <note>original: "pūjārī"</note> walks barefooted over the red-hot embers, followed by other people, who have made vows to perform this act of devotion.

During the festival of the Protective Goddess <note>original: "Pidāri"</note>, there is a chariot <term>procession</term> <note>original: "car procession"; referring to the large, ornate wooden temple chariots pulled by devotees</note> on the ninth day, which is always the day of the new moon, and in the evening one or more buffaloes are sacrificed to the Hero of Madurai <note>original: "Madurai-Vīran"</note> or his companion <note>original: "Munadian"</note>. The victim is always a male buffalo, and is generally brought by some private person. Water and turmeric are first poured over it, and it is garlanded with flowers, and then its head is cut off with a single stroke of the chopper by a man of the <term>agricultural caste</term> <note>original: "Padayachi caste"</note>, who, by the way, is not an "Outcaste" <note>Historically, "Outcaste" referred to those outside the four-tiered varna system, now known as Dalits. The author notes the Padayachi are within the caste hierarchy.</note>. The head is placed in front
