The blood is collected in an earthen vessel and placed near the image of the <term>Protective Goddess</term> <note>original: "Piḍāri"; a fierce guardian deity</note> and left there the whole night. Next morning, the people assured me, only a small quantity of blood is found in the vessel, the <term>Protective Goddess</term> <note>original: "Piḍāri"</note> having drunk the greater part of it. The remains are poured away outside the compound of the shrine. The heads and carcasses of the buffaloes sacrificed are all handed over to the <term>outcastes</term> <note>original: "Pariahs"; a historical term for the Dalit community, who traditionally performed duties involving animal remains</note> of the village, who take them away for a feast.

At the festival of the <term>Guardian Mother</term> <note>original: "Angalamman"</note> pigs are sacrificed to her male guardians as well as sheep, goats, and fowls, not only by the <term>outcastes</term> <note>original: "Pariahs"</note>, but also by any caste of <term>laborers</term> <note>original: "Śūdras"; the fourth category in the traditional caste hierarchy</note>. The <term>Lord Aiyanar</term> <note>original: "Iyenar"; a male village guardian often depicted on horseback</note> festival takes place at the same time as the <term>Protective Goddess</term> <note>original: "Piḍāri"</note> festival, and the same ceremonies are performed, except that no animals are sacrificed at his shrine.

The idea, so naïvely expressed in the <term>Protective Goddess</term> <note>original: "Piḍāri"</note> festival at <note>modern Sirkazhi</note> Shiyali, that the goddess actually drinks the blood of the victims, is not uncommon. In many villages some of the blood is collected in an earthen vessel and placed inside the shrine after the sacrifice. At one village, where pigs are sacrificed to the <term>Hero of Madurai</term> <note>original: "Madurai-Vīran"; a deified warrior-hero</note>, though the blood is not collected in any vessel, but simply allowed to flow on the ground, the people assured me that the <term>Hero of Madurai</term> <note>original: "Madurai-Vīran"</note> drinks it. In the same way the rice and the blood sprinkled through the streets of a village or round the boundaries, which is called *sacrificial food* <note>original: "poli"</note> in <note>The language of the neighboring Andhra region</note> Telugu, is regarded as food for the evil spirits. In many Tamil villages the rice and blood are made up into little balls and thrown up in the air, where, as the people firmly believe, they are seized by the deity to whom the sacrifice is offered, or by the evil spirits that hover round the procession.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of village religious practices in South India, moving from the physical handling of sacrifices to the spiritual process of invoking the deity through a human medium to gain permission for local festivals.</meta>

->### WORSHIP IN THE TAMIL COUNTRY<-

invokes the deity to the accompaniment of a chorus of singers, who are either his relations or who share the income with him. The invocation takes place either near the temple or at some prescribed spot in the direction from which the deity is popularly believed to have arrived at the village. In the latter case, after the <term>ritual anointing</term> <note>original: "abishegam"; the ceremonial washing or pouring of sacred liquids over an idol</note> is over, the persons present move in a body to the prescribed spot and then commence the invocation. This invocation, which to the persons present is a period of some anxiety, lasts from ten to thirty minutes, when all on a sudden one of those present gets inspired. The meaning of the invocation is a call to the deity to come and help them in their celebrations. The inspired <note>Refers to a person who enters a state of trance or religious possession, acting as a vessel for the deity</note> attentively watches the goddess during the early stages of the worship. Later on, with closed eyes he listens to the song of the <term>priest</term> <note>original: "pūjārī"</note> and his chorus. He goes into a counterfeit slumber, first shutting one eye, then the other, then nodding, then swaying so much to one side that the bystanders have to save him from falling. At last he collapses into the arms of one or more of his neighbours. He is watched very intently by all those present. The attention of the votaries is transferred from the goddess to the inspired man. All those seated around him move away from him, and a space is cleared to enable him to move freely. Camphor <note>A fragrant resin used widely in Hindu worship; when burned, it is believed to purify the air and symbolize the burning of the ego</note> is then burnt before him, and the inspired man is moved either to speak or be silent or laugh or weep. The speaking and laughing are welcomed by the votaries with delight. They then ask him to grant them permission for celebrating the festival. Generally the permission is granted when he is either speaking or laughing. But if he should weep or be silent, that is taken as an indication of the wrath of the deity, and fresh songs are sung in louder tones to appease the deity. After a fairly long interval, when all become anxious about their own safety, and when the songs have been well-nigh exhausted, the inspired man is again approached with burning camphor. This time he is generally more sympathetic. Very often he gives his unconditional assent for the celebration of the festival. But occasion-

ally, after according sanction to celebrate the festival, the inspired man <note>A person in a state of religious trance believed to be possessed by a deity</note> lifts up his hand and points at some one whose conduct towards the community might not have been acceptable to them in the previous year. With some reluctance, the man pointed out seeks the forgiveness of the inspired man, and is assured of it on his promising to sacrifice a sheep or a fowl. After permission to celebrate the festival is granted, the people present proceed with the celebration."¹

"Some years ago, it is said, a horse grazing close by the spot where the goddess had been invoked, got terrified by the noise of the drums, etc., and, after galloping round the temple thrice, stopped in front of the entrance. The villagers attributed the horse's action to the inspiration of the goddess."²

On the day of the car procession, which takes place on the second day of the festival, "a well-formed bronze image of the idol is placed in a car <note>A large, ornate wooden chariot used to carry temple deities during festivals</note> immediately after the usual <term>ritual anointing</term> <note>original: "abishegam"; the ceremonial washing of an idol with sacred substances</note> ceremony, and the car is dragged through the several streets of a village by all the villagers. The <term>priest</term> <note>original: "pūjārī"</note> and the others who wore the <term>sacred wrist-tie</term> <note>original: "kapu"; a thread or amulet tied to the wrist to signify the start of a religious vow</note> on the first day will continue to appear in yellow garments and take active parts in the car procession. The car will generally be preceded by drums and trumpets. In front of the car, one of the villagers who has special pretensions to religious fervour carries the <term>sacred pot</term> <note>original: "karagam"; a decorated vessel representing the deity, often balanced on the head during ritual dances</note> on his head, and entertains the people by vigorous movements to and fro without allowing the <term>sacred pot</term> to fall. His dress on such occasions consists of loose drawers, which are prevented from slipping by a tape passing round his waist. Generally nowadays a sash is used to keep it in position. The abdomen of this dancer is left open to public view. A piece of square cloth about a yard in diameter protects his back. The right hand holds a long sword and the left hand either a lime or green leaves in a piece of cloth. he entertains the villagers. Beyond sipping lime-juice he is not allowed to eat or drink anything. As the procession, consisting of drums, the <term>ritual pot dancer</term> <note>original: "karagam"; a performance where a decorated pot is balanced on the dancer's head</note> and the goddess in the car, passes through a village, sacrifices are offered to the goddess at all points where two streets cross. The sacrifices in this <term>administrative district</term> <note>original: "taluk"</note> are fowls on this occasion, owing to the absence of large villages where the people can afford to sacrifice sheep. As the goddess passes through the main streets of a village, at all the houses cocoanuts are broken and incense is burnt. The <term>priest</term> <note>original: "pūjārī"</note> is also given some pecuniary remuneration, but he cannot be sure of it in all villages. He is, however, entitled to retain for his own use the smaller half of the cocoanut presented to him for being offered to the deity. He generally manages to shelve it into a big basket kept by his side for the purpose. The car will go only through the main streets of a village, and will return to its original place of starting without stopping anywhere. It is considered a bad omen amongst the Hindus if the gods and the goddesses have to remain in the streets even for a night in their car. Hence the place of starting must be reached before sunset under any circumstances. The ceremonies for the day will be over when, after reaching the place of starting, a fowl or sheep is sacrificed and the <term>priest</term> <note>original: "pūjārī"</note> and others return homeward. In villages where a so-called ‘husband’ <note>A man chosen to play a symbolic ritual role as the consort of the goddess for the duration of the festival</note> has been appointed, that person is bound to sleep in the temple, or near its precincts, for this night also. During the night a dramatic performance at the expense of the leading <term>farmers</term> <note>original: "ryots"</note> of the village is given. The performance lasts generally from ten p.m. till dawn, and the drama enacted nowadays is a compromise between the rude country dance and the present day dramas.”¹

**Essene, Trichinopoly District.** <note>Now known as the Tiruchirappalli district</note> Another characteristic festival, which is specially conducted and paid for by the <term>lower-caste laborers</term> <note>original: "Pariahs"; a historical term for social groups outside the traditional caste system, now referred to as Dalits</note>, is held in the Trichinopoly district, near the village of Essene, during the month of July or August.

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

About a mile south of the village, on the road to Madras, there is a shrine, consisting of a large open enclosure about thirty feet square, surrounded by a low stone wall. On the west side of the enclosure are three large images of men seated on tigers, each about eight feet high, representing Pandur-Karuppanna <note>original: "Pandur-Karuppanna"; Karuppanna, or Karuppasamy, is a prominent rural guardian deity. Pandur is the name of an ancient village associated with this specific figure.</note>, Padu-Karuppanna (*that is*, the New Karuppanna), and Ursuthiyan (he who goes round the village); and in front of them a number of small stones, black with oil, six carved roughly into the figures of men and women, and about six quite plain, some of them only about six inches high. At right angles to this row of stones, on the south side, runs a small shrine with seven small female figures representing the *kanimars*, *that is*, the seven virgins, while at the north-east corner is a small separate enclosure with the figure of Madurai-Vīran <note>A popular Tamil folk deity, often depicted as a warrior protector.</note> on horseback with his two wives seated in front of him. The presiding deities of the shrine are the goddesses, represented by the small stones, and not the imposing but ugly male creatures seated on tigers.

When the time for the festival has been fixed, each family of <term>Pariahs</term> <note>A historical term for a specific South Indian Dalit community who often served as village watchmen and musicians, and who maintain their own distinct religious traditions.</note> gives about one rupee for the expenses. Then, on the first day, they perform <term>worship</term> <note>original: "pūjā"</note> in the Pariah street of the village Melakari close by the shrine. Three sets of seven brass pots, standing one above the other, are placed in one of the huts, and on the top of each set a small image made of the <term>five metals</term> <note>original: "panchaloha"; a traditional sacred five-metal alloy consisting of gold, silver, copper, iron, and lead or tin.</note>, one image representing Padu-Karuppanna, another Pandur-Karuppanna, and the third a female deity, Malaiyayi, who is the wife of Karuppanna. Boiled rice is first offered, cocoanuts are broken and incense burnt to the pots, and then at night there is a sword and spear dance in the compound of the hut.

On the second day the <term>Pariahs</term> come to the shrine, and wash the small black stones and images representing the goddesses, with oil, milk, cocoanut milk, lime-juice, and water, put on them some new pieces of cloth, garland them with flowers, and mark them with sandal-

wood paste. Then they boil rice on the spot, and offer it to the goddesses, and afterwards bring to the shrine sheep, pigs, and fowls. Water is first poured over each sheep, and, if it shivers, it is accepted by the goddesses; if not, it is rejected.¹ Then one of the <term>Pariah</term> <term>pūjārīs</term> <note>Priests who perform ritual offerings, often in folk or village traditions rather than in large Brahmanical temples.</note> cuts off the head of the acceptable victims with a sword. If the head is cut off at one blow, another <term>pūjārī</term>, who is supposed to be under the influence of the deity, sucks out the blood from the neck of the carcass. During the night he thus sucks the blood of about a hundred sheep. After the sheep have been killed, four or five pigs are offered by a few of the <term>Pariahs</term>, who have made vows. The head of each pig is cut off with a chopper, and then a small quantity of blood is collected in some earthen vessels, newly brought from the potter's house, and placed inside the shrine. When all the people have left the place, the <term>pūjārīs</term> mix this blood with some boiled rice, and throw it about a hundred yards outside the shrine to the north-west, north-east, south-east, and south-west, and that ends the festival.

*Trichinopoly.* <note>The colonial name for the city of Tiruchirappalli in Tamil Nadu.</note> The sucking of the blood is a horrid business, but not so horrid as an annual ceremony which takes place every February or March at Trichinopoly, one of the great centres of trade and education in the Tamil country, during the festival of **Kalumaiamman**. <note>A village goddess (Amman) specifically invoked for protection against epidemics.</note> She is regarded as the guardian against cholera and cattle plague, and epidemics generally. A very fat <term>pūjārī</term> of the **Vellāla** <note>A high-ranking Tamil caste traditionally associated with agriculture and landholding.</note> caste, who holds this unenviable office by hereditary right, is lifted up above the vast crowd on the arms of two men; some two thousand kids <note>Young goats.</note> are then sacrificed one after the other, the blood of the first eight or ten is collected in a large silver vessel holding about a quart, and handed up to the <term>pūjārī</term>, who drinks it all. Then, as the throat of each kid is cut, the animal is handed up to him, and he sucks or pretends to suck the blood out of the carcass. The belief of the people is that the blood is consumed

possessed by the spirit of Kalumaiamman in the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A priest who performs rituals in folk or village traditions, distinct from the Brahmin priests of larger temples.</note>; and her image stands on a platform during the ceremony about fifteen yards away.

A similar idea is probably expressed by a particularly revolting method of killing sheep, which is not uncommon in <term>Tamil</term> <note>A major ethno-linguistic group in South India, primarily in the state of Tamil Nadu.</note> villages during these festivals. One of the <term>pūjārīs</term>, who is sometimes painted to represent a leopard, flies at the sheep like a wild beast, seizes it by the throat with his teeth, and kills it by biting through the jugular vein.

*Irungalur, near Trichinopoly.* <note>Now known as Tiruchirappalli.</note> There is another strange ceremony, which is quite common in the Tamil country, connected with the propitiation of the boundary goddess, where the blood of the victim seems to be regarded as the food of malignant spirits. At Irungalur, a village about fourteen miles from Trichinopoly, it forms the conclusion of the festival of the local goddess Kurumbai. During the first seven days the image is duly washed, offerings of rice and fruit are made, and processions are held through the streets of the village. Then, on the eighth day, a small earthen pot, called the <term>karagam</term> <note>A sacred ritual pot, elaborately decorated, which represents the presence of the deity.</note>, is prepared at the shrine of the goddess. The elaborate decorations of the <term>karagam</term> have been already described,¹ and I need not describe them again. When it is ready, some boiled rice, fruits, coconuts, and incense are first offered to it, and then the <term>pūjārī</term> ties on his wrist a <term>kapu</term> <note>A protective cord or amulet, usually yellowed with turmeric, used to shield the wearer from evil influences during a rite.</note>, <note>original: "i.e."</note> that is, a cord dyed with yellow turmeric, to protect him from evil spirits. A lamb is next brought and sacrificed in front of him, to give him supernatural power, and he then takes the <term>karagam</term> on his head, marches with it in procession through the village to the sound of <term>tom-toms</term> <note>Traditional drums used in South Asian ceremonies.</note> and pipes, and finally deposits it under a booth erected in the middle of the village. On the eighth, ninth and tenth days the <term>karagam</term> is taken in procession morning and evening, and rice and fruits, camphor and incense are also offered to it.

On the tenth day, at about seven a.m., before the pro-

<meta>This page continues the description of the festival for the goddess Kurumbai in the village of Irungalur, following the preparation of the sacred ritual pot.</meta>

->### WORSHIP IN THE TAMIL COUNTRY<-

When the procession starts, a lamb is killed in front of the <term>karagam</term> <note>A sacred ritual pot, often decorated with flowers and neem leaves, which represents the presence of the deity during the festival.</note>. Its throat is first cut, and then the head is severed. The blood is collected in a new earthen pot filled with boiled rice. This pot is placed in a frame made of ropes and carried by a <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A non-Brahmin village priest who conducts the rituals for local deities.</note> to a stone, approximately four feet high, called the <term>ellai-kal</term> <note>From the Tamil words for "boundary" (ellai) and "stone" (kal).</note>, which is planted in the ground about three hundred yards away. A crowd of villagers chases after him with wild shouts, though no drums or pipes are played. When he reaches the boundary stone, he runs around it three times. On the third circuit, he throws the pot over his shoulder, behind him, onto another smaller stone—about two feet high and six feet in circumference—which stands at the base of the <term>ellai-kal</term>. The earthen pot is shattered to pieces, scattering the rice and blood over the two stones and the surrounding area.

The priest then runs quickly back to the booth where the <term>karagam</term> is kept without ever looking behind him, followed by the crowd in complete silence. The man carrying the pot is believed to be possessed by the goddess <term>Kurambai</term>, and he is in a frantic, trance-like state as he runs toward the boundary stone; he must be supported by members of the crowd to keep him from falling to the ground. This pouring out of rice and blood is seen as an offering to appease an evil spirit residing in the boundary stone known as <term>Ellai-Karuppu</term> <note>A fierce "Black God" of the boundary who acts as a guardian, often accompanied by a host of lesser, potentially malevolent spirits.</note>, as well as all the malignant spirits of the neighborhood who serve him. Once the priest returns to the booth, he prostrates himself before the <term>karagam</term>, and all the villagers do the same. They then go to bathe in a nearby <term>tank</term> <note>A man-made reservoir or pond used for ritual bathing and irrigation.</note> before returning to the booth, where another lamb is sacrificed and the procession begins its journey through the village streets.

In the evening of the same day, a pig, a sheep, and a rooster are purchased using the shrine's funds and taken to the shrine itself, located outside the village. There, they are sacrificed in front of a stone image of <term>Madurai-Vīran</term> <note>Literally "The Hero of Madurai," a popular guardian deity in Tamil folklore often depicted as a warrior with a sword.</note>, which resides in a separate small shrine in front of the main temple of <term>Kurumbai</term>. A large amount of rice is boiled within the walls of the compound, and the meat from the three sacrificed animals is cooked and prepared...

->**Pullambadi, Trichinopoly District.**<-

The ceremony of propitiating the spirit of the boundary-stone is very common in the Trichinopoly district, though there are the usual variations of local custom in performing it. At a village called Pullambadi, it takes place in connection with the festival of Kulanthalamman, which lasts for fifteen days. On the first day the image is washed, and a sheep is killed outside the enclosure as a sacrifice to <term>Karuppu</term> <note>Meaning "The Black One," a fierce male guardian deity who often serves as a subordinate to the main village goddess.</note>, the door of the shrine of the goddess being closed. Rice, fruit, flowers, etc., are also offered to the goddess. On the next six days only rice, fruits, etc., are offered; but on the eighth day two more sheep are sacrificed to Karuppu. From the ninth to the fifteenth day the metal image of the goddess <note>See pages 36-37 of the original text for a description of these images.</note> is taken in procession round the village, each day on a different <term>vāhanam</term> <note>A ceremonial "vehicle" or mount, often an animal figure, used to carry the deity’s image. See page 90 of the original text.</note>: on the fifteenth day it is carried on a car, and on this day three sheep are killed in front of the shrine, before the procession starts, the blood being collected in an earthen pot and mixed with boiled rice. 

Then a sheep is sacrificed at each of the nine corners of the streets that surround the temple, and the blood of all the sheep is put into earthen vessels by a <term>pūjārī</term> of the <term>Shervagaru</term> caste, called the <term>Kappukaran</term> <note>Literally "the one who wears the protection" (kāppu); a ritual official who often wears a sacred thread on his wrist during the festival.</note>, the animals being all killed by one of the <term>Pariahs</term> <note>A historical term for members of the Paraiyar community, now generally referred to as Dalits.</note>. The Kappukaran then mixes all the blood and rice together in one large earthen pot and carries it to the village, which is about half a mile away. Nine more sheep are sacrificed at nine other corners of the village itself, and their blood is again collected and mixed with the rest. When the car has come back to

*Sembia, near Pudukkottai.* At another village, Sembia, in the <term>Pudukkottai</term> <note>A historical region and former princely state in Tamil Nadu.</note> <term>taluq</term> <note>original: "taluq"; a local administrative subdivision of a civil district.</note>, the ceremonies connected with the propitiation of boundary spirits are rather more elaborate. There is a boundary-stone at each of the four corners of the village site, five more stones inside the village, and another stone on the boundary of the village land.

During the <term>Pidāri</term> <note>original: "Pidāri"; a fierce Tamil village goddess often worshipped as a protector against evil spirits and disease.</note> festival boiled rice, fruits, etc., are offered at all the nine boundary stones in the village. The metal image of <term>Pidāri</term> is then carried in procession round the village on a wooden horse; and at each of the nine stones in the village itself a lamb is sacrificed. When this procession is ended, the <term>pūjārī</term> with the <term>kapu</term> on his wrist takes the earthen pot, with the blood of the black goat inside it, fastens it inside a frame of ropes, and runs to the

An untoward event happened a few years ago in connexion with one of these <term>Piḍāri</term> festivals, at a village in the <term>Trichinopoly</term> <note>Modern-day Tiruchirappalli.</note> district. The festival had commenced and the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A priest or ritual leader who performs the offerings.</note> had tied the <term>kapu</term> <note>A sacred thread or amulet tied to the wrist to signify the start of a religious vow or period of sanctification.</note> on his wrist, when a dispute arose between the trustees of the shrine, which caused the festival to be stopped. The dispute could not be settled, and the festival was suspended for three years, and during all that time there could be no marriages among the <term>Udaya caste</term> <note>A community of landed agriculturists in Tamil Nadu.</note>, while the poor <term>pūjārī</term>, with the <term>kapu</term> on his wrist, had to remain the whole of the three years in the temple, not daring to go out, lest <term>Piḍāri</term> in her wrath should slay him.

*Tukanapaliam, Tanjore District.* At a village in the <term>Tanjore</term> <note>Modern-day Thanjavur.</note> district, called Tukanapaliam, the boundary spirits are propitiated during the <term>Kāliamman</term> <note>A local form of the goddess Kali, often viewed as a protector of the village.</note> festival by the sacrifice of a buffalo. On the last day of the festival the image of <term>Kāliamman</term>, who in many parts of the <term>Tanjore</term> district is specially the goddess of the boundary, is taken to the boundary-stone, and then one male buffalo is killed beside the stone and buried in a pit close by; but nothing is done either with the head or the blood.

...[Ponni]amman, <term>Vishalakshmiamman</term> <note>A name meaning "the wide-eyed mother," a local form of the goddess Parvati.</note>, and <term>Angalamman</term> <note>A powerful guardian goddess often associated with protection and the fiercer aspects of the divine feminine.</note>, and three subordinate male deities, <term>Madurai-Viran</term> <note>Literally "The Hero of Madurai," a popular deified warrior in Tamil folklore.</note>, <term>Bathalama</term>, and <term>Iyenar</term> <note>A prominent village guardian deity; the author notes it is unusual to find him in a subordinate role.</note>. (This is the only place where I have come across <term>Iyenar</term> as a subordinate deity.) In this temple <term>Ujinihoṅkālī</term> <note>Ujjaini Mahakali, a form of the goddess Kali associated with the city of Ujjain.</note> is worshipped by all classes, including the <term>Brāhmans</term> <note>The priestly social class.</note>, and while some of the <term>pūjārīs</term> <note>Priests who perform the ritual offerings.</note> are <term>Śūdras</term> <note>A broad social category traditionally associated with agriculture and various services; they frequently serve as priests for local village deities.</note>, the others are <term>Brāhmans</term>. An old <term>Munsiff</term> <note>A local judicial or administrative official.</note> of the district told me that he could remember the time when all the <term>pūjārīs</term> were <term>Śūdras</term>. The <term>Brāhmans</term> appear to have secured a footing in the shrine about fifty years ago. The yearly festival is held in February or March, and lasts sixteen days.

On the first day, called <term>Kankanadhāranam</term> <note>A Sanskrit-derived term meaning "the wearing of the bracelet," signaling the start of a religious observance.</note> (*i.e.* the wearing of the bracelet), *kaṅkaṇam*, *i.e.* a gold bangle or bracelet, is prepared for the occasion by the temple authorities and put on the wrist of the image, which is made of the five metals <note>An alloy of five metals (typically gold, silver, copper, iron, and lead/tin) considered sacred in South Indian sculpture.</note> in the form of a woman, and stands about three feet high. This must be done at an auspicious hour either of the day or night. One of the <term>Śūdra</term> <term>pūjārīs</term> at the same time puts a <term>kapu</term> <note>A sacred thread or ritual cord tied to the wrist to mark a period of sanctification or a vow.</note> on his own right wrist. Boiled rice, cocoanuts, plantains, and limes are afterwards offered to the goddess, lights are placed all over the shrine, and incense and camphor are burnt. For eight days the same ceremonies are repeated, the same bangle put on the wrist of the image and the same <term>kapu</term> on the wrist of the <term>pūjārī</term>.

On the ninth day this bangle is removed and put in the treasury of the shrine, and a new one put on. The same offerings are made as on the other days, but on this day, for the first time, the image is taken out and carried in procession on a small wooden platform, adorned with tinsel, through the village with music and <term>tom-toms</term> <note>Traditional drums used in religious and public ceremonies.</note>, torches and fireworks.

These ceremonies are then repeated till the end of the festival, but each day, till the fourteenth, the image is carried on a different vehicle or <term>vahanam</term> <note>The ritual "mount" or vehicle of a deity, such as an animal or a chariot, used to transport the idol during processions.</note>; on the tenth day on a wooden horse, on the eleventh on a car <note>A large temple chariot.</note>, on the twelfth on a wooden lion, on the thirteenth in a palanquin, on the fourteenth on a swan or bull. No animal sacrifices are performed during the festival at

...the shrine itself: but on the eleventh day many sheep and goats are sacrificed in connection with the car procession <note>A ritual where the deity’s image is placed on a large, ornate wooden chariot and pulled through the streets by devotees.</note>. Just after the image is put on the car, a kid <note>A young goat.</note> is brought in front of it and decapitated by a village watchman, or <term>kāvalgār</term> <note>A traditional village guard or watchman who often holds specific ritual responsibilities during festivals.</note>, of the <term>Umbellayar</term> caste. When the car returns, a sheep is sacrificed in front of it. Its head is cut off by the <term>kāvalgār</term>, and its head and body are allowed to lie upon the ground, while fruits, coconuts, and camphor are offered. The man who provides the sheep ultimately takes the body and the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>The priest who performs the ritual offerings.</note> the head. While the car is being dragged through the streets, people who have made vows bring sheep to the doors of their houses, and the <term>kāvalgār</term> comes with his heavy chopper and cuts off their heads.

#### *Kannanur, near Trichinopoly* 
<note>Trichinopoly is the colonial name for the modern city of Tiruchirappalli.</note> 
At the neighboring village of Kannanur there is a curious local variation in the ordinary rite of sacrifice. During the festival of <term>Mariamman</term> <note>The most prominent village goddess in South India, associated with rain and protection against contagious diseases.</note> many people who have made vows bring sheep, goats, fowls, pigeons, parrots, cows, and calves to the temple, and leave them in the compound alive. At the end of the festival these animals are all sold to a contractor. Two years ago they fetched 400 Rupees <note>original: "Rs. 400"</note>, a good haul for the temple, which is particularly a large one, covering two acres of ground enclosed by a high wall.

Buffalo sacrifices are not as common in the Tamil <note>Referring to the Tamil-speaking regions, largely modern Tamil Nadu.</note> as in the Telugu country <note>Referring to the Telugu-speaking regions, largely modern Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.</note>, but they are offered in many villages, especially in connection with the worship of <term>Madura-Kāḷiamman</term> <note>A fierce local form of the goddess Kali, named after the city of Madurai.</note>.

#### *Turayur, near Trichinopoly* 
At a village called Turayur, near Trichinopoly, a buffalo sacrifice is offered once in five or six years. Before the day of the festival is fixed, the chief men of the village go to the shrine, offer rice and fruits, etc., and ask the goddess whether they may perform the festival. If a lizard utters a chirp in a part of the temple fixed on beforehand, it is...

...taken as a sign that permission is given, and the festival is arranged. The buffaloes devoted for sacrifice are generally chosen some time beforehand by people who make vows in sickness or trouble, and then allowed to roam about the village at will. When they become troublesome, the people go and ask permission of the deity to hold a sacrifice. The buffaloes are brought to the shrine on the appointed day and killed by a man of the <term>Kallar</term> <note>A caste historically associated with martial traditions and village policing in the Tamil region.</note> caste, who cuts off the heads with a chopper. Nothing is done with the blood, but both head and carcass are thrown into a pit close by the shrine as soon as the animal is dead. The same pit is used at each festival, but it is cleared out for each occasion. When all the carcasses have been put in, incense and camphor are burnt, coconuts and fruits are offered on the edge of the pit, and then earth is thrown in, and the carcasses are covered up. This takes place outside the temple walls, and during the sacrifice a curtain is drawn before the shrine, where the immovable stone image of the goddess is located; but, on the other hand, the metal image, used in processions, is taken out before the sacrifice begins, carried on a wooden lion <note>The lion is the traditional "vehicle" or mount of the goddess Durga or Kali.</note>, and placed on four stone pillars specially erected for the purpose outside the temple, about four or five yards from the place where the buffaloes are killed. No curtain is drawn before this image: the sacrifice is performed in full view of the goddess. It is a curious little compromise between ancient custom and <term>Brāhman</term> <note>The priestly class of the Hindu social hierarchy, who typically promote vegetarian offerings and view blood sacrifice as ritually polluting.</note> prejudice.

### *Another village.* 

At another village I found that <term>Brāhman</term> ideas had taken one step further in the worship of <term>Madura-Kāliamman</term> <note>A local form of the goddess Kali, "The Mother Kali of Madurai."</note>, as no animal sacrifices of any kind are offered there to the goddess herself, but only to <term>Periyanna-svāmī</term> <note>Literally "Great Brother Lord," a male guardian deity.</note>, a male deity residing on the top of a hill some three miles away from her shrine; and even there the <term>pūjārīs</term> <note>Priests who perform the daily rituals and offerings.</note> lamented that, owing to the degeneracy of the age, offerers now take away both head and carcass for their own use, instead of leaving the head, as was done in better days, to be the perquisite of the <term>pūjārīs</term>. ...that there used to be buffalo sacrifices some twenty years ago; but the people did not know to what deity they were offered, and none are ever offered now.

*Pullambadi, Trichinopoly District.* At Pullambadi, a village of some size in the Trichinopoly district, I was told that **Madura-Kālī** <note>The goddess Kali of the city of Madurai.</note> only accepts **Vedic** <note>original: "Vedic"; This word literally means consistent with the Vedas, the oldest and most orthodox scriptures of Hinduism.</note>—that is, orthodox—sacrifices. All animal sacrifices, therefore, are made to **Madurai-Vīran** <note>The "Hero of Madurai," a popular guardian deity.</note> or **Karuppu** <note>Literally "The Black One," a fierce protective god.</note>, her male guardians, and a curtain is drawn before **Madura-Kālī** while they are being offered. The <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A village priest who performs the ritual offerings.</note> in this village collects the blood of the animals in an earthen pot, mixes it with rice, and makes it up into little balls. Then, possessed by **Karuppu** or **Madurai-Vīran**, he takes the pot and runs round the temple enclosure, and at each corner throws up a ball of rice and blood, which is carried off by **Karuppu** or **Madurai-Vīran** (so the people firmly believe) and never falls down. The <term>Munsiff</term> <note>A local village official or magistrate.</note>, who was quite a well-educated man, assured me that this was a fact, and that he had seen it with his own eyes—only, as he admitted, the ceremony takes place in the dark!

*Vallum, Tanjore District.* Buffaloes are offered in some villages of the Tanjore district both to **Kāliamman** <note>Mother Kali.</note> and **Pidāri** <note>A fierce local goddess often associated with protection against demons and disease.</note>. Where the sacrifice is strictly performed, as at Vallum, the <term>pūjārī</term>, who is a <term>Śūdra</term> <note>A member of the fourth social class in the traditional Hindu hierarchy, often including artisans and laborers.</note>, lives only on milk and fruit, and eats only once a day for a whole month beforehand, and on the day of the sacrifice puts the <term>kapu</term> <note>A sacred thread or amulet tied around the wrist to symbolize a vow or spiritual protection; see page 100.</note> on his right wrist before he takes hold of the sacrificial sword. It is supposed that he is first inspired by the deity before he can kill the victim. He cuts off the head sometimes in one blow, and sometimes in two or three. Nothing is done with the blood, and both head and carcass are buried in a pit near the shrine. The dung of the victim is mixed with water and poured over the image of the deity. In some villages in the Tamil country, it is customary to take the entrails of the victim and hang them round the <term>pūjārī's</term> neck and put...

the liver in his mouth during the procession,¹ when the rice and blood is sprinkled through the village, and sometimes part of the entrails is cooked with rice and presented before the image. At one village I found that, after this procession has gone round the houses, it passes on to the **burning ghat** <note>original: "burning ghat"; the riverside locations or grounds used for the cremation of the dead.</note>,² where the entrails are taken from the *pūjārī's* <note>A village priest, often from a non-Brahmin community, who conducts local rituals.</note> neck and the liver from his mouth; and both are laid down with some curry and rice, which is afterwards eaten by a few of the low-caste people. These extremely repulsive processions, however, are not, as in the *Telugu* <note>Referring to the people and language of the regions north of the Tamil country, primarily modern-day Andhra Pradesh.</note> country, especially connected with buffalo sacrifices.

*Another village.* An unfeeling custom prevails in one village that I came across, which is considerably worse than seething a kid in its mother's milk. <note>A reference to a prohibition in Biblical law (Exodus 23:19), used here by the author to emphasize the cruelty of the local custom.</note> When a pig is sacrificed to *Aṅgalamman* <note>A fierce manifestation of the Mother Goddess, often worshipped as a guardian of the village and associated with cremation grounds.</note>, its neck is first cut slightly at the top and the blood allowed to flow on to some boiled rice placed on a plantain leaf, and then the rice soaked in its own blood is given to the pig to eat. If the pig eats it, the omen is good. If not, the omen is bad. But in any case the pig has its head cut off by a *Śūdra* <note>A member of the fourth traditional social class (varna), which includes many agricultural and artisan communities.</note> *pūjārī*. In some villages the blood of the pig, offered to *Aṅgalamman*, is mixed with boiled rice, taken to the **burning ghat**, where the dead bodies are burned, and thrown into the air at night as an offering to the spirits that hover round the place.

*Pudukkottai sub-division, Trichinopoly District.* Among other curious applications of the blood of animals, not the least interesting and significant is the one that prevails in nearly all the villages of the *Pudukkottai* <term>taluq</term> <note>A sub-division of a district for administrative or revenue purposes.</note> of the *Trichinopoly* <note>The historical colonial spelling of Tiruchirappalli.</note> district, where it is the custom for all the villagers to dip cloths in the blood of animals slain simply for food, and hang them up on the eaves of their houses to protect the cattle against disease. This is probably a relic of an age when the eating of animal food under any circumstances had a religious significance.

¹ See above, p. 52, and below, p. 148.
² The place where the dead are burned.

### THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

*Pullambadi, Trichinopoly District.* <note>Pullambadi is a town in the modern-day Tiruchirappalli district of Tamil Nadu.</note> It is refreshing to turn to a custom connected with the worship of village deities which can make some pretence to practical utility. In the village of Pullambadi, at the shrine of Kulanthalamman <note>A local village goddess; the name may imply she is a "Mother of the Children" or associated with the village tank.</note>, whose festival has already been described,¹ an interesting custom prevails, which seems to be not uncommon in those parts. When a creditor cannot recover a debt, he writes out a statement of his claim against his debtor on dried palmyra leaves <note>The leaves of the palmyra palm were the traditional writing material in South India for centuries before the widespread use of paper.</note>, presents it to the goddess, and hangs it up on a spear before her image. If the claim is just and the debtor does not pay, it is believed that he will be afflicted with sickness and terrifying dreams, and that in his dreams the goddess will warn him to pay the debt at once. If, however, he disputes the claim, then he in turn writes out his statement of the case and hangs it up on the same spear. The deity then decides which statement is true and afflicts the perjurer with dreams and misfortunes till the false statement is withdrawn. When the claim is acknowledged, the debtor brings the money to the <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A village priest who performs the daily rituals and acts as an intermediary between the devotees and the deity.</note>, who places it before the goddess, and then sends for the creditor and informs him that the debt is paid. All the money thus paid into the temple coffers is handed over to the various creditors during the festival in April or May, after deducting the amount due to the temple treasury. This is certainly a simple method of doing justice in the matter of debts, and probably just as effective as the more elaborate and more expensive processes of our courts of law. I was told that about ten creditors come to the temple every year, and that the temple had made about Rs. 3,000 <note>Rupees. At the time of this writing, 3,000 Rupees was a very substantial sum of money.</note> as its commission on the debts collected during the last thirty years. Before that time the people came and stated their claims to the goddess orally, promising to give her a share if the debts were recovered; but some thirty years ago the system of written statements was introduced, which, evidently, has proved far more effectual in the settlement of just

# CHAPTER VII

## FOLKLORE OF THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA <note>The story of Ammavaru <note>original: "Ammavāru"; a title meaning "The Mother," referring to a primordial goddess in the Telugu and Kannada-speaking regions, often associated with the creation of the world.</note> in this chapter is reprinted from an article in the *Nineteenth Century* magazine, by kind permission of the Editor.</note>

A FEW specimens of the folklore connected with the village deities <term>Known as *gramadevata*, these local gods and goddesses are believed to protect the village and oversee its moral and social laws, distinct from the major gods of the high Hindu tradition.</term> will serve to throw some light on the religious ideas of the people, the antiquity of the village deities themselves, the struggles that have taken place in former years between the worship of these primitive goddesses and the more modern cults of **Shiva** <note>The "Destroyer" and "Transformer" within the Hindu trinity.</note> and **Vishnu** <note>The "Preserver" and "Protector" within the Hindu trinity.</note>, and the efforts made in the later times to connect the simpler village deities with the more established gods and goddesses worshipped by the **Brahmans** <term>The priestly and scholarly class who oversee the rituals of the high Hindu tradition.</term>.

Many of the stories are wild and fantastic, marked by a thoroughly Indian extravagance and exaggeration; some seem to be faint echoes of actual events in the past; and many of the details were evidently invented to account for pieces of ritual, the meaning of which had been forgotten. Here is one which probably preserves the traditional story of some palace tragedy and the conversion of the victim into a local deity and also the memory of some attempt made to put down a primitive form of worship.

*Minakshiamman of Madura.* <term>The "Fish-Eyed Mother" of Madura; a powerful local deity who was eventually incorporated into mainstream Hinduism as the consort of the god Shiva.</term> In **Madura** during the time of the **Pandya dynasty** <note>A prominent Tamil dynasty that ruled parts of South India for centuries, known for their patronage of the arts and religion.</note>, there was a wicked irreligious king called **Pandian**. In his pride and presumption he closed the temple of **Minakshiamman**, <note>original: "Mīnākshī" (Sanskrit); literally "fish-eyed," an epithet of the wife of Shiva. This likely refers to eyes that are shaped like a fish—a mark of beauty—or eyes that never close, signifying the goddess’s constant watch over her devotees.</note> the renowned local goddess. She was enraged at this,

King Pandian <note>The ruler of the Pandya Kingdom, an ancient dynasty of South India centered in the city of Madurai.</note>, who greatly desired to have a child, one day found the deity incarnate as a little girl, lying in the palace, with a very curious bracelet <note>While the author uses "bracelet," this refers to the famous "anklet" of the Tamil epic *Silappatikaram*.</note> on her arm, which was the exact copy of one belonging to his wife. It happened that King Pandian's wife had lost her bracelet, which exactly resembled this one, a few days before this. So the merchant was arrested on the charge of stealing it, brought before the king and put to death. In a few days, his wife, Kannahai, went to Madura, heard what had happened, took the form of Thurgai¹ <note>original: "Thurgai"; a phonetic spelling of Durga.</note>, the demon-killing goddess, and slew Pandian. Since then she has been worshipped by the people. The slaughter of Pandian created in her a desire for bloodshed, and she is now a deity whom it is thought prudent to propitiate <term>To win the favor of a god or spirit through offerings and rituals to avoid their anger.</term>.

The daughter of the king fell in love with him. So Madurai-Vīran gave up his position and all his prospects of promotion and went off with the king's daughter. After their death both Madurai-Vīran and the king's daughter were deified <term>To be treated or worshipped as a god.</term> and worshipped. Madurai-Vīran is also known as Patinet- <note>The text cuts off; likely referring to "Patinettampadi Karuppu," a guardian deity associated with eighteen steps.</note>

...the Guardian of the Eighteen Steps <note>original: "tampadi Karuppan"; the text likely refers to *Pathinettaampadi Karuppan*, a specific form of the deity Karuppan worshipped at the entrance of the temple.</note>, because, in the courtyard of the Azhagirisami temple <note>Referring to the Alagar Koyil temple, one of the most significant Vaishnava shrines in Tamil Nadu, dedicated to the god Vishnu as Azhagar.</note>, which is one of the richest shrines in all India, there is a flight of eighteen steps, nine of which lead up to a platform on one side while nine lead down from it on the other. On the platform is a huge image of Karuppan, twenty feet high, with enormous eyes as big as umbrellas. The image is covered with spears, guns and arms, which people who have made vows come and offer to Karuppan. The room where the treasures of the temple are kept is locked up every night, and the key, instead of being taken away, is placed on the platform in front of the image. It seems an invitation to burglars; but nobody would ever dare to take the treasure which is guarded by Karuppan. 

It is said, in the folklore of the country, that some centuries ago eighteen magicians <note>original: "Māyāvīs"</note>, so called from the illusion <note>original: "māyā"; the philosophical concept that the world is a deceptive play or illusion created by the divine.</note> which they produce in the minds of people, came to the shrine of Azhagiri with the intention of carrying away the essence of the sanctity of the shrine and transporting it elsewhere. Their idea was to carry away the spiritual essence of the god in a wooden cylinder. The god the Beautiful One <note>original: "Azhagar"</note> became aware of the plot to carry away his essence, and so he entered into the body of a small boy, and by his mouth informed the king of the intended outrage and asked him to prevent it. He also told the king that the magicians would render themselves invisible by a black paste which they put on their foreheads. (This paste is generally made by a distillation of the head of a first-born child that has died, with some other ingredients. If, therefore, a first-born child dies, people generally bury it carefully, in the backyard of their houses, to prevent the head being taken away by magicians for this purpose.) The king consulted Rāmānuja <note>A famous 11th-century philosopher and social reformer who is highly revered in South Indian temple traditions.</note>, who was his family priest, and Rāmānuja advised him to shut the doors of the temple and then pour boiling rice-water into the courtyard so that the steam arising from it might melt the paste. One of the many stories current about <term>Mariamma</term> <note>The primary South Indian goddess of smallpox and other infectious diseases; she is both feared for bringing the illness and petitioned for its cure.</note>,

When she recovered, the people worshipped her as the goddess of smallpox, and hung up <term>margosa leaves</term> <note>Also known as neem leaves; they are prized for their medicinal and antiseptic properties and are traditionally used in rituals to ward off disease.</note> over their doors to keep the smallpox away.

Quite a different story about <term>Mariamma</term> <note>A prominent South Indian mother goddess associated with the weather, fertility, and infectious diseases like smallpox and cholera.</note> was given me by an Indian Christian, who was told it by his Hindu father. According to this legend, Mariamma was the mother of <term>Paraśurāma</term> <note>The sixth incarnation (avatar) of the god Vishnu, typically depicted as a warrior-sage carrying an axe.</note>, one of the incarnations of <term>Vishṇu</term> <note>One of the primary deities of Hinduism, known as the "Preserver."</note>, and wife of <term>Jamadagni</term> <note>A celebrated ancient sage and father of Paraśurāma.</note>, a famous <term>Ṛishi</term> <note>A divinely inspired poet, seer, or sage.</note> (Vedic seer). She was so chaste in mind that she could carry water in a mass without any vessel, and her wet cloth would fly up into the air and remain there till it was dry. One day, as she was coming home from bathing, some of the <term>Gandharvas</term> <note>Celestial musicians and singers in Hindu mythology, often associated with beauty and the arts.</note>, or heavenly singers, flew over her, and she saw their reflection in the ball of water in her hand. She could not help admiring their beauty; and, through this slight lapse from the perfect ideal of chastity, she lost her power, the water flowed down to the ground, and her cloth fell from the sky. So she arrived home with no water and with a wet cloth. 

The Ṛishi questioned her as to the meaning of this and she confessed her fault. Her stern husband ordered her son Paraśurāma to take her into the wilderness and cut off her head. So the son took his mother away, but when they came to the appointed place Mariamma met a <term>Pariah</term> <note>A historical term for people belonging to the lowest social strata or those outside the traditional caste system; today many prefer the term Dalit.</note> woman, and in her longing for sympathy embraced her in her arms. So Paraśurāma cut off both their heads together and went back in great sorrow. His father promised him any reward he chose to ask in return for his obedience: so Paraśurāma asked that his mother might be restored to life. 

The father granted his request and gave him some water in a vessel and a cane, telling him to put his mother’s head on her body, sprinkle the water on her, and tap her with the cane. In his eager haste he put his mother’s head on the body of the Pariah woman and vice versâ <note>Latin: "the other way around."</note>, and restored them both to life. The woman with the <term>Brāhman</term> <note>The highest social class in the traditional Hindu hierarchy, historically composed of priests and scholars.</note> head and Pariah body was afterwards worshipped as Mariamma; while the woman with the Pariah head and Brāhman body was worshipped as the goddess <term>Yellamma</term> <note>A popular goddess in the Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh regions, often considered a patron of the marginalized.</note>. To Yellamma

...buffaloes are sacrificed; but to <term>Mariamma</term> <note>A prominent South Indian mother goddess associated with the weather, fertility, and infectious diseases.</note> goats and cocks are offered, but not buffaloes.

The story is an interesting one, because it probably describes the fusion of the <term>Aryan</term> <note>Historically referring to the Indo-European speaking groups that migrated into northern India.</note> and <term>Dravidian</term> <note>The indigenous peoples of Southern India and their language family.</note> cults in the days when the Aryans first found their way into South India. A <term>Pariah</term> <note>A historical term for the "Outcaste" or Dalit communities; here it refers to the indigenous, non-Brahminical cultural elements.</note> body with a <term>Brāhman</term> <note>Or Brahmin; the priestly and scholarly caste, representing the highest social rank in the traditional hierarchy.</note> head is an apt description of the cult of <term>Śiva</term> <note>One of the principal deities of Hinduism, often associated with destruction and transformation.</note>, while a Pariah head with a Brāhman body might well describe some of the cults of the ancient Dravidian deities, modified by Brāhman ideas and influences. The fact that the deity to whom the buffalo is offered was the one with the Pariah head shows that the buffalo sacrifice was specially characteristic of the old Dravidian religion, and suggests that the buffalo was the <term>totem</term> <note>A sacred object or animal representing a specific clan or social group.</note> of the Pariahs.

## *The Buffalo-Sacrifice.*

Another quaint story, that is found all over the <term>Telugu</term> <note>A major Dravidian language spoken primarily in the south-eastern part of India.</note> country in various forms, attempts to account for the prominent part taken by the Pariahs in the worship offered to the village deities, and also to explain some strange features in the ritual. In ancient days, the story runs, there lived a <term>karnam</term> <note>original: "karnam"; a village official responsible for maintaining land records and accounts.</note>, that is, a village accountant, in a village to the east. He was blind, and had only one daughter. A Pariah, well versed in the <term>Vedas</term> <note>The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism.</note>, came to the village in the disguise of a Brāhman. The elders of the village were deceived and induced the blind <term>karnam</term> to give his daughter to him in marriage, so that he might succeed to the office of <term>karnam</term> in due time. The marriage was celebrated by Brāhman rites, and the <term>karnam</term>'s daughter bore sons and daughters to her Pariah husband, without any suspicion arising in her mind as to his origin. After a time a native of the Pariah's own village came to the place where they were living, and recognized the Pariah disguised as a Brāhman. Seeing, however, that he was a man of influence, he said nothing to the villagers, but went and told the Pariah's old mother. As he was her only son, the old woman set out in search of him, and came to the village where he lived, and sat down by the well used by caste people. The Pariah happened to go there, and recognized his mother; so he took her to a...

barber, had her head shaved <note>In traditional Brahminical culture of this period, a shaved head was a mandatory sign of widowhood.</note>, passed her off as a <term>Brāhman</term> <note>The highest priestly and scholarly caste in the traditional Hindu hierarchy.</note> widow, and brought her to his house, telling his wife that she was his mother and was dumb. One day the wife ordered a meal with a dish called <term>Savighai</term> <note>A traditional dish of wheat-flour filaments, similar to vermicelli.</note> (wheat flour baked with sugar and made into long strings) as a mark of respect to her mother-in-law. During the meal, the mother, forgetting the injunction of silence, asked her son what the <term>Savighai</term> was, saying it looked like the entrails of an animal <note>This comment betrays her identity, as Brahmins were strictly vegetarian, whereas the lower castes, to which she actually belonged, would be familiar with animal entrails.</note>. The wife overheard the remark, and her suspicions were aroused by the fact that her mother-in-law could speak, when her husband had said that she was dumb, and did not know a common <term>Brāhman</term> dish like <term>Savighai</term>; so she watched their conduct, and felt convinced that they belonged to a low caste, and were not <term>Brāhmans</term> at all.

Accordingly, she sent their children to school one day, when her husband was away from home, managed to get rid of the mother-in-law for a few hours, and then set fire to the house and burnt herself alive. By virtue of her great merit in thus expiating <note>To make amends or atone for a sin.</note> the sin she had involuntarily committed, she reappeared in the middle of the village in a divine form, declared that the villagers had done her great wrong by marrying her to a <term>Pariah</term> <note>A historical, now often derogatory, term for the "Outcaste" or Dalit communities.</note>, and that she would ruin them all. The villagers implored mercy in abject terror. She was appeased by their entreaties, consented to remain in the village as their village goddess, and commanded the villagers to worship her. When she was about to be burnt in the fire, she vowed that her husband should be brought before her and beheaded, that one of his legs should be cut off and put in his mouth, the fat of his stomach put on his head, and a lighted lamp placed on the top of it.

(These are details of the buffalo sacrifice, which has been already described, and this part of the story was evidently composed to explain the ritual, of which the true meaning had long been forgotten.) The villagers therefore seized the husband, stripped him naked, took him in procession round the village, beheaded him in

*A Tragic Tale.* <note>This story was originally published in the *Madras Mail*, a prominent English-language newspaper in British India.</note> Such ceremonies as the buffalo-sacrifice, gruesome as they seem, when witnessed in broad daylight, with the accompaniments of devil-music, bell-ringing and shouting, or rather shrieking, are much more awe-inspiring when seen at night, and are likely to impress a stranger in an unpleasant manner, as the following will show. A—— was a stranger to the country and its ways. He was returning home late one night, guided along his path by the uncertain rays of a young moon. Missing his way, he strayed towards the shrine of the village goddess; and when passing the low walls of the temple his attention was suddenly arrested by a heart-rending moan, seemingly uttered by some one in great distress, inside the walled enclosure. Impelled by thoughts of rendering help to a fellow creature in distress, A—— approached the temple wall, and looking over it, saw the prostrate form of a young and handsome female, of the better class of <term>Hindus</term> <note>In the context of this 19th/early 20th-century text, this likely refers to a woman of a higher-status caste.</note>, lying motionless as death on the stone pavement. Thoughts of dark intrigues and mysterious murders of a decidedly Eastern type <note>This reflects the typical "Orientalist" perspective of the era, where Western observers viewed Indian rituals as inherently mysterious or sinister.</note> impelled him to climb over the wall; and he was bending over the woman, his hand stretched out in the act of raising up what he believed was the lifeless remains of the victim of some ghastly

The figure held a naked sword in one hand and a bunch of margosa <note>The margosa or Neem tree (*Azadirachta indica*) is considered sacred in India, particularly in the worship of village goddesses; its leaves are used for purification and to ward off evil spirits.</note> leaves in the other, and bounding up to A——, peremptorily, and with a glance whose meaning could not be mistaken, motioned him away.

He was told that the woman was a matron of a respectable Hindu family, who, having had no children since her marriage, had come, by the advice of her elders, to invoke the assistance of the goddess, as she was credited with the power of making women fertile, and by prayers and offerings prevail on her to make her the mother of a son, and thus save her from the displeasure of her husband, who frequently rated <note>In this context, "rated" means to scold or berate someone angrily.</note> her on her barrenness. The grotesque figure which had so terrified A—— was the village <term>pūjārī</term> <note>A *pūjārī* is a priest who performs the rites and rituals (pūjā) in a temple; unlike Brahmin priests in large temples, village pūjārīs often come from non-Brahmin communities.</note>, and a noted exorciser of evil spirits; and he was then exercising his art over the terrified woman in attempting to drive away the malignant spirit that possessed her, and had thereby rendered her childless. It is said to be a common belief among many Hindus that barrenness in females is sometimes the result of possession by evil spirits, some of whom have to be propitiated <note>To propitiate is to win the favor of a spirit or deity through gifts or sacrifices.</note>, while others are terrified into leaving their victims. In this case it was a demon of the latter kind, and that accounted for the <term>pūjārī</term>’s appearance, in all the majesty and terror of his office as exorcist, sword in hand, to coerce the unwilling one to take his flight.

A— had seen Hindu superstition in all its nakedness, and the effect of it had been heightened by every circumstance that could make it awe-inspiring—the sombre shadows of night, the dim flickering of the temple light that threw a ray like a sanctuary lamp, the silence, except when broken by the woman's moans; all helped to impress him deeply.

Frequently, while worshipping at the shrine, it happens that one of the more spiritual of the worshippers becomes possessed of the goddess, and commences to execute the usual *ritual possession dance* <note>original: *devil dance*; a trance-like performance where the devotee is believed to embody the deity</note>, with dilated eyes, distended nostrils, and a frame suddenly endued with extraordinary activity and strength, proud to act as the mouthpiece of the goddess and to give out her oracles. It not seldom happens also that unscrupulous characters take advantage of this favor by the deity, to impose on the ignorant masses by practicing on their credulity. An example of the way in which the deity of an indigenous <note>original: *aboriginal*</note> family might become a deity of a conquering race and acquire a widespread popularity, is seen in the history of Koniamma in the Coimbatore district. The story goes that at a very remote date, when the tract now occupied by the town of Coimbatore was forest land, inhabited by indigenous hill-tribes known as the *Mountain Dwellers* <note>original: *Malaisar*</note>, *that is*, dwellers in the mountain, a certain man, named Koyan, who was of some repute among the indigenous people, dwelt there and worshipped a goddess who was called after his name, *Mother of Koyan* <note>original: *Koyanamma*</note>. After some years she became the village deity of the Malaisar <note>A hill-dwelling tribe of the Western Ghats in South India</note>, and a temple was built in her honour, with a stone image of the goddess in front of it. In the course of time, a Hindu king, named Mathe Raja, happened to go there on a hunting expedition, and, finding the spot very fertile, colonized the country with his own subjects. Gradually a flourishing town grew up, and Koniamma was adopted as one of the deities of the new colony. Centuries afterwards, Tippu Sultan, the "Tiger of Mysore" <note>The ruler of the Kingdom of Mysore (1782–1799) known for his fierce resistance against the British East India Company</note>, when he passed by the town during one of his marches, broke down the image and demolished the temple. The glory of persecution greatly increased the fame of the goddess. The head, which had been broken off the image, was brought back to the town, a new temple built, and in a few years the goddess became very popular over the whole district. Her title to divine honour rests upon the legend that she killed a certain demon, who was devastating the land and took the form of a buffalo when he attacked her. She is regarded as a benevolent being, who does not inflict diseases, but is capable of doing much good to the people when duly honoured. She is worshipped only at Coimbatore. This word is the English form of the Tamil *Koyamputhur*.

Some of the legends bear witness to the bitter conflict between the aboriginal inhabitants of the land, generally described as demons <note>original: *Rakshathas* (Sanskrit *Rakshases*); in Hindu mythology, these are often depicted as powerful, sometimes malevolent, supernatural beings or ogres</note> and the superior races which conquered them, whether Turanian or Aryan.

The legend of Śavadamına, the goddess of the weaver caste in the Coimbatore district, is a case in point. It runs as follows: Once upon a time, when there was fierce conflict between "the men" and the demons, "the men," who were getting defeated, applied for help to the god Śiva, who sent his wife, Pārvatī,¹ as a divine incarnation <note>original: *avatāra*</note> into the world to—

The <term>divine incarnation <note>original: *avatāra*</note>: the manifestation of a deity in earthly form</term> enabled them to defeat the <term>demons <note>original: *Rakshathas*</note>: a race of powerful and often malevolent supernatural beings in Hindu mythology</term>; and, as the weaver caste were in the forefront of the battle, she became the goddess of the weavers, and was known in consequence as Śavadamma, a corruption of *Sedar Amma* <gloss>Mother of the Weavers</gloss>, *Śedar* or *Chedar* being another title for the weavers. It is said that her original home was in the north of India near the Himalayas.

Another deity, whose worship is confined to a particular caste in South India, and about whom a similar legend is told, is Kanniha Parameśvarī <gloss>The Supreme Virgin Goddess</gloss>, the goddess of the *Komatis*, or traders. The story goes that in ancient days there was bitter hatred between the *Komatis*, who claim to belong to the <term>merchant <note>original: *Vaiśya*</note>: the third of the four traditional social classes in India, responsible for trade and agriculture</term> caste, and the <term>outsiders <note>original: *Mlecchas*</note>: a term used to describe non-Vedic or "barbarian" peoples</term>. When the *Komatis* were getting worsted in the struggle for supremacy, they requested Pārvatī, the wife of Śiva, to come and deliver them. It so happened that about that time Pārvatī was incarnate as a girl of the *Komati* caste, who was exceedingly beautiful. The *Mlecchas* demanded that she should be given in marriage to one of their own people, and the refusal of the *Komatis* led to severe fighting, in which the *Komatis*, owing to the presence of the <term>divine incarnation <note>original: *avatāra*</note></term> of Śiva among them, were completely victorious, and almost exterminated their enemies. After their victory, the *Komatis* entertained doubts as to the chastity of the girl, and compelled her to purify herself by passing through fire. This she did, and disappeared in the fire, resuming her real shape as Pārvatī, and taking her place beside Śiva in heaven. Her last words were a command to the *Komatis* to worship her, if they wished their caste to prosper.

It will be noticed from these stories that there has been a strong tendency in the Tamil country, where <term>priestly <note>original: *Brāhman*</note>: referring to the highest social class in the traditional Hindu system, typically scholars and priests</term> influence is strong, to connect the old village deities with the Hindu <term>pantheon: the collective group of recognized gods and goddesses</term>, and especially with the god Śiva, the most popular deity in South India. # THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

In the Tanjore district <note>now known as Thanjavur in Tamil Nadu</note>, the chief goddesses of the large tribe of village deities are seven sisters, who are regarded as emanating from Pārvatī <note>the mother goddess and consort of Śiva</note>, the wife of Śiva. <note>See p. 122 n.</note>

## *Revenge by Suicide.*

In the Warangal taluq <gloss>administrative subdivision</gloss> of the Hyderabad State there are numerous slabs of stone with figures of a man in the act of cutting his throat carved on them in bas-relief. The story goes that in ancient days a king of Warangal promised some Wudders <note>a caste of itinerant laborers and stone-cutters</note> a sum of gold for digging a large tank <note>a reservoir used for irrigation or drinking water</note>. When they appeared before him for payment, he offered them silver instead; and they went away very sad and angry, and came back again a few days afterwards and all cut their throats in the presence of the king, so that their spirits might haunt and torment him for the rest of his life. They have been worshipped from that day to this, and are among the most popular gods of the district. It was a truly Indian method of taking revenge, and I have often heard of similar acts of retaliation even in modern times.

## *Basavanna of the Badagas.*

The following stories, current among the Badagas <note>an agricultural community living in the mountains</note> on the Nilgiri Hills, in South India, may possibly preserve, in a perverted form, the memory of some trivial incidents, which the superstitious fancy of the villagers turned into signs and wonders. The village of Kateri is about ten miles from Ootacamund <note>a famous hill station now called Udhagamandalam</note>, and the Kateri falls have been utilized to generate the electric power that now works the Government cordite <note>a smokeless explosive used in ammunition</note> factory in the broad valley on the other side of the hills. 

But long before cordite or electric power were thought of, when the Muhammadans <note>Muslim rulers, likely referring to the Sultanate of Mysore under Hyder Ali or Tipu Sultan</note> ruled in Mysore, one of the villagers of Kateri went down to the plains to pay tribute. When he went to a river to perform *pūjā* <gloss>ritual worship</gloss> to a *liṅgam* <term>liṅgam: the sacred pillar-like emblem representing the god Śiva</term>, the emblem of the god Śiva, he found on the river bank a stone in the form of an ox <note>This represents Basava, or Nandi, the sacred bull and vehicle of Śiva</note>. He put it in his pocket, intending to give it to his children as a toy. But when he got home, he forgot all about the stone; and it remained in his pocket till he went down to another river near

...eri <note>continuing from "Kat-" on the previous page to form "Kateri," a village in the Nilgiri Hills</note> to perform <term>pūjā <note>a Hindu ritual of devotional worship and offerings</note></term> again. As he came to the bank, he touched his pocket and there found the stone. He took it out, put it down on the bank, and went to do his *pūjā*. When he came back, it was gone! This greatly astonished him. But when he returned to the river next morning, lo and behold! he saw on the bank the stone turned into a real live ox! Then the ox went off to a neighbouring village, Naduhatty, and there fought with another ox. The owner of this other ox killed the aggressor; but no sooner had he done so, than he turned upside down, and stood on his head with his heels in the air, unable to move. The villagers were filled with astonishment, as well they might be, at this extraordinary conduct; but the man who had found the stone told them that the slaughtered ox was really a god, which he had brought up from the plains, without knowing what it was, to give to his children. The villagers were in great alarm at this; but, when the man returned to his hut, there was the stone figure of the ox, with one of its horns broken and a spear-wound on its left side. The village <term>pūjārī <note>a priest who conducts the rites of a local temple</note></term> was hastily sent for, and he declared that a daily offering of milk must be made to the stone figure. For some time this was done; then the owner neglected the *pūjā*, and the stone promptly turned back into a live ox, which attacked the villagers, and would not let any one enter the shed where it stood. The villagers, however, made a hole in the roof, and poured milk upon it from above, and once more it turned into stone, and stands there in the same shed to this day. Warned by the experience of the past, the villagers were careful to make the daily offering of milk, lest it should once more turn into a troublesome ox. The name of the god is Basavanna.¹

The story reads like a description of a scene from a pantomime <note>a traditional British form of musical comedy theatrical production</note>, when the harlequin appears on the stage. But it is sober truth to the Badagas of Kateri and the neighbouring villages. It was told to me by the only

*Mahālinga of the Badagas.* Another story circulating among the Badaga people is similarly simple, and serves as an example of many local traditions found among them. As the story goes, a cow had a calf, but she would not give any milk to her master. Instead, she frequently ran off to a <term>shola <note>a type of high-altitude evergreen forest found in Southern India</note></term> near his house. One day, he followed her to see why she went there, and he witnessed her approach a stone image and pour milk over it from her udders. He then went to fetch a spade and tried to dig the image up, but he could not reach the bottom of it; furthermore, whenever his spade struck the stone, it began to bleed. He returned to the village to tell his story, and consequently, the villagers built a shrine over the image and began to worship it as the god Mahālinga.¹

*Hathay of Paraṅganad.* The tradition of the goddess Hathay (whose name means "grandmother") likely preserves the memory of a historical event, as the worship of men or women who have died violent or tragic deaths is common throughout South India. About a hundred years ago, a man had a daughter whom he wanted to marry to a man in the Paraṅganad district of the Nilgiri Hills. The girl refused, but her father insisted. At last, she went to the village <term>tank <note>a large, man-made pond or reservoir used for water storage</note></term> and sat under a tree; she bathed herself and then threw herself into the water and drowned. Later, one of the men in the Paraṅganad district saw the woman in a dream. She told him that she was not a human being but a goddess—an incarnation of Pārvatī, the wife of the god Śiva.

This story illustrates how many deities originate in India, and it also shows how these local goddesses are incorporated into the formal religion of the <term>Brāhmans <note>the priestly class of Hindu society</note></term> by being identified as the wives or incarnations of the wife of Śiva.

*Ammavaru, or Aṅkamma.* During one of my travels along the East Coast, north of Madras, I obtained a copy of a manuscript written on palm leaves that belonged to a village <term>pūjārī <note>a priest who performs the rites and offerings in a local temple</note></term>—

¹ <note>original: "I.e. Great Liṅga, the liṅga being Śiva’s phallic emblem."</note> This name means "Great Linga." The <term>linga</term> is a symbolic object representing the Hindu god Shiva.

<summary>This page details two Badaga legends—one concerning a bleeding stone image named Mahālinga and another about a woman named Hathay who became a goddess after a tragic suicide—highlighting how local folk figures are integrated into mainstream Hinduism.</summary>
