<meta>This page transitions from the local legends of the Badaga people to a significant myth involving the goddess Ammavaru (the Mother Goddess) and her role in the creation of the Hindu trinity. # FOLKLORE

...which contains the story of the village goddess Mother Goddess <note>original: "Ammavaru"</note>, now worshipped as <term>Aṅkamma <note>a fierce regional form of the Mother Goddess</note></term>. The story is recited by the <term>Asādis <note>a class of priestly heralds and musicians who preserve oral traditions</note></term> during the annual festivals. It is a strange, rambling tale, full of weird details, describing the birth of the newer deities, **Brahmā** (the Creator), **Śiva** (the Destroyer), and **Vishṇu** (the Preserver), and the struggle that ensued between the rival religions. It is not improbable that it describes an attempt on the part of the <term>Brāhmans <note>the priestly social class of India</note></term> to supplant the worship of the village deity by the new cults and the revival of the primitive religion through some epidemics or other disasters. A bad epidemic of smallpox or cholera, just at the time when the newer forms of worship had caused the old deities to be neglected, would be quite sufficient to revive their popularity and give rise to a fantastic myth describing the event.

The myth begins by describing the extreme antiquity of the Mother Goddess. “Even before the existence of the four ages <note>original: "Yugas"</note>, *i.e.* ages, before the birth of the nine priests, when sleep did not exist in towns and villages, when the ages had no time, before the birth of the Great God <note>original: "Maheśvara", a title of Śiva</note>, before the appearance of sky and lightning, before the birth of Gautama Buddha and the sages, before the appearance of the Ocean of Truth <note>original: "Satyasāgara"</note>, before the appearance of water reservoirs, such as tanks and lakes, when there were no roads, streets or lanes to towns and villages, before the creation of the world, even before the coming into existence of wells to be defiled by the spittle of fishes, and before the Goddess of Human Sacrifice <note>original: "Narayāga Ammavaru"</note> came into existence, three eggs were laid by the Mother Goddess in the sea of milk, one by one in three successive ages. The egg laid first got spoilt, the next filled with air, and only the third was hatched. This egg had three compartments, from which came the Triple Form <note>original: "Trimūrti"</note>—**Brahmā**, **Vishṇu**, and **Śiva**. The lower half of the egg was transformed into the earth and the upper half became the sky. The king, who was the divine incarnation <note>original: "avatāra"</note>,

---

² Human Sacrifice <note>original: "Narayāga"</note> is the term used for human sacrifice; "Goddess of Human Sacrifice" is the goddess worshipped by human sacrifice.
³ See above, p. 24 n. <note>This refers to an earlier explanation of the Hindu Trinity (Trimūrti).</note>

<meta>This page continues the mythological narrative of the Mother Goddess Ammavaru, following the description of her ancient origins. ->THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA<-

"...*that is* <note>original Latin: "i.e." (id est)</note> incarnation, of **Vishṇu** <note>the Preserver god in the Hindu trinity</note>, was fed on butter; **Brahmā** <note>the Creator god</note> was made to live on turmeric; and **Śiva** <note>the Destroyer god</note> was fed with the milk of **Ammavaru** <note>the Mother Goddess</note>. Then, as they grew up, she made each of the gods put on his forehead characteristic religious marks, and finally built three towns, one for each to live in, and a fourth for herself."

This probably preserves a tradition of the relation of the popular Hindu religion of modern days to the older worship of the village deities. It is doubtless true that the <term>Brāhmans <note>the priestly social class of India</note></term> gained the victory over their enemies the **Buddhists** by borrowing largely from the pre-Aryan religions, which had a great hold over the masses of the people. This may be practically expressed by saying that **Brahmā**, **Vishṇu**, and **Śiva** sprang from an egg laid by a village deity, and that she built for them the sacred cities which were the centres of their worship.

The goddess took special pains to protect her own city. She enclosed it with walls of bronze, brass, and gold; posted at the gates several thousand spirits of various sorts, and among them, a barber, a washer-man, and a potter. It seems odd to find these humble members of village society in such exalted company; but it is explained by the fact that they are the people who in many parts of South India take a prominent part in the sacrifices offered to the village deities at the annual festivals.

After a time, **Ammavaru** heard that the three kings, **Brahmā**, **Vishṇu**, and **Śiva**, were neglecting her worship; so she determined to exhibit her power by destroying their towns. Her resolve was strengthened by an insult offered her by **Śiva**. The god one day called his servant and asked him why the people were neglecting the worship of **Ammavaru**, and was told in reply that they were calling on his name instead. He then bade his servant go to **Ammavaru's** town and abuse her, which he did with a will. When she heard of it she smiled grimly, "trimmed her moustaches," <note>In South Indian folk art, fierce goddesses are often depicted with moustaches to represent their masculine strength and warrior power</note> and waxed very wroth. She then dressed herself up in a yellow cloth and yellow bodice, put on copper jewels, a...

Drums were sounded during the march. The investment of the town was a quaint proceeding. Besides several kinds of animals, Ammavaru created **Gañgā-bhavanī** (a fortified place with a ditch round it) and a sage to conduct the siege. The military operations of the sage were truly original. Seven **sacred berries** <note>original Sanskrit: *rudrāksha*; the berry of the *Elaeocarpus Ganitrus* used for making rosaries for the devotees of Śiva</note> were placed on the ground, and on these seven **eye-beads** <note>original Sanskrit: *bhadrakshīs*</note>, **that is** <note>original Latin: *i.e.* (id est)</note> a kind of bead in which are marks said to resemble eyes, and on these needles were stuck to support balls of sacred ashes. <note>Śiva is usually represented as covered with sacred ashes, and his followers usually smear their bodies in the same way.</note> Through these balls were driven steel spikes which supported a single-headed sacred berry, with seeds of a sacred plant on the top. The sage then put his head on the seeds and raised his legs high up in the air. but apparently they were successful, as Ammavaru moved steadily on, and appointed her sister to keep people off the road, and then placed her sisters, the hundred **śaktis**,¹ <note>Divine feminine powers or energies, often personified as goddesses or sisters of the supreme Mother Goddess.</note> to keep watch, and also a twelve-headed snake which coiled its body all round the town, keeping its hooded heads just opposite the gate and emitting poisonous fumes from its mouths. A horse was then brought her, drums were beaten, what Shakespeare would call **alarums and excursions** <note>A stage direction from Elizabethan drama meaning the sounds of battle and soldiers running across the stage.</note> took place, and the sky was turned into a pestle and the earth into a mortar. <note>This metaphor suggests a total upheaval of the universe, as if the world were being ground down and reshaped.</note> After this general upset of the universe, Ammavaru made the dumb to sing her praises, created some tents with little demons inside who did **pūjā** <note>original Sanskrit: *pūjā*; Hindu ritual worship involving offerings to a deity.</note> to her, and so finally arrived at **Devagiri**. <note>The "Mountain of the Gods," identified in this text as the city of the god Śiva.</note> Apparently this overwhelming display of military power and science at first crushed all resistance. The heads of the kings (**Brahmā**, **Vishṇu**, and **Śiva**) <note>The Hindu Trinity: the Creator, the Preserver, and the Destroyer.</note> who refused to worship Ammavaru were cut off, also the heads of seven other kings, and then all put on again! One king’s throne was made red-hot like the fire in a potter’s kiln, and his hair made all bloody, while demons were set to watch the corpses of the slain. Then Ammavaru afflicted the unhappy citizens with many disasters and started off to attack four other kings. Drums were sounded as before and then a bloody battle ensued outside the walls of Devagiri. Horses and elephants were slain by Ammavaru, one king “felt a bad pain in his chest, as if pierced with arrows, and pains in various parts of his body,” and died. Another king took a sword and plunged it into the body of a third king, and both died. Then all the horses and elephants and kings died, and finally Ammavaru brought them all to life again, and they all began to worship her. **Śiva** grew very angry, abused **Brahmā** <note>The Hindu god of creation.</note>, and bit off one of his heads. The head fastened on **Śiva’s** hand and remained immovable. So he sent at once for a number of **Brāhmans** <note>Members of the priestly and scholarly caste.</note>, and asked why he could not get it off. They told him that it was because he had committed murder, which is a most heinous crime, and suggested that he should wander about as a beggar, and make pilgrimage to **Benares** <note>Modern-day Varanasi, the holiest city in Hinduism.</note>, **Rāmeśvaram**, and other sacred places, and then receive alms directly from the hands of **Lakshmī** (the wife of **Vishṇu** <note>The Hindu god who preserves and protects the universe.</note>). **Śiva** then disguised himself as a beggar, and wandered far and wide, and at last came to **Lakshmī**, and cried out, “O **Original Lakshmī**! <note>original: "Ādi Lakshmī." Alms!” She ordered her servants to take him alms, but he refused to receive it except at her hands, and said that **Lakshmī** was his sister. This is a ritual where lighted lamps are waved before a deity or person to ward off evil or show honor; see page 39.</note> before **Śiva**, and gave curry and rice to the skull, which promptly fell towards the north and broke in five pieces, murmuring, as it broke, that something must be done for it. **Śiva** replied that it might take hold of pregnant women, women during confinement, and babies, and that this would enable it to obtain worship and offerings. <note>This myth explains the origins of certain spirits believed to cause illness or complications during childbirth, which must be appeased with offerings.</note>

**Ammavaru** <note>The local village mother goddess.</note> then related how she herself had desired marriage and gone to **Vishṇu**, who sent her to **Brahmā**, who passed her on to **Śiva**. She danced before **Śiva**, who promised to grant her wish, if she would give him the three valuable things she possessed—a rug, some betel leaves and a third eye. She gave them all to **Śiva**, who at once opened the third eye and reduced her to ashes. <note>Śiva is traditionally depicted with a third eye set vertically in his forehead. After telling this moving story, **Ammavaru** <note>A title meaning "Mother," used for the principal village goddess in Telugu-speaking regions.</note> demanded entrance into the town, when she transformed herself into a parrot and sat on a stone pillar. Many of the inhabitants she caused to faint; on many others she sent fevers and other diseases. Then she flew to the **gopuram** <note>The ornate, towered gateway of a South Indian temple.</note>, *i.e.* the towered gateway of the temple, where nine men were worshipping **Śiva** with his emblem <note>The lingam, a symbol representing the energy and potential of the god Śiva.</note> in their hands. Suddenly the emblems became red-hot in their hands, and, dropping them, the nine men cried out, “O **Śiva**, you are powerless to-day; now we have lost faith in you. Before the moon rises, may your temple be burnt to ashes.” **Śiva**, hearing their cries, came up and threw some sacred ashes over them and touched them with his cane. Then they all got up and said to him, “O **Iśvara** <note>original: "Īśvara," meaning "Lord" or "Supreme Ruler."</note> (*i.e.* Lord), listen to our complaints. We have had enough of your **pūjā** <note>The act of ritual worship or offering.</note>. Some calamity has befallen us. Give us leave and we will go to our homes.” **Śiva** went off in anger to the gate-keepers and demanded why they had admitted strangers. They replied that they had turned back an old woman selling fruit, and only admitted a **Liṅgāyat** <note>A member of a religious sect that worships Śiva exclusively and wears a small lingam in a casket around the neck.</note> woman, because she was a devotee. **Śiva** ordered one of the demons to find her; but **Ammavaru** transformed herself into a girl of the **Velama** caste <note>An influential agricultural and land-owning community in Andhra Pradesh.</note>, and mixed with the **Velama** women in the **Brāhman** <note>The priestly and scholarly social class.</note> street, and the man looked for her in vain. Then another was ordered to find her; but this time **Ammavaru** turned herself into a parrot. When the man could not find her, he cried out, “O goddess! Please come! You are the deity of my ancestors. We hear that you have entered our town in the form of a **Liṅgāyat**.” Then **Ammavaru** asked him what kind of form he meant, saying, “I am your emblem of life.” Then the demon felt bad pains all over his body, as though his chest and ribs were broken, rose up high into the air, flapping his hands like wings, caught hold of the parrot and brought her to **Śiva**.

**Śiva** complimented the demon on his success, but said that a female deity should not be brought into his presence. He commanded her to be tied to a red-hot pillar of glass and crows with iron beaks to peck at her. But their uplifted arms remained fixed in the air and they could not move them. **Śiva** then ordered **Ammavaru** to be tied to the feet of an elephant and dragged through the streets of the town; but as soon as she was tied to his feet, the elephant became stiff and stood motionless as a pillar. Then **Śiva** said that she must be thrown on to a frying-pan and fried like grain; so they took her up and threw her on to red-hot plates of glass, which at once became cool as water. **Ammavaru** grew wild with anger at this treatment, and, whirling round and round, became huge as a mountain, and then once more turned into a parrot, and addressed **Śiva** thus: "O **Śiva**! Then **Ammavaru** in her wrath threatened to destroy the town. **Śiva** declared that under no circumstances should she be worshipped as a goddess, and that she might do her worst. Then **Ammavaru** did her worst and greatly troubled the people. From east to west crows flew over the town, in vast flocks. A strong wind arose, and there was a storm of rain that lasted seven <term>gadiyas</term> <note>A traditional Indian unit of time; one gadi is 24 minutes.</note>. The people caught colds, coughs, and fevers; small-pox and other epidemics spread rapidly; horses, elephants, and camels were afflicted with disease; pregnant women suffered severe pains; babies could not take their mothers’ milk. For these seven <term>gadiyas</term> the town suffered terribly. The god threw some sacred ashes on the car, touched it with his cane, and all the stalks became living men, chanting "**Hara, Hara**,"¹ <note>A ritual chant; "Hara" is a name for Śiva.</note> *that is, Destroyer.* When they asked for food, they were told that they might wander over the country, and would then get food in the shape of offerings and sacrifices. **Ammavaru** then went off with all her drums and instruments to **Kunthalasaman**, the town of **Brahmā** <note>The creator god in the Hindu trinity.</note>, where she hoped to find three kings worshipping her. Then the nine kings came to the town of **Aṅkalathavatha** (another name for **Ammavaru**) riding on clouds, to steal flowers from her garden. As they were plucking the flowers, **Ammavaru** seized them, took them off to an open space, where she had erected stables of gold, silver and diamonds, and impaled them in such a way that their blood could not curdle and no flies could touch them. She then placed her steed, the jackal, to guard the corpses, and then vanquished her enemies.

I have given the story almost exactly as it is told in the palm-leaf manuscript that was lent me to have copied. It is a weird rambling piece of mythology; but its interest lies in the light that it throws upon an obscure page in the history of religious life in India. # CHAPTER VIII

## PROBABLE ORIGIN OF THE WORSHIP OF VILLAGE GODS

The account given above of the rites and ceremonies connected with the worship of the **village deities** <note>Local guardian spirits, often female, who protect a specific community, as opposed to the "high" gods of the pan-Indian pantheon.</note> in South India does not pretend to be an exhaustive one. It would require many bulky volumes to enumerate the countless varieties of local use and custom prevailing in the different villages, and the result would be wearisome in the extreme; but enough has been said, I think, to give a fair idea of the general nature and character of this phase of **Hinduism**, and to form a basis of comparison, on the one hand, between the cult of the village deities and the **Brahmanical** <note>The orthodox, priestly tradition of Hinduism based on the Vedas and led by the Brahman caste.</note> cults of **Vishnu** <note>One of the principal deities of Hinduism, known as the Preserver.</note> and **Siva** <note>One of the principal deities of Hinduism, known as the Destroyer or Transformer.</note>, and, on the other hand, between the cults of village deities existing among the **Telugus**, **Canarese** <note>Modernly referred to as Kannada-speaking people.</note>, and **Tamils**; and, at any rate, this brief sketch of the religion of about 80 per cent. of the Hindu population of South India may serve to dispel the idea that the people of India are, as a body, a race of philosophers, or that what is vaguely termed Hinduism is a system of refined philosophy in the purity of its morality and subtlety of its doctrines. Religious philosophy, undoubtedly, has played a great part in the development of the higher thought of the Indian people; but in South India, at any rate, the outlook of about 80 per cent. of the population on the visible world in which they live, and the invisible world which borders closely upon it, and their ideas about God and religion are represented, not by Hindu philosophy, but by the worship of their **village gods** <note>Original: *grāma-devatās*. From the Sanskrit *grāma* (village) and *devatā* (deity).</note>.

Considerable caution must be used in drawing con-

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

[con]clusions from the striking resemblances between the ceremonies observed in the worship of **village deities** among the **Telugus** <note>People who speak the Telugu language, primarily from the region of modern-day Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.</note>, **Canarese** <note>Modernly referred to as Kannada-speaking people from the Karnataka region.</note>, and **Tamils** <note>People from the southernmost part of India who speak the Tamil language.</note>, as the value of all evidence of this kind is largely discounted by the unifying influence of the great **Vijayanagar empire** <note>A powerful South Indian empire (1336–1646) that served as a major center for Hindu culture, art, and resistance against northern invasions.</note>. For about 250 years, from A. D. 1326 to A. D. 1565, the whole of South India was united under this great empire, which had its capital on the **Tungabhadra River**, and formed the main bulwark of **Hinduism** against the advance of the **Muhammadans** <note>A historical term used by the author to refer to Muslims.</note>. The capital itself was of vast extent, and gathered together men and women of all races from every part of South India. It must have formed, therefore, a great centre for the fusion of different ideas and customs; and, when the City of Vijayanagar was captured and rased to the ground by the Muhammadans in A. D. 1565, Tamils, Telugus, and Canarese may well have carried home with them many new ideas and customs borrowed from one another. 

We cannot assume, therefore, that, because a custom is widespread in the Tamil, Telugu, or Canarese country now, it was necessarily widespread before the foundation of the Vijayanagar empire. Allowing, however, for this possible borrowing of religious rites and ceremonies, the resemblances between the rites in all three countries are very striking. Such a curious ceremony as that of cutting off the right fore-leg and putting it into the mouth of the victim <note>This refers to a specific animal sacrifice ritual where a goat or buffalo's leg is placed in its own mouth, a common feature in several South Indian village festivals.</note>, which is found to exist all over the three countries in various villages and towns, might possibly have been borrowed; but the general resemblance in type, which underlies all local differences of custom, can hardly have been due to this cause, and the general impression left by a study of the various festivals and sacrifices in the three countries would be, I think, that they all belong to a common system and had a common origin.

In the same way caution is needed in drawing conclusions from the resemblances between the worship of the village deities and the **Brāhmanical cults** <note>The formal, priestly traditions of Hinduism led by the Brahmin caste, often focused on the Sanskrit scriptures known as the Vedas.</note> of **Vishnu** <note>One of the primary deities of Hinduism, worshipped as the supreme protector and preserver of the universe.</note> and **Śiva** <note>One of the primary deities of Hinduism, worshipped as the destroyer of evil and the transformer.</note>. The two systems of religion have existed side by side in the towns and villages for many centuries,

and the same people have largely taken part in both. Naturally, therefore, they have borrowed freely from one another. In the **Tamil** <note>The region of Southeast India where the Tamil language is spoken, largely corresponding to the modern state of Tamil Nadu.</note> country the influence of **Brahmanism** <term>Brahmanism</term> <note>original: "Brāhmanism"; an early form of Hinduism characterized by the religious and social supremacy of the Brahmin priestly class and adherence to the Vedas.</note> on the cult of the village deities is very noticeable, and it is more than probable that many ceremonies, which originally belonged to the village deities, have been adopted by the **Brahman** <note>The highest-ranking social class in the Hindu varna system, traditionally serving as priests and scholars.</note> priests. 

No conclusions, therefore, can safely be drawn from the folklore, which represents various village goddesses as, in some way, connected with **Shiva** <note>original: "Śiva"; one of the principal deities of Hinduism, often regarded as the destroyer of evil and the transformer within the Trimurti (the Great Trinity).</note>. It is quite possible that stories of this kind are simply due to a desire to connect the less dignified village deities with what was regarded as the higher form of worship controlled by the Brahmans. 

On the other hand, the points of difference between the worship of the village deities and that of **Shiva** and **Vishnu** <note>original: "Vishṇu"; a principal Hindu deity known as the "Preserver" or "Protector" of the universe.</note>, which have been noted in the introduction, are very strongly marked, and clearly indicate that the two systems of religion are quite distinct. The village goddesses are purely local deities, inflicting or warding off diseases and other calamities. They seem never to be regarded as having any relation to the world as a whole, and their worship is the religion of ignorant and uncivilized people, whose thoughts do not travel beyond their own surroundings and personal needs; while **Shiva** and **Vishnu** represent a philosophic conception of great forces at work in the universe, forces of destruction and preservation, and their worship is a religion that could only have originated among men accustomed to philosophic speculation. 

They may have borrowed many ideas, customs, and ceremonies from the more primitive religion of the villages; but the foundation and motive of the whole system are to be sought in the brain of the philosopher rather than in the fears and superstitions of uneducated villagers. At the same time, it is also true that morally the Brahmanical system has sunk to lower depths than have been reached by the cruder religion of the village people. The worship of the village deities contains much that is physically repulsive. The details of a **buffalo sacrifice** <note>A common ritual in South Indian village religion where a buffalo is offered to a goddess to appease her anger or seek protection for the community.</note>

are horrid to read about, and still worse to witness, and the sight of a **priest** <note>original: "pūjārī"; a local temple priest or ritual leader who performs the offerings.</note> parading the streets with the entrails of a lamb round his neck and its liver in his mouth would be to us disgusting; and, doubtless, there are much drunkenness and immorality connected with the village festivals; while the whole system of religion is prompted by fear and superstition, and seems almost entirely lacking in anything like a sense of sin or feelings of gratitude towards a higher spiritual Power. But still, it is also true that, setting aside a few local customs in the worship of the village deities, there is nothing in the system itself which is quite so morally degrading and repulsive as the **worship of the phallic symbol** <note>original: "Lingam worship"; the veneration of the linga, an abstract representation of the god Shiva. The author is referring to the historical Devadasi system, where women were dedicated to temple service.</note>. If the worship of **Shiva** and **Vishnu** has risen to greater heights, it has also sunk to lower moral depths than the less intellectual and less aesthetic worship of the **village deities** <note>original: "grāma-devatās"; from the Sanskrit "grama" (village) and "devata" (deity).</note>.

What the origin of the village deities and their worship may have been, it is difficult to say. The system, as it now exists, combines many different ideas and customs, and has probably resulted from the fusion of various forms of religion. In the **Tamil** <note>A linguistic and cultural region in Southeast India, primarily the modern state of Tamil Nadu.</note> country there are many features in the worship of the village deities, which, obviously, have been adopted from **Brahmanical Hinduism** <term>Brahmanism</term> <note>original: "Brāhmanism"; the early religious system of India led by the Brahmin priestly class, which later evolved into modern Hinduism.</note>, **for example** <note>original: "e.g."</note> the elaborate washing of the images, and the growing aversion to animal sacrifices. So in **Mysore** <note>A region and former princely state in South-Central India, now part of Karnataka.</note>, there are traces of sun-worship in the cult of **Bisal-Mari** <note>In the local Kannada language, "Bisal" means the heat of the sun, and "Mari" is a common name for a mother goddess.</note>; and there are many features in the system everywhere, which seem to be borrowed from the worship, or rather propitiation, of the spirits of the departed. But the system as a whole is redolent of the soil, and evidently belongs to a pastoral and agricultural community. The village is the centre round which the system revolves, and the protection of the villagers the object for which it exists. At the same time, it is quite possible that the ultimate origin of many of the rites and ceremonies may be traced further back to a nomadic stage of society. Most of them have now entirely lost

their meaning, and, when the people are asked what a particular ceremony means or what its object is, their usual reply is simply "It is **custom**" <note>original: "mamul"; a common term across South Asia for established tradition or precedent.</note>; and there are many details of the sacrifices, which seem strangely inconsistent with the general idea and theory of the worship which now prevails. The one object of all the worship and sacrifices now is to propitiate <note>To win or regain the favor of a god or spirit; to appease.</note> various spirits, good and evil. And this is done by means of gifts, which, it is supposed, the spirits like, or by ceremonies, which will please them. Some of the spirits are supposed to delight in bloodshed, so animals are killed in their presence, and sometimes even the blood is given them to drink; or blood and rice are sprinkled over the fields and streets, or thrown up in the air for them to eat. To the less refined goddesses or to the coarser male attendants, like <term>Madurai-Viran <note>A popular Tamil folk deity, often honored as a heroic protector-warrior.</note></term>, <term>arrack <note>A potent distilled alcoholic spirit common in South and Southeast Asia.</note></term>, <term>toddy <note>A fermented palm wine.</note></term>, and <term>cheroots <note>A type of cigar with both ends clipped square.</note></term> are freely offered, because it is assumed that these gifts will rejoice their hearts and propitiate them. 

But a great deal of the ritual and many of the most striking ceremonies are quite inconsistent with this gift-theory of sacrifice and the idea of propitiation, which is now assumed to be the one motive and purpose of the festivals. For instance, one of the main features of the animal sacrifices is the varied applications of the blood of the victims. Sometimes the blood is applied to the bodies of the worshippers themselves, to their foreheads and breasts; sometimes it is sprinkled on the lintel and door-posts of the shrine, sometimes on the houses or cattle, sometimes on the boundary-stones, sometimes it is mixed with rice and scattered over the streets, or sprinkled all round the boundaries of the village lands. But what possible meaning could these various uses of the blood have according to the gift-theory of sacrifice? On this theory it would be intelligible why it should be presented, as is sometimes done, at the shrine of the deity, or even drunk, as at <term>Trichinopoly <note>A major historical city in Tamil Nadu, now known as Tiruchirappalli.</note></term>, by the **priest** <note>original: "pūjārī"; a non-Brahmin temple priest who performs rituals and offerings.</note>, who represents the goddess; but of these other uses of the blood the gift-theory seems to furnish no adequate explanation. theory suggest for the widespread custom of putting the entrails round the neck of the <term>pūjārī <note>A priest or ritual leader who performs the service and makes offerings to the deity.</note></term> and the liver in his mouth? It is not probable that such a custom as this originated without some reason or idea at the back of it; but on the gift-theory it seems absolutely meaningless.

Or again, another leading feature of the worship is the sacrificial feast in various forms. Sometimes the feast takes place on the spot, in the compound of the shrine; more often the carcass is taken home by the offerer for a feast in his own house. Sometimes it is a formal and ceremonious act, as in certain villages of the Telugu country, where five little <term>Mālā <note>A community in South India, specifically in the Telugu-speaking regions, who often hold significant roles in village ritual life.</note></term> boys, called <term>Siddhalu <note>Meaning "perfected ones" or "innocents"; young boys selected for ritual purity.</note></term>, or innocents, are fed with the flesh of the victim under cover of a large cloth, to keep off evil spirits or the evil eye. Here, again, the gift-theory seems quite inconsistent with the whole idea of the sacrificial feast. The explanation often given, that the goddess consumes the essence or spirit (<term>Saram <note>Original Sanskrit/Telugu: "essence" or "strength".</note></term> or <term>Avi <note>Original Sanskrit: "vapor" or "spirit-offering".</note></term>) of the gifts, while the worshippers take the material substance, is perhaps in accordance with the animistic idea found in other countries that, even for men, the important thing in their food is the soul-stuff it contains rather than the outward, material part of it. But in any case this would still leave unexplained the fact that the eating of the flesh by the worshippers is in many cases regarded as a religious act and as an important part of the sacrifice, like the feast on the victims offered in the peace offerings under the Jewish law. On the other hand, the sacrificial feast finds a natural and ready explanation, if we assume that the system originated in the desire for communion with the spirit world and not in the idea of propitiation <note>The act of winning the favor of or appeasing a deity through a gift.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 144 regarding the "animistic idea" that the essence of food is what truly matters. The text completes the thought from the previous page, transitioning from the concept of a "soul" to the broader theory of "soul-stuff" as defined by contemporary ethnologists.</meta>

### PROBABLE ORIGIN OF THE WORSHIP

lifeless things share in a universal soul or a <term>soul-stuff <note>Original German: *Seelenstoff*. A concept in early 20th-century anthropology suggesting that a physical-spiritual substance pervades all things and can be transferred through contact or consumption.</note></term> that pervades everything in the world. "The vital question for the Animist," he says, "is how to place his own soul in relation to the souls surrounding him, and to their powers, which are partly injurious and partly useful, with as little danger and as much advantage to himself as possible. What must I do to protect and enrich my soul? That is the cardinal question of the animistic catechism." The main object of eating the flesh of an animal, therefore, is to absorb this soul-stuff and appropriate the special virtue which belongs to the animal. "The flesh of an animal that is eaten produces an effect on man corresponding to the animal in question. The flesh of the stag gives nimbleness. Gamecocks are made to devour centipedes in order to assimilate their fierceness. Javanese thieves carry with them crow-bones to be as clever at stealing as crows." And Mr. Warneck <note>Johannes Warneck (1867–1944) was a German missionary and scholar who wrote extensively on the Batak people of Sumatra; his work was central to the study of "primitive" religions at the time.</note> is probably right in thinking that this is the explanation of Cannibalism. It is not an act of ferocity or revenge, still less of epicureanism, since the <term>Battaks <note>Now spelled Batak; an ethnic group from the North Sumatra highlands of Indonesia.</note></term> dislike human flesh so much that it nearly makes them sick; but "it is supposed that in eating a man's flesh the eater appropriates the other's soul." And in accordance with this idea those parts of the body in which the soul-power is supposed to be concentrated, the liver, the palms of the hands, the sinews and the flesh of the head are specially prized. To the same idea we may trace the horrid custom of drinking the blood of victims offered in sacrifice, which is so common in South India, and the various uses of the blood described in Chapter III. "The soul-stuff," says Mr. Warneck, "has special vigour in the blood," and it is repeatedly stated in the Jewish law with reference to the sacrificial victims that "the blood is the life." <note>A reference to the Hebrew Bible, specifically Deuteronomy 12:23—*ki hadam hu hanefesh*—which forbids the consumption of blood because it is viewed as the seat of the living soul.</note>

It can readily be seen how easily in primitive times these animistic ideas gave rise to that particular form of Animism, which is generally known as <term>Totemism <note>A system of belief in which a group of people has a spiritual connection or kinship with a particular animal or plant, often serving as their emblem or ancestor.</note></term>. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the communal nature of early societies. While the previous page focused on the consumption of "soul-stuff" to gain power, this page shifts to the social mechanism of the "clan" and how ritual blood exchange allows these groups to expand their kinship beyond birth-ties.</meta>

-># THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA<-

any rate, are assumed to be united by ties of blood relationship. All the members of the clan, then, are blood relations, and are bound together, as members of one family, for mutual help and protection. The normal attitude of every clan towards other clans is one of suspicion, hostility and war, and this constant pressure of hostile clans compels each individual clan, not only to maintain its unity and brotherhood, but, if possible, to enlarge its limits and add to its numbers. It becomes possible to do this by a convenient extension of the idea of blood relationship. If a man is not one of the clan by birth, he can be made one by, in some way, being made a partaker of its blood. In his *Introduction to the History of Religion* <note>Frank Byron Jevons (1858–1936) was a British scholar of the history of religion; this 1896 work was a standard text in early comparative religion.</note>, Mr. Jevons quotes several instances of this from different parts of the world, in both ancient and modern times. The following examples from Africa will suffice to illustrate the custom:—

> “The exchange of blood is often practised amongst the blacks of Africa, as a token of alliance and friendship. The Mambettu <note>An ethnic group from the northeastern Democratic Republic of the Congo.</note> people, after having inflicted small wounds upon each others’ arms, reciprocally suck the blood, which flows from the incision. In the Unyora <note>Also known as Bunyoro, a kingdom in Western Uganda.</note> country the parties dip two coffee berries into the blood, and eat them. Amongst the Sandeh <note>The Azande people of North Central Africa.</note> the proceedings are not so repulsive; the operator, armed with two short knives, inoculates the blood of one person into the wound of another. The exact manner in which this last operation is performed is described by Mr. Ward <note>Herbert Ward (1863–1919) was a British explorer and artist who traveled extensively in the Congo region.</note>, who himself submitted to it.” After noting that <term>blood brotherhood <note>A ritual where two people intermingle their blood to create a legal and spiritual bond identical to that of siblings by birth.</note></term> is a form of cementing friendship and a guarantee of good faith, popular with all the Upper Congo tribes, he proceeds: “An incision was made in both our right arms, in the outer muscular swelling just below the elbow, and as the blood flowed in a tiny stream, the <term>charm-doctor <note>A traditional ritual specialist or healer, often referred to in historical texts as a "medicine man."</note></term> sprinkled powdered chalk and potash on the wounds, delivering the while, in rapid tones, an appeal to us to maintain unbroken the sanctity of the contract, and then our arms being rubbed together, so that the flowing blood intermingled, we were declared to be brothers of one blood, whose interest henceforth should be united as our blood now was.”

These examples will suffice to illustrate the wide-spread idea that the actual drinking or application of the blood of a clan will create a blood-relationship and alliance among men, who are not actually members of

...the same family. But the human clan in its struggle for existence found itself surrounded, not only by other human clans, but also by various tribes of animals, which it looked upon as analogous to the clans of men; and it desired to strengthen its position by an alliance with one or another of these animal clans, which, for some reason, impressed itself upon its imagination as animated by some supernatural power. The animal clan then became what is now called the <term>totem</term> <note>The term "totem" is derived from the Ojibwe word *doodem*. In the late 19th century, scholars believed that "totemism"—the spiritual connection between a group and an animal or plant—was a universal stage in the evolution of religion.</note> of the human clan; and the spirit that was supposed to animate the totem clan became, in a certain sense, an object of worship. One great purpose of the <term>sacrifice</term> <note>Derived from the Latin *sacrificium* (to make sacred); here, the author argues it is a communal meal rather than a gift to a god.</note>, then, was to cement and strengthen the alliance between the human clan and the animal clan; and the way in which this was done was through some application of the blood of the totem, or by, in some way, coming into contact with that which was specially connected with its life, or by partaking of its flesh. The object, then, of killing a member of the totem tribe becomes clear. Under ordinary circumstances it would be absolutely forbidden, and regarded as the murder of a kinsman; but on special occasions it was solemnly done in order to shed the blood and partake of the flesh, and so strengthen the alliance. The blood is regarded as the life, and when the blood of a member of the totem tribe of animals was shed, the life of the totem was brought to the spot where it was needed, and the blood could be applied to the worshippers as a bond of union, and then the union could be still further cemented by the feast upon the flesh, by which the spirit of the totem was absorbed and assimilated by its human kinsmen. The object of the animal sacrifice, therefore, was not in any sense to offer a gift, but to obtain communion with the totem-spirit.

Now, if we apply this theory of sacrifice to the sacrifices offered to the village deities in South India <note>The author refers to the *Gramadevatas* or local village goddesses of the Madras Presidency and surrounding regions, whose rituals often involve animal sacrifice and communal participation.</note>, we see that the main ceremonies connected with them at once become intelligible; the various modes of sprinkling and applying the blood, and the different forms of the sacrificial feast were all originally intended

This framework is proposed as the key to understanding the ritual traditions of village deities in South India.</summary>

...to promote <term>communion</term> <note>In this context, the author means a spiritual union or "sharing of life" between the worshiper and the spirit.</note> with the spirit that was worshipped. In the same way, even such a ceremony as the wearing of the entrails round the neck, and putting the liver in the mouth, acquires an intelligible meaning and purpose. The liver and entrails are naturally connected with the life of the animal, and the motive of this repulsive ceremony would seem to be an intense desire to obtain as close communion as possible with the object of worship by wearing those parts of its body that are specially connected with its life. So, too, this theory explains why the animal sacrificed is so often treated as an object of worship. In the case of the **buffalo sacrifices** in the <term>Telugu country</term> <note>A region in South India where the Telugu language is spoken, encompassing the modern states of Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.</note>, as we have seen, the buffalo is paraded through the village, decked with garlands and smeared with **turmeric** and <term>kunkuma</term> <note>A sacred red powder, often made from turmeric and lime, used for making ritual marks on the body or on holy objects.</note>, and then, as it passes by the houses, people come out and pour water on its feet, and worship it. But why should this be done if the animal sacrificed is regarded as only a gift to the goddess? When, however, we realize that the animal sacrificed was not originally regarded as a gift, but as a member of the totem tribe and the representative of the spirit to be worshipped, the whole ceremony becomes full of meaning.

Then, again, this theory of the origin of sacrifice supplies a very plausible and intelligible explanation of the origin of the use of stones and images to represent the **village deities** in India. At first sight it seems a complete mystery why a common ordinary stone should be regarded as representing a god or goddess. Most of the stones used for this purpose in South Indian villages have absolutely nothing that is peculiar or distinctive about them. Often they are simply stone pillars of varying heights, and a large number are only small, conical stones, not more than six or seven inches high. Some, again, are flat slabs with figures carved on them in <term>bas-relief</term> <note>A style of sculpture where the figures are carved to project only slightly from the background.</note> and others are regular images. The images and carved bas-reliefs we can understand; but how could these ordinary stones and stone-pillars have ever come to be regarded as the representatives of spiritual beings? The theory of...

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the "totemistic" theory of sacrifice. Having previously described how worshippers might eat the animal to achieve communion with a spirit, the author now explains how the physical location of the sacrifice—and the stones used to mark it—evolved into the sacred pillars and images found in village shrines today.</meta>

->### PROBABLE ORIGIN OF THE WORSHIP<-

The theory of sacrifice connected with <term>totemism</term> <note>A belief system where a group of people shares a spiritual connection or kinship with a particular animal or plant (the totem), often involving rituals to maintain that bond.</note> supplies, at any rate, a possible and intelligible explanation. The totem animal was killed in order to shed its blood, and so secure the presence of the totem deity at a particular spot, which then became sacred or <term>Taboo</term> <note>A term used here to describe something set apart as holy or forbidden to common use because of its association with the divine.</note>. To violate it would be a grievous offence. Accordingly, the spot was marked by a simple heap of stones, or by an upright stone pillar, which would perhaps be sprinkled with the blood. 

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then, as totemism gradually died out and gave place to higher religious ideas and <term>anthropomorphic</term> <note>Describing deities as having human form or human characteristics.</note> conceptions of deity, the old totemistic conception of sacrifice became obscured. The animal that was killed was regarded no longer as the representative of the object of worship, but as a gift to the deity. At the same time, the sanctity of the spot became associated with the stones, originally set up to mark the place of sacrifice, and so in time the stone pillar itself became sacred. It came to be treated as the symbol of the deity to whom the sacrifice was offered, while the heap of stones developed into the sacred altar. 

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We can probably trace one stage of this process of evolution in the ideas now connected with the boundary-stone, *ellai-kal* <note>original Tamil: *ellai* (boundary) and *kal* (stone). These stones are common sights in rural South India, marking the limits of village lands.</note>. No doubt it was once simply a stone placed to mark the spot, on the boundary of the village lands, where the sacrifice was offered. Then the stone became sacred, and the idea grew up that it was inhabited by the spirit who was worshipped. There, however, the process of evolution stopped, and the stone is not now regarded, like the other stones, as representing the deity, but simply as her abode.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Probably the other stones were once regarded in exactly the same light, and then advanced a step further and became representatives of the deities worshipped. The next step, to the carved human figures, whether bas-reliefs <note>A type of sculpture where figures are carved into a flat surface so they stand out slightly from the background.</note> or complete images, would be easy and natural, when once the deity had been conceived no longer as the spirit of a whole species of animals, but as akin to human beings.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When this change in religious ideas took place must, of course, be a matter of conjecture, but it probably...

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that village religious practices evolved alongside social changes. Having established how sacred stones replaced totem animals, the author now explains why village deities are predominantly female and how these ancient "totemistic" roots still provide the best explanation for modern rituals.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA

This shift coincided with the transition from a nomadic, wandering lifestyle to a settled one focused on herding and farming. It was a time when wandering clans grew into stable village communities, and humans had firmly established their dominance over wild animals.

Similarly, it is possible that the link between the growth of agriculture and the birth of village communities—and therefore village deities—explains why the village deities of South India are almost always female.

Throughout the world, the spirit of the earth is typically seen as female. The deities who oversee farming are mainly goddesses because the concepts of fertility and reproduction are traditionally connected with women. Therefore, when a nomadic herding clan settled down to a farming life in a village, they would naturally worship the spirits of the village lands as goddesses rather than gods.

Furthermore, because farming among primitive peoples was the work of women rather than men—as is still seen among some cultures today—it is likely that village goddesses were initially worshipped by women. A lingering trace of this history can be found in the custom of the <term>Mālā pūjārī</term> <note>original: "Mālā pūjārī"; a priest belonging to the Mala community, a large social group in Andhra Pradesh and Telangana.</note>. Though the priest is a man, he dresses as a woman while sitting in a cart surrounded by animals that have been impaled alive, as he is pulled in a procession through the village.¹ Another trace is seen in the prominent role women play in some regions during the waving of the <term>āratī</term> <note>original: "āratī"; a ritual of waving lamps, incense, or water before a deity to show devotion or ward off evil spirits.</note>.²

These theories regarding the origins of village deities, <term>idolatry</term> <note>The worship of physical objects or images as gods.</note>, and animal sacrifice in South India are, of course, only hypotheses. However, when we see how the "totemistic theory" provides a logical explanation for the crude forms of image-worship found in many villages—and for many sacrificial details that seem to contradict modern religious ideas—we find enough evidence to justify accepting it.

And there can be no doubt that the ceremonial observed in these sacrifices gives very substantial support to the theory, that the original idea of sacrifice was not that of a gift to the deity but **communion** <note>In this context, "communion" refers to the act of sharing or becoming one with a divine power, rather than simply offering a bribe or gift to a god.</note> with a supernatural power. The mysterious efficacy attributed to the sprinkling of the blood might almost be regarded as an unconscious prophecy of the Christian doctrine of the **Atonement** <note>The "Atonement" is the Christian belief that the death of Jesus serves to reconcile humanity with God, often described as "at-one-ment."</note>, while the whole ritual of the sacrifices, even in its crudest and most revolting forms, bears witness to that instinctive craving after communion with God, which finds its highest expression and satisfaction in the **sacramental system** <note>The "sacramental system" refers to the formal rituals of the Church, such as the Eucharist or Holy Communion, which use physical elements to connect the believer to the divine.</note> of the Christian Church.

# CHAPTER IX

## SOCIAL, MORAL, AND RELIGIOUS INFLUENCE OF THE SYSTEM

The results of this system of religion might at first seem to be wholly degrading intellectually, morally, and spiritually. It appears on the surface to be a religion of fear and superstition, finding its outward expression in mean, ugly symbols, and in forms of worship that are to a very large extent disgusting and even immoral. The account of a village festival in the <note>original: "Telugu country"</note> <term>Telugu country</term> <note>The region in South India, primarily the modern-day state of Andhra Pradesh, where the Telugu language is spoken.</note> reads like mere midsummer madness; many of the rites in which animals are impaled or buried alive are revolting in their cruelty; and the animal sacrifices with their crude butchery and coarse bloodshed bear witness to a low and unworthy conception of deity. Whatever may have been the origin of these animal sacrifices in prehistoric times, they are now regarded by the worshippers simply as a means of appeasing the <term>deity's wrath</term> <note>The anger of a goddess or spirit, which worshippers believed must be calmed to avoid disasters like disease or famine.</note> by satisfying her lust for blood. In the ancient Jewish sacrifices there may have been the same amount of bloodshed and butchery, when on such an occasion as the dedication of the <term>Temple at Jerusalem</term> <note>The central place of worship for ancient Judaism; the text refers to the massive dedication ceremony performed by King Solomon in the 10th century BCE.</note> "King Solomon offered a sacrifice of twenty and two thousand oxen and a hundred and twenty thousand sheep," but the Jewish sacrifices symbolized great moral and spiritual truths; the victim represented the worshipper, the killing of the animal and the offering of the blood expressed the consecration of the worshipper's own life to God; in the <term>sin offering</term> <note>A sacrifice made specifically to seek forgiveness for a moral or religious transgression.</note> and the <term>peace offering</term> <note>A sacrifice offered to celebrate a harmonious relationship with God or to give thanks.</note> the presentation of the blood and the feast on the flesh were symbolical of penitence for sin and communion with God. But in the sacrifices to the village deities

<summary>The author examines the moral impact of South Indian village religion, characterizing its rituals as being born of fear and cruelty. He contrasts these "revolting" blood sacrifices with the ancient Jewish tradition, arguing that while both involved slaughter, the latter was elevated by moral and symbolic meaning.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of South Indian village religious practices. # INFLUENCE OF THE SYSTEM

In India at the present day there are no traces of those higher ideas in the minds of the worshippers. There is no penitence for sin, no thought of the consecration of human life to a just and holy God, but simply the desire to appease the ill-temper of a vengeful spirit by an offering of blood. And even in unbloody offerings of fruit, camphor, and incense to the more refined and respectable of the goddesses, who are supposed to be shocked by the sight of blood, the idea of sacrifice does not rise above the conception of a <term>propitiatory gift</term> <note>An offering intended to regain the favor or appease the anger of a person or deity.</note>. It is the kind of offering that is made to the local policeman or a tyrannical government official to secure his favour. And in almost all the festivals held in honour of the <term>village deities</term> <note>Local spirits or goddesses, known as Grama-devatas, who are believed to govern the daily life and health of a specific community.</note> there is a wild orgiastic excitement, and often a sad amount of drunkenness and immorality that is most degrading.

So, too, there is nothing morally elevating in the conception formed of the characters of the deities themselves. They have not even the grandeur of such a deity as <term>Śiva</term> <note>One of the principal deities of Hinduism, often associated with destruction and transformation; he represents a sophisticated, universal philosophical system.</note>. Śiva may be terrible and cruel, but at any rate there is something grand and majestic about him: he represents a world-force; he is an interpretation of the universe and the embodiment of a philosophy. But the village deity is nothing more than a petty local spirit, tyrannizing over or protecting a small hamlet, occasionally venting her spite or her ill-temper on a handful of poor villagers. She inspires fear because of her power to do grievous harm by inflicting diseases and injuries on man and beast when she is offended, but she has no relation to the universe or even to the world: she is the product of fear untouched by philosophic reflection; so she does not draw out any feelings of wonder and admiration, still less of love and gratitude, nor does she lead her worshippers on to any higher ideals of morality.

Taking the system, therefore, as a whole, as it exists at the present day, we can only condemn it from a moral and religious point of view as a debasing superstition, and the only attitude which the Christian Church can possibly take towards it as a working system is

His primary temple is in Puri, Odisha.</note> in <term>Orissa</term> <note>A state on the eastern coast of India, now called Odisha.</note>, where all castes, including the <term>Brāhmans</term> <note>The highest-ranking priestly class in the traditional Hindu social hierarchy.</note>, eat together. If they cannot feel that they are respected, the next best thing is to feel that in times of trouble they are needed.

Then, in the second place, deep down in the system, buried beneath a mass of traditional rites that have lost their meaning, there is still the instinctive craving of the human heart for <term>communion with God</term> <note>The spiritual experience of intimate fellowship or communication with a deity.</note>. This instinctive feeling after God has indeed been degraded by unworthy and petty ideas of the spiritual world; it has been distorted by fear and superstition; it has found expression in weird and horrid forms; but still, in spite of all corruptions and distortions, we can discern in it, not merely a belief in a spirit world, but a desire to come into personal communion with spiritual beings. In the previous chapter it has been shown that the original idea underlying the system of animal sacrifice was that of communion rather than that of <term>propitiation</term> <note>The act of winning the favor of or appeasing a deity to prevent their anger; the author argues the original intent was connection, not just fear-based bribery.</note>; and, though at the present day propitiation by acceptable gifts is undoubtedly the dominant idea in these sacrifices and offerings, still the idea of communion is not wholly lost. The ***pūjārī*** <note>A priest or ritual leader who performs the service of worship (puja) in a temple or village shrine.</note> is often regarded as possessed and inspired by the deity, and the sprinkling of the blood of the victim on the houses, the fields and the persons of the worshippers is regarded as a means of securing the presence and protection of the deity. <meta>This page continues the author’s analysis of the spiritual state of rural populations in South India. While the previous page discussed how local rituals provided a sense of social belonging to the "depressed classes," this section explores the psychological transition from fearing local spirits to embracing Christian concepts of a loving God.</meta>

# THE VILLAGE GODS OF SOUTH INDIA 156

simple desire for communion with a deity of some sort, there is a germ and root of true religious feeling which craves for expression. It is pathetic to notice how real is the desire among many of the more religious men and women in the villages, even among the <term>depressed classes</term> <note>A historical term for Dalit communities, who were traditionally excluded from the formal caste system and subjected to severe social discrimination.</note>, to see God. I have often met with and heard of men who have spent what are for them large sums of money, and undergone much hardship, to satisfy this desire. We must not undervalue this rudimentary religious feeling; and if, in the worship of the <term>village deities</term> <note>Local guardian spirits, often female, believed to protect the village from disease and disaster, distinct from the major gods of the Hindu pantheon.</note>, it has for many centuries been feeding on carrion <note>The author uses this metaphor—referring to decaying flesh—to describe rituals involving animal sacrifice which he views as spiritually "impure."</note>, perhaps it is better for it to feed on carrion than to die of starvation.

Then, again, the belief in the village deities has undoubtedly fostered an attitude of mind towards the spiritual world which is to a certain degree a preparation for the <term>Gospel</term> <note>The teachings of Jesus Christ and the Christian message of salvation.</note>. It has made men feel a sense of dependence on spiritual beings. The mental attitude of the ordinary villager is the very antithesis of <term>materialism</term> <note>The belief that only physical matter exists, denying the spiritual.</note> or <term>agnosticism</term> <note>The view that the existence of God is unknown or unknowable.</note>. He has a very vivid belief that the world in which he lives is surrounded by unseen spiritual beings, and in all times of trouble he feels intensely his dependence on his village deity for help and protection. And even where the village deity is conceived of as an ill-tempered, revengeful being, the fear which she inspires is not a bad preparation for a belief in a God of love.

The experience of most evangelists among the <term>Out-castes</term> <note>A term formerly used for those outside the traditional Hindu caste hierarchy; see "depressed classes."</note> of South India would be, I think, that their fear of evil spirits is one reason why the doctrine of an omnipotent God of infinite love appeals to them with so much force. It makes them realize their need of help. It does for them what the fear of powerful and malicious enemies did for the Jewish people of old. The <term>Jewish Psalms</term> <note>A book of the Hebrew Bible and Christian Old Testament containing sacred songs and prayers, often focused on seeking protection from enemies.</note> show how closely the need of protection from powerful enemies was bound up with the deepest religious feelings of the chosen people. The need of protection against evil spirits is playing a similar part in the religious development of the villagers of South India.

The Christian Church thus brings to the villagers,

# INFLUENCE OF THE SYSTEM 157

and especially to the <term>Outcastes</term> <note>A historical term for Dalit communities, who were traditionally excluded from the formal Hindu caste system and subjected to severe social discrimination.</note>, three great truths which their belief in the <term>village deities</term> <note>Local guardian spirits, often female (Gramadevata), believed to protect villages from disease and disaster; the author views these as distinct from the "high" gods of Hinduism.</note> specially prepares them to accept:

(*a*) First, the truth of the existence of an omnipotent God of infinite love, the creator and the ruler of the universe, and the Father of all mankind, a truth which stands out in vivid and startling contrast to their belief in a multitude of evil or ill-tempered spirits always ready to do them grievous harm, with no superior power to control them.

(*b*) Second, the truth of the universal redemption from sin and the great gift of direct, personal access to an almighty, all-loving God through **Jesus Christ**. This truth stands in equally striking contrast to the poor and miserable communion with a petty local deity offered through the blood of their **animal sacrifices**. To compare great things with small, it is as though a poor villager suffering from the persecution of a petty local official were suddenly told that he had free right of access to the kind and powerful <term>collector</term> <note>The District Collector was the highest-ranking British colonial official in a local district, wielding significant administrative and judicial power.</note> of the district. The good news of free access to God is a real <term>Gospel</term> <note>The Christian message or "good news" regarding salvation.</note> of freedom.

(*c*) And thirdly, there is the great truth of the equality of all men in God's sight and the universal brotherhood of man. It is a truth very dimly foreshadowed in the rites of their primitive cult; but in the Christian Church it stands out as the very essence of the Gospel message. And it is a truth that makes a powerful appeal to the hearts of the downtrodden and depressed.

Thus, while the cult of the village deities provides little foundation of belief or practice on which the Christian Church can build; on the other hand it has kept alive a sense of deep spiritual needs, which Christianity alone can satisfy. It certainly brings religion down into the every-day life of the people. The ordinary villager of **South India** does nothing without offering prayer to the village deity, while the shrines and symbols that are scattered all over the countryside keep constantly before his mind the

# APPENDIX I

## ON THE CEREMONY OF THE <term>OPENING OF THE MOUTH</term> <note>A symbolic ritual performed on a mummy or statue so that the deceased's soul could breathe, eat, drink, and speak in the afterlife.</note> IN THE FUNERAL RITES OF ANCIENT EGYPT

There is an interesting parallel to the practice of cutting off the right foreleg and putting it in the mouth of the buffalo (described on page 51) in the ancient funeral ceremonies connected with the cult of <term>Osiris</term> <note>The Egyptian god of the afterlife, death, and resurrection.</note> in Egypt. The legend ran that, after Osiris had been murdered by his brother <term>Set</term> <note>The god of storms, chaos, and violence who, in Egyptian mythology, murdered his brother Osiris.</note>, his son <term>Horus</term> <note>The falcon-headed god of the sky and kingship, who sought to avenge his father and restore him to life.</note> sought out his body, in order to raise it to life; and, when he found it, he untied the bandages so that Osiris might move his limbs and rise up. Under the direction of <term>Thoth</term> <note>The god of wisdom, writing, and magic, often credited with inventing the rituals of mummification.</note>, Horus recited a series of formulas, as he presented offerings to Osiris; and he and his sons and <term>Anubis</term> <note>The jackal-headed god associated with mummification and the protection of graves.</note> performed the ceremonies which opened the mouth and nostrils, and the eyes and ears of Osiris. This opening of the mouth was one of the regular funeral rites in ancient Egypt.

There is a book found in tombs called the *Book of the Opening of the Mouth*; and in a **British Museum** bulletin, entitled *The Book of the Dead*, written by Mr. <note>Sir Ernest Alfred Thompson Wallis Budge (1857–1934), a prolific English Egyptologist and curator at the British Museum whose work was widely read in the early 20th century.</note> **E. A. Wallis Budge**, it is said that, on the upper margins of the insides of coffins, there are frequently given two or more rows of coloured drawings of the offerings which under the <term>fifth dynasty</term> <note>A period of the Old Kingdom of Egypt, approximately 2494 to 2345 BCE.</note> were presented to the deceased or his statue during the celebration of the service of “Opening the Mouth.” In one of the illustrations the ceremony of Opening the Mouth is shown as being performed on the mummy of a royal scribe. In the picture there is a calf walking in front of its mother with its left foreleg cut off, and in front of the calf are two slaves, one with the heart of the

<summary>This appendix draws a comparison between South Indian sacrificial practices and the ancient Egyptian "Opening of the Mouth" ritual, detailing the mythological origins of the ceremony involving Osiris and Horus.</summary>

deceased in his hand, and the other holding the left foreleg of the calf, which is apparently being placed upon a table. It is not clear what part the foreleg plays in the ceremony of the **"Opening of the Mouth,"** <note>A ritual meant to restore the senses to the deceased.</note> but there is an obvious resemblance between this ancient ceremony in Egypt and the widespread custom in South India mentioned above. The Egyptian ceremony suggests that one object of putting the foreleg in the mouth in the case of sacrifices in India is to keep it open and enable the spirit of the animal to go in and out.

We give in *Plate XVIII* <note>Referring to a photographic illustration in the original printed book.</note> a photograph of a <term>buffalo sacrifice</term> <note>A central ritual in many South Indian village festivals, often dedicated to a local goddess.</note> carried out by the servants of Dr. Hunt of Secunderabad. The scene is the garage in which his motor bicycle stands. Round it the servants have grouped his sword, the gardener's shears, a baby's chair, a tea-kettle, etc., and to these the sacrifice was made. <note>This reflects a practice where tools and modern implements are treated as sacred objects or vessels for divine power, similar to the festival of Ayudha Puja.</note> In the right foreground lies the body of the buffalo, to the left its head with the right foreleg in its mouth, while between the head and the bicycle may be seen a bottle of liquor and various other offerings.

## APPENDIX II

### ON THE USE OF IRON TO DRIVE AWAY EVIL SPIRITS

A curious custom connected with the worship of <term>Mariamma</term> <note>A prominent village goddess in South India, traditionally invoked for protection against—or as the source of—contagious diseases like smallpox and cholera.</note> was brought to my notice by Dr. Hunt of Secunderabad. An Indian friend of his came across it in a village of the Bellary district in the Telugu country. <note>Bellary is located in the modern state of Karnataka, along the border of the Telugu-speaking region of Andhra Pradesh.</note> The villagers hold a festival in honour of the goddess Mariamma every year and offer the usual sacrifices. In 1917 there was a very severe epidemic of influenza in the district; <note>This likely refers to the early local outbreaks of what became the devastating 1918-1919 global flu pandemic.</note> so a special festival was held to appease the wrath of the goddess. A wooden bust of her was made with arms akimbo and sacrifices were duly offered to it. Thousands of people came from neighbouring villages for the occasion, to do <term>pūjā</term> <note>A Sanskrit term for ritual worship involving offerings such as flowers, fruit, or incense to a deity.</note> and make their offerings. 

On the following night the image was placed on a small wooden cart about three feet high, and taken in procession to a place outside the village. The head of each family then came and drove an iron nail into the image, till it was dotted all over with nails. A goat was then sacrificed, and the blood sprinkled over the goddess; after which the image and the cart were covered over with a red cloth and left in the field. A rough drawing of the nail-filled image is reproduced in Plate XVIII. The explanation of this ceremony given by the villagers themselves was that the nails were driven in to the goddess to attract her attention and induce her to be kind to each family and protect them against the disease. It seems an odd way of doing it; and probably the real meaning of the ceremony in prehistoric times was somewhat different. It is a very old and wide-spread idea that malignant spirits are afraid of iron; and it is possible that the hammering 

<summary>This appendix describes a 1917 ritual in the Bellary district where villagers drove iron nails into an image of the goddess Mariamma to seek protection from an influenza epidemic. The author suggests this practice stems from an ancient belief that iron has the power to repel or control malevolent spirits.</summary>

<keywords>Mariamma, Bellary, Telugu, pūjā, influenza, iron, sacrifice, malignant spirits, ritual nails, folk medicine</keywords>

of the nails into the goddess was originally intended to put the fear of iron into her and drive out of her the evil temper. The villagers do not now connect the practice with <term>bhanamati</term> <note>original: "bhanamati"; a term used in various Indian languages for sorcery or black magic.</note>; but the original idea may have been in some way connected with it. But whatever the origin, it throws an interesting light on the driving of nails into the famous <term>Hindenburg statue</term> <note>Referring to the "Iron Hindenburg," a massive wooden statue of Field Marshal Paul von Hindenburg erected in Berlin in 1915. Citizens paid to drive nails into it to raise funds for the war effort, a practice known as "nail-men" (Nagelmann).</note> in Berlin during the war. Evidently this is a survival of an old pagan custom dating back to a remote antiquity.

<margin>1 These notes about <term>Malabar</term> <note>The southwestern coastal region of India, primarily modern-day Kerala.</note> were kindly given me by one of the assistants of the Government Museum, Madras.</margin>

It is a common belief among all castes in Malabar^1 that lonely places, such as cremation grounds, the sides of tanks or groves of tamarind trees, are haunted by <term>pisachas</term> <note>original: "pisachas"; in Hindu mythology, these are malevolent, flesh-eating demons or spirits of the deceased.</note> or evil spirits. At about the middle of the night these evil spirits are supposed to roam about their haunts with the intention of possessing those who chance to pass their way. People who say that they have seen the demon give us to understand that it has the form of a woman, and less often of a man, while others say that its form is too fearful to describe, attesting that, if they could believe their eyes, they saw a hideous and most appalling figure towering right up to the skies. Men are much afraid to pass through such places between nine p.m. and three a.m., but feel themselves safe when they have sharp iron weapons with them. It is supposed that the devil is afraid of iron and goes away in a fright. A man versed in magic, when he has to walk through such places, draws a cabalistic figure on the earth, and inscribes on it some mystical letters. At the center of the figure he plunges the pointed end of an iron knife or peg. Having done this, he feels quite secure from the baneful influence of evil spirits.

Sometimes a house is believed to be haunted by some of these aerial beings. When calamities come thick upon the inmates of a house, it is a certain sign that it is possessed by evil spirits. In such a case the
